              HOSMER 100 years of Dreams and Realities, 1887-1987

FOREWORD

This is a compilation of "100 Years of Dreams and Realities".  It is a century
of times told in biographical and autobiographical form and includes pictures
of pioneers and their families and the events in their lives.  This publication
tells the story, preserving a rich past so dear to those of use who homesteaded
our hearts here.  The good people of the Hosmer community who have shared their
stories, have left a legacy of written word for the yet-unborn generations.

"100 Years of Dreams and Realities" was planned to preserve a period of history
that can never be repeated.  For those who live in the Hosmer and surrounding
area it will be a sentimental journey back into time, and for us who live here
now it is a glimpse of how it was "way back when".  We hope it will keep alive
memories of these people and their efforts in establishing and molding a
century in the history of our town.

The Centennial Book Committee is happy to have a part in preserving and
immortalizing the history of Hosmer and surrounding area through the efforts of
those who contributed material.  For the people whose history does not appear
or for those who find mistakes or omissions in their history we apologize as we
do not claim to be infallible.

For the many people who find "100 Years of Dreams and Realities" interesting,
our appreciation goes out to those who labored long in research to provide us
with the detailed history.  The words of President Theodore Roosevelt sums up
our intentions -- "The best heritage the pioneer can leave to future
generations is the simple yet powerful story of his life, of hardships
endured, of dangers passed, and the final victory over wilderness and the
desert plains."
                Amelia Walth

The contents of this history book have been compiled with information acquired
from family records and memories.  If mistakes have been made or if your family
history has been omitted, it was unintentional.  We double checked our material
and made every effort to be accurate.

The book committee wishes to thank those who willingly gave their time and
talent to help us complete this book.  We have done our best and sincerely hope
you enjoy your book.

                                City

Hosmer Community Has Rich Heritage

The people of our community feel that they have a very rich heritage--rich not
in material things, to be sure, but in culture and in character.  The national
background of most of these people is German, despite the fact that their
pioneer ancestors were erroneously labeled Russians, or more commonly
"Rooshans," by their non-German neighbors.  Russian they were, indeed, as far
as citizenship was concerned; and they may very well have seemed Russian in
appearance, since they came directly from Russia.  They were no doubt listed as
Russian immigrants in the statistical records of the United States Immigration
Bureau.  But the correct name for them was German-Russians, indicating that
they were Russian citizens of German descent who had kept very much intact
their German culture and language.

Not often in the history of the world have any group of people who have lived
for several generations in an alien land, clung so tenaciously to the culture
and folkways of their forbearers or absorbed so little of the civilization of
their adopted homeland as these German people did during their long sojourn in
Russia.  For at least three generations the Germans who had migrated into the
great Russian Ukraine during the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries lived
there, holding themselves aloof from the Russians and seeking no social
contacts with the natives, maintaining the standards and practicing the customs
of their fatherland.  They hired Russian laborers on their farms; they
conducted business with the Russians when it was necessary; they learned the
Russian language in school because the government required them to do so; those
who had the opportunity to do so even acquired their higher education through
the medium of the Russian language, because that was the only form in which it
was available.  But they kept their own Swabian dialect intact; they conducted
their church services, their village schools, their local business and
government in the German language.  They never considered themselves anything
but German.

Why, then, had these people, who were so proud of their national heritage,
moved to a foreign land and lived for so many years among people whose language
and customs were entirely strange to them?  Many years ago there was, in the
southwest part of Germany, a duchy called Swabia (Schwaben in German).  This
comprised Modern Baden and Wuerttemberg, as well as parts of Bavaria and
Switzerland. The Swabians were so sorely oppressed that when they received news
that the Russian rulers were asking for immigrants to colonize virgin land in
the Ukraine, many of them decided to leave their German homeland.  The
invitation was particularly attractive to the immigrants because of a number of
special privileges promised them.  They were to have complete religious
freedom, exemption from military service, free land, government loans to buy
seed, and tax exemptions for ten years.  The offer of free land was
particularly attractive to people who were unable to own land in their own
country.  So began a migration which lasted for nearly one hundred years, and
the cultivation of untitled land into the rich Ukrainian wheat country, which,
in most recent time, has come to be known as the "bread basket of Europe."

But eventually, when the original purpose of the colonization had been
accomplished, the government began to renege on its promises and, among other
things, to draft Germans into the Russian armed services.  Now these south
German colonists had always been a congenial people, and, unlike their Prussian
cousins in northern Germany, did not take kindly to militarism.  One of their
reasons for leaving their homeland had been to avoid compulsory military
service, and they had no desire for their sons and grandsons to become soldiers
of the Russian Czar.  In the meantime they had heard of land where there was
real freedom and plenty of land for the taking; so, with virtually empty
pockets, but with great hope, high spirits, and with their habitual faith and
ambition, they packed what few personal belongings they could carry with them,
bade sad farewells to their loved one, and started for America.  Eventually
many of them found their way to the prairie country of the Dakotas.

It would be inaccurate to say that all of the early settlers of our community
were German-Russians.  A few families came direct from Germany.  In the early
days of the settlement there were also many families of British and of
Holland-Dutch descent, with a sprinkling of various other nationalities. But
many of these became discouraged and many were forced to leave by repeated crop
failures due to drought, dust storms, and grasshoppers.  Others were driven out
by persistent epidemics, as diptheria and flu.  Apparently the German-Russians
were able to make a "go" of it under even the most adverse conditions because
of having been inured to such hardships in Russia--one might say that they were
colonizing for the second time, and even the worst hardships in America did not
seem as bad as some of the things they had to endure in Russia.  Some of them
stuck it out through sheer necessity--they simply hadn't the money to go
anywhere else; some were held by rugged perseverance, an inherent quality of
their race; some were kept by a dream-better times would come and they could be
landowners, if they could hold on for only one more year.  Their experiences in
both Germany and Russia had taught them to value existence more than comfort.
They knew how to get the most out of land--when there was not enough moisture
to grow wheat, they raised beets and potatoes in the sloughs; if they couldn't
afford horses they used oxen; because they couldn't afford to pay hired help,
the women worked in the field and children were put to work at a very early
age; many of them lived in their sod shanties and dugouts after the other
settlers had finer houses, so that they could acquire more land and "get
ahead." And always they had their faith--that intense faith that eventually a
better day would come.

Perhaps our German-Russian people have been slower than some of the other
national groups in relinquishing the distinctive characteristics of their
ancestry, and in blending into the American melting pot.  But they are a proud
race with a proud heritage, which they are reluctant to give up.  While they
are proud to be Americans and to contribute to American nationalism, they still
cook the dishes their ancestors savored, they still possess the wonderful
Gemuetlichkeit which enables them to take strangers into their homes and treat
them as members of their families.  The older people still like to speak German
with their distinctive Swabian dialect, though a large number of the words used
are English words, with German prefixes and suffixes appended, rolled out in
Swabian-sounding syllables.  They are still industrious and conservative, but
they still love the kind of good time they have at an old-fashioned German
wedding with a wedding dance.

In short, these people possess the typical German qualities of courage,
perseverance, integrity, loyalty, profound religious faith, and great pride in
their heritage.  But these are tempered by the special congeniality, the
infinite patience, the fine sense of humor and the emotional sensitivity
characteristic of the South German and Austrian people. Surely all of these are
wonderful traits for any American to inherit and to pass on to his descendents.

Petition for Incorporation of the Town of Hosmer

In the matter of the incorporation of the town of Hosmer, in the county of
Edmunds and state of South Dakota.

The above entitled matter coming on to be heard before the board of county
commissioners in and for said county of Edmunds, South Dakota, upon the 6th day
of July, A. D., 1904, at a regular meeting of said board upon application by
petition consisting of not less than one-third of the qualified voters residing
within the proposed territory to be included within said proposed corporation
with which petition set forth the boundaries and the quantity of land embraced
in the survey, and the resident population therein contained according to the
census taken, which petition and application contained an affidavit verifying
the fact therein alleged, and it further appearing from such application by
affidavit and examination that the survey map and census were subject to
examination in the manner and for the period required by Sec. 1419 of the
Political Code of South Dakota.  And, it further appearing to the satisfaction
of this board that the requirements have been fully compiled with and that all
thing necessary have been done to carry into force and effect, Chapter 15 of
the Political Code of the State of South Dakota, relating to incorporated towns
and the board being fully advised in the premises.

It is ordered and declared, that the territory mentioned and described in said
application, to wit:

Commencing at the Southwest corner of the Northwest quarter of Section twelve
in Township one hundred and twenty-four, North Range seventy-two, in Edmunds
County, South Dakota, and running thence eighty chains East on the half section
line, thence North on the section line eighty chains, thence West eighty
chains, thence South eighty chains, to the place of beginning, containing six
hundred and forty acres more or less according to U.S. Government survey
thereof, shall with the assent of the qualified voters thereof, be an
incorporated town by the name of Hosmer.

And it is further ordered that a meeting of the qualified resident voters of
the proposed limits, on the 29th day of July, A. D., 1904, for the purpose of
determining by ballot whether said proposed territory shall be an incorporated
town, polls to be opened at nine o'clock in the forenoon of said day and remain
open until four o'clock in the afternoon.

L. E. Amerpo
T. A. Evans
C. H. Rebrud

Incorporation of the Town of Hosmer
Ipswich, S. D.    August 11th, 1904

The board of county commissioners met in conformity
with the published call of the County Auditor,
present commissioners Amerpohl, Evans and
Rebrud.  On motion the following resolution was
passed unanimously:

STATE OF SOUTH DAKOTA :SS COUNTY OF EDMUNDS

In the matter of the incorporation of the town of Hosmer:

This matter coming on to be heard at a session of the board of County
commissioners, at the town of Ipswich in the County of Edmunds and State of
South Dakota on the 1lth day of August A. D. 1904, in pursuance to a notice
duly published and given according to law, and it appearing from the returns of
the board of inspectors elected by said town, that an election was held in said
town of Hosmer on the 29th day of July A. D. 1904, and at said election there
were twenty-seven votes cast in favor of said incorporation and one against
said proposed proposition, and that said election was in all respects conducted
according to law, and the said board being satisfied of the legality of said
election, it is ordered:

That the incorporated town of Hosmer in the County of Edmunds and State of
South Dakota has been incorporated under the name of the Incorporated Town of
Hosmer.

L. E. Amerpohl
T. A. Evans
C. H. Rebrud
Board of Country Commissioners

On motion the board adjourned.

L. E. Amerpohl, Chairman

F. J. Tracy, County Auditor

Plat of Hosmer

I. F. P. Dobson, surveyor do hereby certify that I have surveyed and staked out
on the 1lth day of April, A. D. 1887, the Townsite of Hosmer, Edmunds County,
Dakota territory, situated on the southeast quarter of the southwest quarter of
section one (1), and on the northeast quarter of the northwest quarter and the
northwest quarter of section twelve (12), all in township 124 North, Range 72
west, and that all the Streets and Avenues are Sixty-six (66) feet wide, except
Main Street, which is Eighty (80) feet wide, and Railway Street, which is Sixty
(60) feet wide.  All alleys are sixteen (16) feet wide.  The large figures in
black indicate the numbers of the blocks, the small figures in black indicate
the numbers of lots.  The size of each lot, and width of each street, avenue
and alley are represented in feet by figures in red.  The position of
Government comers are as represented.  The angles between the different lines,
are represented in degrees and minutes by figures in red, upon this plat.  I
have placed stone monuments at the points shown on the annexed plat, and I
hereby certify this plat is correct.

F. P. Dobson, Surveyor

                        HOSMER - 1920's

Hosmer, a thriving town on the Linton Line of the Milwaukee, has a population
of about 450 and is growing along with the development of her trade territory.

Electric lights and good fire protection afforded by a well organized fire
department, all lines of trade represented by enterprising establishments, two
good banks to take care of our finances, a splendid public school and churches
of the Catholic, Lutheran, Congregational and Reform denominations take care of
the needs of our people in these directions.

Hosmer has many beautiful homes exceptionally well kept that show effectively
that her people are content and happy as well as prosperous.

Good roads from Hosmer in all directions penetrate a large trade territory
peopled with the most thrifty and successful farmers in the county.

It is a source of very great pride to us to say that Dun and Bradstreet both
give our business institutions the best ratings of credit of any town in the
Northwest. Practically every business house in Hosmer discounts its bills
before due.

We have four splendid general stores, three good hardwares, one fine drug
store, a racket store, a confectionery store, a furniture store, two large
lumber yards, blacksmith shop, four implement dealers, three automobile
garages, five cream stations, one flour mill, five grain elevators, two oil
stations, a good hotel and the best meat market in the county, and two live
stock buyers. Thus you can see that Hosmer is prepared in every way to take
care of the needs of all who may come to establish a home with us. Good
mechanics in the building trades are kept busy the year around on the
improvements being made.

Below we give a list of the shipments of farm products from Hosmer during the
year 1922 and we leave the figures to speak for themselves:
 Hogs,            28 cars      $39,460.00
 Cattle,          43 cars       40,850.00
 Horses,           2 cars        3,170.00
 Poultry,          4 cars       17,500.00
 Wool,             1 car         9,000.00
 Hay,              3 cars          450.00
 Barley,           11 cars       8,200.00
 Wheat,           110 cars     167,480.00
 Flax,             14 cars      39,020.00
 Rye,              13 cars      11,560.00
 Potatoes,          3 cars       1,940.00
 Cream,     85,318 gallons      84,021.00
 Eggs,         3,751 cases      22,506.00
    Total                     $445,167.00

                The History of Hosmer South Dakota

The community of Hosmer was founded by Russian citizens of German descent and
culture who followed the promise of free land and freedom of religion to
America and then to Dakota Territory. As the expansion of the railroad moved
west so did the immigrants into Central Dakota Territory.

These German-Russians settled shortly after the townsite of Hosmer was platted
by F. P. Dobson working as a surveyor for the Chicago, Milwaukee, St. Paul &
Pacific Railroad. The official date of platting was April 29, 1887, and
endorsed by the county Registrar of Deeds on May 9, 1887.

The name for the town of Hosmer came from the townsite railroad agent's wife's
maiden name, Stella A. Hosmer, formerly of Lockport, Illinois.

Most of the earlier settlers were of German descent with a small percentage
each of Dutch-English, Scandinavian and Irish peoples. After a few years, or
sometimes only a few months, the settlers other than the German-Russians left,
discouraged by low rainfall, poorer land than expected, grasshopper
infestations, lack of trees and the Indian uprisings of late 1889 which were
greatly exaggerated by the time they reached the Hosmer area.

The German-Russians were used to these conditions, coming from South Russia
where they were raised and learned to endure under similar climatic conditions.
As some settlers left, others took over their land by quick-claim deed and so
slowly and carefully the German-Russians began to make headway in their new
home.

From 1887 until the towns official incorporation August 11, 1904, the town was
overseen by the county commissioners. Hosmer residents elected their first
board of trustees in 1904. In 1928, the town government changed to a mayor,
aldermanic form of governing. The first ordinance passed was one prohibiting
livestock from running loose in town. In 1908, the town received its first tax
income from real property --$62.87. An example of valuation around 1900 was 160
acres assessed at $345.00 for a total tax of $5.28, or an average dwelling in
Hosmer taxed at $2.89 a year for a $25.00 assessed value.

Some of the earlier buildings in Hosmer were the train depot, the Lutheran
church, McKoane's general store, a lumberyard and a hotel. Some highlights of
interest in the history of Hosmer include:
 1909: A fire alarm bell and a fire engine and hose are purchased at a cost of
 $2,500.00. Doctor Quinn opens a medical practice here.

 1917: Hosmer residents vote to make liquor sales illegal.

 1924: The Hosmer State Bank closed on May 15. From then until 1932 many banks
 closed their doors.

 1926: A franchise is granted to Northern States Power to furnish electrical
 power to Hosmer.

 1928: Jacob G. Walth was elected first Mayor.

 1930: A new City Hall was built at a cost of  $6,380.00. Two chemical engines
 are added to the fire department.

 1931 - 1938: A catastrophic drought with crop failures descends on the area
 leading to many business closings and farm failures.

 1939 - 1942: Farming again becomes profitable and prosperity comes back to
 farms and businesses.

 1946: The town's electric franchise was transferred to Montana-Dakota
 Utilities Company.

 1949: An 8" municipal well was dug and a water tower with a complete
 distribution system was constructed.

 1952: A municipal sewer system was installed eliminating the need for
 individual septic tanks.

 1961: The town becomes debt-free as bonds for the water and sewer projects
 were repaid.

 1965 - 1966: A forty-bed nursing home facility was constructed at a cost of
 $190,000.00.

 1968: A water treatment plant was built anticipating the drilling of a new
 well. The cost was $69,675.00.  Bonds were sold to finance some of the project.

 1969: A new well was drilled at a cost of $11,617.00.  A new well house,
 wiring and pumps were installed, also.

 1973: The city combines the office of treasurer and auditor to make a
 finance-officer system.

 1975: Streets and ditches are reshaped and graveled to prepare for oiling.

 1976: Hosmer city streets were paved for the first time at a cost of
 $38,253.00

 1979: Streets are resealed at a cost of $43,625.00.

 1981 - 1984: Hosmer experiences well trouble and is out of water for a time.
 New stainless steel pumps  and shafts were installed at a cost of $17,953.00.

 1985-1986: The complete water main system was replaced at a cost of
 $131,294.00, and the streets were  resealed at a cost of $46,531.00.

Hosmer was founded by a strong and determined people. They had and still have
faith in God and love of the land and the families they raised on it. We
citizens are privileged to celebrate the 100th birthday of Hosmer and should
look back with pride at our fathers and mothers, grandfathers and grandmothers
who "stuck it out." They endured drought and famine, fire and hailstorms,
grasshoppers, flu epidemics, blizzards and dust storms to give us what we have
today. Hosmer must look back for a moment, to be able to look ahead to the
future, and whatever is given to us to make the best of it and not give up. For
the sake of our ancestors, lets continue to make Hosmer the best possible place
to live and raise our children.

A SMALL TOWN

 Is a place many people leave,
 but they almost always come back --
 for an occasion, a celebration,
 a reunion, or for no reason at all.
 A small town
 Is a place wherein some spend a
 lifetime, with scattered excursions,
 or none at all.
 Where some, like birds, come home
 to roost, or to rest, or to stay.
 There isn't much to do, in a small town,
 that's unknown by many,
 There isn't much you know
 that many don't know you know,
 No tabloid needed.
 If "you want to know"...
 just meet on Main Street,
 In the P. O., in a store, at a corner,
 or bend in the road --
 Unload, share, care, if you will,
 but don't expect to keep a secret,
 (not for long)
 A small town is not meant for the recluse;
 The withdrawn are drawn out,
 the garrulous are heard.
 You can't always count on empathy,
 but you can almost always be sure of sympathy
 in a crisis, in a bind, in sorrow.
 A small town becomes extended family
 when you're on your own, or feel alone.
 A small town may not have the world,
 and all, to offer,
 But it offers all it has to all.
 A small town may not be much,
 But there's much to be said for
 A SMALL TOWN
        Arline Heckenlaible
        Hosmer, SD 57448

Hosmer's First Election Held

The first election was held on July 6; 1904, to elect a board of trustees,
result: F. W. Homeyer, Adam Pressler, and Henry Nusz, B. F. Pucket, Clerk;
Gotlob Blumhardt, Marshall. Shortly after Homeyer resigned, Knittel was
appointed in his place.

1st Ordinance: To Prohibit people from letting their stock run loose in town.

2nd Ordinance: To license the saloon and make a charge of $17.00 per month
payable in advance. This ordinance has been kicked around more than any other
ordinance in the book.

 1905 The same board held over.

 1906 Election results: Christoph Heckenlaible, Wm. Knittel, Jacob Kundert,
 Pucket, Clerk; Jacob Heckenlaible, Treas. The question: Shall liquor be
 legalized in Hosmer? Result: Yes, 18 No, 3. This question seems to have been
 voted on every year until it finally was voted against and Hosmer became dry
 in 1917.

 1907 Board as above. An assessor was appointed, Henry Nusz.

 1908 Board: Fred Kunde, John Haerter, Frank Boldt, Ed. Brinkma, Marshall;
 Martin Pressler, Treasurer. The first money coming from taxes was received by
 the town  treasurer from the county treasurer in the amount, $62.87. During
 the foregoing years the receipts of the town were exclusively for liquor
 licenses. So many were interested in dispensing liquor that the board raised
 the fee so high so that they could not afford to pay the license and
 eventually they got the saloons down to two. The license fee from 1909 on was
 $50.00 per month.

 1909 The above board held over. A fire engine was bought at this time and
 also hose and other equipment at a cost of $2500 which was on payments. The
 old Lutheran church was bought and used to store this fire fighting equipment.
 Uniforms for the band were bought at a cost of $150. Also purchased was a
 bell for giving the fire alarm. By this we can see that the town was growing.
 Doctor John Quinn arrived to open his practice in Hosmer. The salary of the
 Marshall was set at $75 per year.

 1910 Board: John Haerter, Wm. Knittel & Wm. Keim. John Quinn, Clerk. Dr. John
 Quinn was the first chief of the Fire Department. Pay scale for common labor
 was set at $.30 per hour.

 1911 Board: John Heckenlaible, Wm. Keim, Gottlieb Stoecker. John Wruck; Clerk.

 1912 Board: Jacob Heckenlaible, Gottlieb Stoecker and Jacob G. Walth. Wm.
 Knittel-Wm. Keim; Treasurer.

 1913 Board: John Heckenlaible, Adam Herrboldt and Chris Jakober. Adam
 Pressler Jr.; Clerk.

 1914 Board: John Haerter, G. Stoecker, Wm. Knittel. Geo Guthmiller; Clerk,
 John Haerter, Ben Ochs, Jacob Schlaht, Adam Pressler Jr.; Clerk, Martin
 Pressler, Ed. Brinkma, Frank Boldt, Ed. J. Herrboldt; Clerk-Jacob G. Walth;
 Treasurer.

 1917 to 1920 Inclusive records missing.

 1921 Board: Adam Pressler, John G. Kranzler, Frank Boldt, Ed. J. Herrboldt,
 Clerk. Jacob G. Walth, Treasurer.

 1922 Board: Jacob Heckenlaible, Wm. Knittel, & D. M. Joachim. A. Pressler
 Jr.; Clerk G. J. Aman: Assessor.

 1923 Same board except Emil Sonnenfeld; Clerk.

 1924 & 25 Board: Jacob Heckenlaible, G. Sto and John Schlaht. The question
 for opening Fourth Street to the east by moving stock yards shows up for the
 first time. The Hosmer State Bank closed May 15, 1924. From now on until 1932 a
 lot of bank failures are reported.

 1926 & 27 Board: M. Pressler, G. Stoecker & Frank Boldt. Albert J. Haerter;
 Clerk. Jacob Ct. Walth; Treasurer. Chris Maier appointed Town Marshall at $30.
 per month. Franchise granted to Northern States Power & Light Co. to furnish
 lighting and power to the town and residences. At this time the town was
 changed over to city Mayor and common council government.

 1928 Jacob G. Walth being elected as the first mayor of Hosmer; Aldermen:
 Adam Pressler, John Gutjahr, Jacob Lammle, A. Herrboldt, Wm. Meier and George
 Guthmiller. Albert Haerter; auditor-Ed. C. Ulmer; Treasurer.

 1929 Mayor-Jacob Heckenlaible; Aldermen: G. Guthmiller, G. Keszler, C. J.
 Huber, J. Gutjahr, Jacob Lammle, A. Herrboldt, Paul Boldt; Auditor, Ed. C.
 Ulmer; Treasurer.

 1930 New City Hall built at a cost of $6,380.00. Henry Hueter, Ashley, N. D.
 Contractor. Two Chemical Engines were added to the fire fighting equipment.

 1931 Mayor: Art J. Haerter; Alderman: G. Aman, G. Stoecker, Ed. Ritz, D.
 Bieber, J. Lammie, J. Dockter, Ted Stoecker; Auditor.

 1932 No change in the board.

 1933 No change in the board.

 1934 Mayor, Art Haerter; Aldermen; H. Krebs, G. Schaible, G. Kranzler, F.
 Boldt, J. Lammle, and J. Dockter. Ed. Pressler, Auditor.

 1935 Same

 1936 Mayor, Ed. Eisenbeisz; Aldermen: G. Aman, J. J. Mehlhoff, A. Stoecker,
 Ed. J. Herrboldt, G. Schabible. Auditor-Ed Pressler.

 1937 Same

 1938 &39 Mayor, Ed. Eisenbeisz; Aldermen: A. Stoecker, J. J. Mehlhoff, C. J.
 Huber, Gus Maisch, Elmer Hieb, H. Krebs. Auditor-Ed. Pressler

 1940 & 41 Same

 1942 Art Haerter, Mayor; Aldermen: Jacob Eisenbeisz, Carl Martel, G. Kiesz,
 G. Guthmiller, Gus Maisch, John Gisi. Mel Sonnenfeld-Auditor. Fred Martel-City
 Marshall, appointed, $45. per month.

 1944 Art Haerter, Mayor; Aldermen: P. Kunde, A. Dockter, A. Stoecker, Jacob
 Rueb, Carl Martel, G. Kranzler. Roland Jakober-Auditor.

 1945 Art Haerter, Mayor; Aldermen: G. Kiesz, Geo. Martel, Rudolph Dewald, J.
 G. Kranzler, Jacob Rueb, P. Boldt. Auditor-C. J. Huber. Erwin Haerter-City
 Attorney.

 1946 The board not changed. The question "Shall a liquor license be issued to
 the City" was defeated by a vote of 159 to 128. The electric franchise was
 transferred from the Northern States Power and Light Co., to The Montana-Dakota
 Utilities Co.

 1947 Jacob Sonnenfeld, Mayor, Alderman: Wm. Heer, Jacob Lammle, Geo. Martel,
 R. Dewald, E. Hauck, G. Kiesz. Again the liquor question was voted on and this
 time it carried by a vote of 162 to 134.

 1948 Mayor, Sonnenfeld; Aldermen: Wm. Heer, Wilmer Heckenlaible, Jacob
 Lammle, P. Hauck, Earl Walth, Carl Martel. At this time the city liquor store
 was established.

 1949 Elmer Hieb, Mayor; Aldermen: C. Gramm, G. Schaible, Sam Pietz, Geo.
 Keszler, Earl Walth, Wilmer Heckenlaible, Walter Grossman-Auditor; Ed. C.
 Ulmer, Treasurer. Bids were asked for a new well. At a special meeting held on
 April 29, 1949 bids were opened for the following: 8" well, the pump and
 lines, for water distribution system, for towers and tank. The following firms
 were the successful bidders: Independent Well Drilling Co., for 8" well
 $4,964.; Pump and Line Fairbanks Morse $4,225.90; for the water distribution
 system; Jacobs and Sons $57,871; for the tower and tank; Chicago Bridge and
 Iron Co. $17,700.00. George Keszler was hired as City Police for $180 per
 month.

 1950 Elmer Hieb, Mayor; Alderman: Earl Walth, Sam Pietz, Ed. Sonnenfeld,
 Jacob Mohr, Gerhardt Bauman, Wilmer Heckenlaible, Walter Grossman-Auditor. The
 water tank froze as the insulation was insufficient for this weather. Wm.
 Schilling was hired to take charge of the water system and electrical
 equipment in the pump house, read the meters, and collect the money for the
 water used. John Biederstedt was to make all taps and install the meters. He
 is to receive $5.00 for each tap and pay to the city $1.00 per tap for the use
 of the tapping machine. John Kramer was hired as chief of police at $135 per
 month.

 1951 Elmer Hieb, Mayor; Aldermen: Val Krumm, Jacob Mohr, J. Mehlhoff, Sam
 Job, G. Bauman, Sam Pietz, C. J. Gross-Police.

 1952 Elmer Hieb, Mayor; Aldermen: J. Mehlhoff, H. Krebs, Jacob Mohr, John
 Kramer, Sam Job, G. Bauman, Walter Grossman-Auditor.

 1953 Same members of board, except Geo. Keszler, Chief of Police at $245.00
 per month.

 1954 Arnold Pietz, Mayor; Aldermen: Milton Aman, Henry Krebs, Jacob Mohr,
 Carl Stoecker, G. Bauman. Ed. C. Ulmer-Treasurer; John Haerter-Auditor.

 1956 Mayor; Arnold Pietz; Aldermen: Leo Goehring, Morris Maisch, Reiny Walth,
 Ed. Mehlhaff, Jacob Sonnenfeld, Carl Stoecker. John Haerter-Auditor; Ed. C.
 Ulmer-Treasurer; J. Hettich-Chief of Police.

 1957 No change

 1958 C. J. Gross, Mayor; Aldermen: Leo Goehring, Wm. Heer, Ed. Mehlhaff, Sam
 Job, Milton Aman, G. Bauman.

 1959 Mayor, Gottlieb J. Walth; Aldermen: G. Bauman, L. Goehring, Ed. J.
 Walth, D. Jung, Sam Job, H. Krebs. John Haerter-Auditor; Ed. C.
 Ulmer-Treasurer.

 1960 Same board. Arrangement has been made to retire all bonds as they become
 due, which will be next November. This will make the city debt free by the
 time of our Jubilee celebration in 1962.

 1961 Mayor, Leo Goehring; Aldermen: Henry Eisenbeisz, G. Bauman, Ed. J.
 Walth, Sam Job, Clarence Dewald, H. W. Mehlhop. It may be of interest to the
 citizenry of Hosmer to know the cost of our water and sewer system all expense
 connected therein. We have tried to catch all items in connection with the
 system, which are the following:
 Engineer Fees                     $10505.00
 Well and Pump                       7777.00
 Tank and Tower                     15700.00
 Jacobs & Sons Construction        114983.00
 Legal Service (bonds)               1950.00
 Interest and Service charges       24460.88

                FAMILY HISTORIES

ALBERT and SELMA AMAN

Albert Aman, son of Jacob and Rose Aman, was born May 10, 1908, on a farm
northeast of Hosmer.  He was the second youngest of seven children.  His father
died when Albert was three years old.  His mother stayed on the farm until he
was eight years old when she moved into Hosmer.

Albert married Selma Ulmer in January, 1936.  Selma was born on May 25, 191 1,
in Menno, South Dakota and came to Hosmer when she was five years old.  Albert
was employed at the Hosmer Lumber Yard by Bill Maier at the time of his
marriage.  Albert and Selma have one son, Curtis, Hosmer, and a daughter, Faye
(Mrs. Dick Jones) of Ipswich. They have been blessed with seven grandchildren.
Albert worked for the state of South Dakota until his retirement in June, 1973.

Selma passed away on May 13, 1986.

THE CHRISTIAN AMAN FAMILY FARM HISTORY

4 Generations 1886-1986
Emanuel Aman Family
and Herbert Aman Family

Christian Aman was born December 12, 1857, in South Russia.  He was the son of
Andrew Aman.  He was married to Katharina Koerner in March, 1881, in Freeman,
South Dakota.  She was the daughter of Andrew and Elizabeth (Mehlhaff) Koerner.

They homesteaded six miles north of Hosmer, South Dakota in 1886.

They were blessed with nine children: Alvina was married to John Huber.  She
died October 19, 1960.  Rosa was married to Adolph Meidinger.  She died
December 19, 1967. Andrew was married to Elizabeth Koenig.  He died in May
1972.  Katharina was married to August Sayler.  She died December 26, 1972.
Edward was never married.  He died January 5, 1921.  Ida was married to
Christian Gross.  She died November 24, 1964.  Christ was married to Magdalena
Fischer.  He died May 17, 1973.  Otto was married to Anna Gross.  He died
November 11, 1978.  Emanuel was married to Margaret Hieb.  He died November 11,
1983.

Christian Aman died May 2, 1902.  His wife Katharina continued farming with her
children until she retired.  Her youngest son Emanuel took over the farm in
1920.  Christian and Katharina are both buried in the Homestead Cemetery with
one son, Edward.

Emanuel Aman was born August 7, 1900 in Hillsview, South Dakota.  He was the
son of Christian and Katharina Aman.  He married Margaret Hieb on November 23,
1920. She was the daughter of Jacob and Elizabeth (Renschier) Hieb of Hosmer.

They were blessed with nine children: Edward was married to Lavina Opp.  He
died September 13, 1978.  Alice was married to Elmer Eisenbeisz.  She died
February 25, 1979. Reuben is married to Irene Brandner.  They live in Abercleen,
South Dakota.  Bernice is married to Bill Ottenbacher.  They live in Aberdeen,
South Dakota.  Rose is married to Phillip Wolff.  They live in Eureka, South
Dakota.  Frances was married to Reuben Wolff.  He died in March 1973.  She
married Art Merkel in June, 1982.  They live in Aberdeen, South Dakota.  Calvin
died in November 1933 at the age of 22 years.  Herbert is married to Virginia
Forkel.  They live on the homestead at Hillsview, South Dakota.  Jean married
Leroy Forkel, but is divorced.  She lives in Rapid City, South Dakota.

Emanuel's wife died November 22, 1960.  He retired in 1958 and moved to Hosmer.
Emanuel married Rose Walth on June 8, 1962.  He died November 11, 1983.  His
youngest son, Herbert, took over the farm in 1958.

Herbert Emanuel Aman was born June 18, 1936, at Hillsview, South Dakota.  He is
the son of Emanuel and Margaret Aman.  He married Virginia Forkel, daughter of
Bill and Lorina (Kranzler) Forkel, on June 22, 1958 at Hosmer.

They were blessed with five children: Brian was born May 2, 1959.  He married
Shelly Eberhardt on May 30, 1981 and are the parents of one daughter, Tiffany
Margaret.  They reside in Warner, South Dakota.  Brian is employed with the
Burlington Northern Railroad and Shelly is employed with Super 8 Headquarters.

DuWayne was born April 28, 1960.  He married Agatha Kappenmann on November 17,
1979, at Hosmer.  They have two children, Jerrad DuWayne and Joshua Emanuel.
DuWayne and his family moved to the family farm in October, 1984.

Gwendolyn was born October 4, 1962.  She is presently working at a travel
agency in Darien, Connecticut.

Keith was born March 15, 1965.  He is working on his father's farm.

Audrey was born July 1, 1968.  She graduated from Hosmer High School in 1986.

Many stories could be told as this farm will be 100 years old in 1986.  As one
can look back, there were joyous times, hardships, many hours of hard work, and
many lifestyle changes with four generations.

CURTIS and KARIN AMAN

Curtis Aman was born January 7, 1939 at Aberdeen, South Dakota to Albert and
Selma (Ulmer) Aman.  He attended grade school and high school at Hosmer
graduating in 1956.  He spent one year at NSC before joining the army. After
spending two years in the army, he returned to Northern, graduating in 1962
with a B.S. degree in social science. Curt taught and coached at Roslyn, South
Dakota for three years.  In 1966, he received his Masters degree in school
administration at NSC.  He then spent the next 12 years as secondary principal
at Bristol, South Dakota.  In 1976, he moved back to Hosmer and became a cattle
buyer.  He is presently buying cows for Huron Dressed Beef and operates P & A
Trucking.

Curtis married Karin Monson from Langford, South Dakota on February 2, 1963 at
Aberdeen, South Dakota. Curtis and Karin have four children.

Jeffrey was born August 14, 1963 at Aberdeen.  He graduated from Hosmer High
School in 1981.  He is presently living in Hosmer and is in the cattle business
with his father. Jeff married Jane Heilman on September 2, 1982.  They have a
daughter, Jennifer, born May 4, 1985.

Kari was born July 6, 1967 at Webster, South Dakota. She graduated from Hosmer
High School in 1985 and is attending Northern State College, majoring in
business.

Dawn was born April 15, 1969 at Webster, South Dakota and Shane was born
December 12, 1973 at Webster.  Both are presently attending the Hosmer Schools.

DUWAYNE and AGATHA AMAN

DuWayne Aman was born April 28, 1960, to Herbert and Virginia (Forkel) Aman.
He grew up on a farm northwest of Hosmer, graduating from Hosmer High School in
1978, and attended Northern State College one year.  DuWayne was employed by
the Milwaukee Railroad in 1979.

Agatha Kappenman was born to Wilfred and Catherine (Heier) Kappenman on
February 3, 1961.  She grew up on a farm northwest of Hosmer and graduated from
Hosmer High School in 1979.

DuWayne and Agatha were married November 17, 1979, in Hosmer.  They established
their first home in Aberdeen where Agatha worked at Super City and DuWayne
worked for the Milwaukee Railroad until the embargo on March 1, 1980.

DuWayne then became employed by the South Dakota Wheat Growers of Roscoe, where
they lived until 1984.  The then moved to the farm where DuWayne was raised.

They are the parents of two sons, Jerrad and Joshua.

ELMER and IDA AMAN

Elmer Aman was born to Christ and Magdalina Aman on February 22, 1921, on a
farm northwest of Hosmer.  Elmer attended country school.  In December 1941, he
was united in marriage to Ida Schick, daughter of August and Lydia Schick of
Bowdle.  Ida was born April 2, 1924.  Elmer and Ida lived on the farm 7 1/2
miles northwest of Hosmer from 1946 to 1985 when they built a home in Hosmer
and that is where the are presently residing.  Two children were born to this
marriage. Maurice was born April 26, 1942, and married Caro Dockter, they have
two sons.  They live on the home farm. Janice was born March 18, 1944, and is
married to Denni Cleland and they live in Aberdeen, South Dakota.

EMIL J. AMAN FAMILY

Mrs. Martha Aman was born on September 23, 1905 to John L. Job (May 30,
1868-August 4, 1936) and Katrina Jo (August 20, 1878-May 30, 1968).  In 1903,
they immigrate from Neudorf, South Russia.  Martha's father worked for several
cattle ranchers upon arriving in South Dakota.  Due to relocating, distance to
a school, and a meager early education, Martha managed in her own fashion.  The
Jobs homesteaded on 160 acres of land seven miles northeast of Hosmer. Martha
had four brothers and a sister.  Adolph, August, and Emma are all deceased.
Walter resides in California and Rueben lives in Frederick, South Dakota.

In 1925, Martha Job married Emil J. Aman.  Emil was born on May 17, 1904, to
Carolina Tesky (November 22, 1867 in Ukraine Region of South Russia-November
20, 1949) and Jacob Aman (June 1, 1863 in Bergdorf Village of Russia-September
14, 1927).  Emil attended grammar school for eight years and later graduated
from Cleveland Township #2, McPherson County.  He remained on the original
homestead of his parents located six miles northeast of Hosmer.  Prior to
Emil's birth, two children died in infancy: E. J. and Ottila. The Amans had
three more children in addition to Emil.  They were Gottlieb, Christina
Kirschman, and Albina Schaffer.

Both families attended the Reformed Church to express their faith in God.  Much
time and energy was spent preserving, repairing, and restoring the farm; it was
necessary to be a jack-of-all-trades to get ahead.  Some winters blizzards hit
the area.  It then became a grand effort to reach the school house by horse and
buggy or on foot.  Weary though they were, they would keep going only by
exercising their last ounces of strength.  Without phones they anxiously awaited
the mail, bringing news from the outside world.

If imagining be permitted, start with teams of horses for power and small
equipment.  Then look ahead to see hopes gradually realized.  It was on to
modern equipment meaning the labor was less arduous and the results more
satisfactory even though the operation of farming was run on a somewhat small
scale.

Comments about farming then were: hard work, thrift, dedication, and good
management.  Martha raised a huge garden.  Responsibilities were thrown on the
courageous woman's shoulders.  Having no one to tend to her little ones she
took them along to the field where she worked.  It goes without saying that
they met and overcame all hardships and privations faced by many families in
those times.  There was not much said about the hardships; they took it all as
a matter of course.

Every member of Emil and Martha's family was able to work industriously.
Tragedy entered the family when a son had a fall resulting in a paralytic
condition and convulsions. A journey to Mayo Clinic in Rochester showed most
distressing test results.  The best medical aid in those days proved futile
with or without surgery.  A short or long life despite the injury could be
expected.  Martha tenderly cared for this child.  He was afflicted with a
severe visual impairment.  Thus, his enjoyment in life was marred as was the
assurance of a comparatively carefree old age.

Emil was the type of person, once having an object in mind, worked
energetically and tirelessly to realize the goal of his ambition.  As the
children grew older, Mr. Aman would have liked to turn the family farm over to
another son.  Ray had other interests.  He graduated from the University of
South Dakota with a degree in business and moved to Denver. Emil trained his
girls to become farmhands and was successful.

Mr. Aman's retirement was prompted by a heart condition. After retirement his
health improved.  With the children of the family settled in their lives, the
elder Amans moved to Hosmer accompanied by their son, Milbert.  Here they
planned to spend their old age at ease.  Emil J. Aman died suddenly in 1979.

The Amans had two sons and four daughters.

Irene married Elmer Burrer and lives in Mitchell, South Dakota.  Irene and
Elmer have two daughters.  Dr. Debra Schuster is married to Kurt Schuster,
Madison, Wisconsin. Julie is married to Kirk Hammon, Pensacola, Florida.  Julie
and Kirk have three sons: Jacob, Kenton, Stuart.

Ray lives in Denver, Colorado.

Milbert lives in Bowdle, South Dakota.

LaVerne married Ervin Opp and lives in Eureka, South Dakota.  LaVerne and Ervin
had four sons.  Arnold (deceased) married Susan Punton, Fargo, North Dakota.
Arnold and Susan had three daughters: Sheri, Shawn, Shannon.  Kenneth married
Jeannie Streeter, Eliendale, North Dakota.  Kenneth and Jeannie have three
children: Camie, Kara, Ryan.  Dr. Darold married Pam Gruneich, Aberdeen, South
Dakota. Mark lives in Eureka, South Dakota.

Florence married Lawrence Opp and lives in Sioux Falls, South Dakota.  Florence
and Lawrence have one daughter. Charlotte attends college.

Delores (deceased) married Duane Neuharth, Redwood Falls, Minnesota.  Delores
and Duane had two sons.  Steve lives in Phoenix, Arizona and Brian attends
college.

ERWIN and CHRISTINA AMAN

Erwin R. Aman was born on November 14, 1917, in McPherson County, Hosmer, South
Dakota, to Jacob H. Aman and Louise Barth Aman.

He married Christina Eisenbeisz, daughter of David M. Eisenbeisz and Juliana
Rohrbach Eisenbeisz, on January 21, 1940, at St. John's Lutheran Church in
Hosmer, South Dakota.  Christina was born on August 11, 1921, in Edmunds County.

After their marriage they moved to a farm five miles northeast of Hosmer where
they still reside.

Erwin and Christina have three children: Harlan, who has established his
residence on the family homestead along with his wife, Janice, and their six
children.  Lester, who also lives on the family homestead with his wife, Carol,
and their three children.  Lucille, who married Dean Gross of Bowdle, South
Dakota, resides in Aberdeen, South Dakota.  Lucille and Dean have one son.

Erwin and Christina have been members of St. Paul's Congregational Church, now
the United Church of Christ.

GOTTLIEB and EMMA AMAN

Gottlieb Aman, son of Jacob and Carolina Tesky Aman was born May 11, 1890, six
miles east of Hillsview, South Dakota.

Emma Hieb Aman was born July 4, 1892, fourteen miles east of Hosmer, South
Dakota, to Jacob and Elizabeth Renschler Hieb.  Emma and Gottlieb grew up on
their parents' farms.

Gottlieb and Emma were united in marriage December 2, 1912, at the Heidelberg
Reformed Church by Rev.  Zenk.  The couple lived on the farm with his parents
until a new home was built, only a short distance from the homestead.  The
couple engaged in farming until 1919, when Gottlieb purchased a grain elevator
in Hosmer from Jacob Heckenlaible.

In 1936, the grain elevator burned down.  He then did carpenter work until
1940, when he became employed at the Farmers Co-op Elevator in McLaughlin,
South Dakota.  In 1942, the family moved to McLaughlin and in 1945, he became
manager of Central Lumber Co. until he retired in 1955.

They continued to live in McLaughlin until they entered the Senior Citizens
Home at Hosmer in 1973.  Gottlieb passed away on January 19, 1975.  Emma passed
away July, 1986.

The couple had three children: Eugene, born on May 17, 1917, is living in
McLaughlin, and is married to Mary Laemmle. Eugene graduated from Hosmer High
School in 1935.

Vera and Verna were born May 17, 1923.  Verna passed away at the age of seven
months.  Vera is married to Gust Adam and lives in McLaughlin.  They have four
children. Vera graduated from Hosmer High School in 1941.

HARLAN E. and JANICE AMAN

Harlan Aman was born April 19, 1940, to Erwin and Christina (Eisenbeisz) Aman.
He married Janice L. Goltz who is the daughter of John G. and Rose (Perman)
Goltz of Mobridge.

Harlan and Janice have six children: Jeff, Kelly, Christopher, LeRoy, Tina, and
Donald.

Harlan and his wife and family live on the farm five miles north of Hosmer.
Harlan's parents, along with his brother, Lester, live on the same farm.
Harlan and his brother own and operate Aman Trucking.

HERBERT and ERNA AMAN

Herbert C. Aman was born on November 8, 1911 to Jacob and Rosina (Goehring)
Aman in McPherson County six miles northeast of Hosmer.  When he was eight
years old he moved to Hosmer with his mother.  He worked for Christ
Kirschenmann until 1934, when he was joined in marriage with Erna Weisz.

Erna was born in Temvick, North Dakota to Peter and Carolina (Heyne) Weisz on
October 16, 1917.

Their union was blessed with two children, LaVern (Mrs.  Harry Rieger) and
Betty (Mrs.  Milton Mohr).

Herbert was mail carrier on the Star Route fro 1934-1938.  Then he was a
sub-clerk in the post office and sub carrier for mail route for 25 years and in
1972 he retired.

Erna was an Avon sales representative for 23 years in the Hosmer area.

Herbert died on December 8, 1983.

JACOB and LOUISA AMAN

Jacob Aman was born on October 2, 1895, in McPherson County, six miles
northeast of Hosmer, South Dakota, to Jacob H. Aman Sr. and Rosina Goehring
Aman.

Louisa Barth Aman was born on November 7, 1895, in Glueckstal, South Russia to
William and Barbara Henne Barth.  She moved with her family to South Dakota in
1898. They settled on a farm about eight miles northeast of Hosmer, South
Dakota.

Jacob and Louisa were married November 30, 1916. They made their home one-half
mile south of his father's farm.  There he built up their farm.  Six children
were born to Jacob and Louisa.  Erwin, November 14, 1917; Julius, March 25,
1921; Edmund, January 16, 1924; Milton, December 15, 1927; Lorrine, July 15,
1929; Jacob M., April 28, 1935.  Lorrine passed away August 29, 1933, of
intestinal inflammation.

Jacob and Louisa lived on the farm until 1950 when they retired and moved to
Hosmer May 30th.  They lived in their home until 1985, when because of failing
health they moved to the Senior Citizen's Home in Hosmer.  Louisa passed away
on May 6, 1986.

JEFFREY and JANE AMAN

Jeff was born August 14, 1963, to Curtis and Karin Aman.  Jane was born January
31, 1964, to Christ and Agatha Heilman.

Jeff graduated from Hosmer High School in 1981 and Jane in 1982.  They were
married September 4, 1982, and since that time have made their home is Hosmer.

Jeff is self-employed as a cattle buyer.  Jane graduated from Presentation
College, Aberdeen, in 1984, with an Associate Degree in registered nursing.
She is presently employed at the Hosmer Senior Citizen's Home.

They have one daughter, Jennifer Mary, born May 4, 1985.

LESTER and CAROL AMAN

Lester Aman was born October 8, 1946, to Erwin and Christina Aman at Eureka,
South Dakota.  Carol Aman was born on May 12, 1948, to Henry and Ella Weisz at
Bowdle, South Dakota.  They are living on the farm 5 1/2 miles north of Hosmer
engaged in grain and cattle farming.  Lester, along with his brother, Harlan,
also own and operate Aman Trucking.

Lester and Carol were married December 18, 1964 at Bowdle.  This marriage has
