                Businesses - Old and New

BOWLING ALLEY

The Bowling Alley building was constructed in the early 1920's by Samuel
Peterson.  It was built and used as a dance hall and later was also used for
roller skating and living quarters for Mr. Peterson.  After Mr. Peterson's
death in 1943, the building was owned by the Samuel Peterson Estate, with
Rebecca Erickson being the administrator.

On November 16, 1945, Albert Schlaht and Sam Job purchased the building and
used it for a blacksmith shop.

On August 31, 1962, Verna and Albert Schlaht purchased the building and
converted it into a bowling alley.

Robert Stoecker and Lester Preszler purchased the bowling alley from the
Schlahts on August 30, 1969.  In December, 1969, Lester sold his share to
Robert.

On July 30, 1974, Dennis and Frances Ellsworth purchased the building.  They
built a 25' x 50' addition to it, and used it as a recreation center and cafe.
This addition was later remodeled into living quarters.

On May 22, 1980, Roger and Kathy Gramm purchased the building and started a
motorcycle repair shop in a part of it, and the rest is used for storage.
Lawrence Mohr is also renting part of the building for his auction service.

FARMERS ELEVATOR COMPANY, INC.

The present elevator was built in 1926 by John Kranzler. He operated it until
November, 1944, when it was sold to Carl Stoecker and Jacob Goehring.  In
January, 1950, Carl sold his interests to Goehring and his son, Leo.  In July,
1958, Leo sold his interests to his father, who operated the elevator until
Lyle Clopine and Harold Peterson bought him out in 1959.

This new partnership lasted for only a short time due to a stroke suffered by
Mr. Peterson.  From February, 1960, to February, 1967, at which time the
elevator was destroyed by fire, Lyle Clopine operated the business under the
name of the Farmers Elevator Company of Hosmer.

G. J. WALTH & CO., INC.

One of the oldest business places in Hosmer was the store known as the Super
Valu Store, or G. J. Walth & Co. This building was built in 1898 by the
Heckenlaible brothers and enlarged in 1904.  In 1904 it incorporated under the
name of Hosmer Merchantile Company, the corporation consisted of the following
stockholders: Jacob Heckenlaible, John Heckenlaible, Frank Boldt, William
Gundert, William Knittel, and Gottlieb Gross.  This corporation operated until
1910 when the stock was sold to Frank Boldt and Christ Jakober. In 1919 this
partnership dissolved and Christ Jakober became the sole owner.

In 1933, Christ Jakober sold one-half of the stock to Edwin Eisenbeisz and upon
the death of Mr. Jakober in 1935 the remainder of his stock was sold to Albert
Stoecker.  In October of 1940, Stoecker and Eisenbeisz sold this business to
Gottlieb J. Walth.

In 1943, frozen food storage lockers were added to the store.  In 1945, more
frozen food storage lockers were added and the store was remodeled.  In 1948,
this business was incorporated under the name of G. J. Walth & Co., Inc. with
the stockholders being G. J. Walth, Emma Walth, Vera Pietz, Arnold Pietz, Earl
Walth, Nora Danduran and Ellen Hoffman. The store was again remodeled and a new
brick front was added.  In 1966, the floor space of this business was enlarged
by moving the dry goods department into the neighboring building that was
previously a bowling alley.  The dry goods and the groceries were joined by an
opening between the two stores.  In 1969, the dry goods store was liquidated
and the grocery store was sold to James Bauer.

GUTHMILLER'S FURNITURE AND KOERNER'S NYAL STORE

George Guthmiller came to the United States with his parents at the age of
twelve.  They settled at Marion, South Dakota.  In 1911, George and his wife
moved to the Hosmer area.

They settled on a farm five miles east and one mile north of Hosmer.  Before
the year was over they bought a home in Hosmer from Willie Kundert and moved to
town.

In 1913, Mr. Guthmiller entered the hardware and furniture business with
partners Adam Pressler, Sr. and Adam Pressler, Jr. In 1924, this partnership
was dissolved, and George took over the furniture store and Adam Sr. took over
the hardware store.

After their marriage in 1929, Gust and Martha Koerner purchased the Nyal Drug
Store from Mr. "Duke" Hembd, and operated the store in the corner building on
Main Street, where Stoecker's Jewelry Store is located.

In 1942, Gust and Martha, daughter and son-in-law of George Guthmiller,
remodeled the front portion of Guthmiller's Furniture Store, and moved the Nyal
Drug Store into this part and also started a cafe along with this business.

Gust and Martha operated their business along with the furniture store for
forty-eight years.  On May 20, 1977, they sold the business to Lester Preszler
and went into retirement.

Mr. Guthmiller died on July 13, 1961, at the age of 84 years.  Gust Koerner
died on May 8, 1980, at the age of 73 years.  Martha is living in the Senior
Citizen's Home in Hosmer.

HIEB ELECTRIC

The former Hieb Electric building, now owned by Merle Hieb, was built in 1921
and operated by Reinhardt and Art Kunde as a butcher shop.  Later Art and Paul
Kunde bought the business.

In 1939, Henry and Albert Hieb opened a Red and White Store.  In March, 1941,
the Hieb Brothers sold the store to Roland Jakober and the inventory was moved
out leaving the building vacant until the summer of 1941 when Edmund Sonnenfeld
opened an Ice Cream Store.

In January, 1942, Joe Gisi purchased the business and sold it in May of the
same year to Annie Schock.  She operated a cafe in it until October, 1942, when
a Red and White Store was opened by Roland Jakober.

In May, 1945, the Red and White Store changed hands with Wilmer Heckenlaible as
manager.  In September, 1950, Art Pfaff purchased the business, and operated
the store until October, 1956, when he sold out to Art Scherf of Roscoe, who
operated the store for about one month.  The inventory and fixtures were sold
to G. J. Walth and Company.

In the spring of 1957, Ferdinand Hieb purchased the building and remodeled it
into living quarters in the back and the front was used as a combination
Insurance Office, Radio and TV, Electrical Supplies and Paint Shop.  In May of
1958, Mr. and Mrs. Hieb and 4 children moved into their new home.  Before
moving to town the Hiebs farmed 10 miles northeast of Hosmer.

In 1972, the business was sold to Merle Hieb and family. Since 1979 the
building has been vacant.

THE HOSMER HOTEL

The Hosmer Hotel was built as early as 1901.  As far as we have been able to
find out, B. F. Puket built it.  We have not been able to learn who the first
manager of the hotel was. In 1905, Mr. & Mrs. J. F. Geeslin came to Hosmer and
shortly after became the occupants and managers of the Hotel. Mrs. Geeslin had
at first been appointed as depot agent of the Milwaukee station here in Hosmer.
This office was later handled by her son Keil.

In those days traveling salesmen would travel by train with a lot of trunks and
would stay in the hotel for a week or longer at a time, so hotel business was a
paying business.  Mrs. Geeslin was known to be a good cook and also manager.
They always had local men who made the hotel their home, as Bill Meyer, Mr.
Pucket, Dr. Quinn, Dr. Grace, Mr. Dudley, who later married Florence Geeslin,
and others.  The country teachers would make the hotel their weekend home.

Some years later the Post Office was moved to the hotel which gave the Geeslins
some more work.  This office stayed in the hotel until after the first World
War when John Schoch took it into his business place.  This building, after
many years of service, was torn down with the lumber being used in the building
of a barn on a local farm.

In 1919, the Geeslins left Hosmer to make their home in California and spend
the rest of their days in a warmer climate.  Mr. Geeslin died at the age of 86
and Mrs. Geeslin died in 1951 at the age of 95 years.

Pictured below is the waiting room of the Hosmer Hotel. Mr. Geeslin, owner, is
standing behind the counter.  Also appearing on the picture are two traveling
men and Kyle Geeslin.

        Pictured above is the Hotel Dining Room with Florence (Flossy) Geeslin.

HOSMER PRODUCE

In 1925, Jacob J. Schumacher put up a building which John Stoecker used for a
cream station.  He sold his business to Carl Gramm.  In 1952, Mr. Gramm sold
the business to Julius Schnabel.  In February of 1953, August Dockter purchased
the business and later on the building; when Jacob Schumacher passed away.  Mr.
Dockter continued operating a cream station until 1966 when Herbert and Sheila
Graff bought the business and building.  They bought cream and eggs with the
adjoining room being used as a shoe repair shop, which Herbert also operated.
This section of the building had at one time been a watch repair shop which was
operated by August and Jake Dockter.

In the early 40's, Leonora Weller (Mrs.  Martin Imbery) operated a beauty shop
in this section with Esther Mohr helping Miss Weller in her business.  The
Graffs were in business for two years.  Jacob Rohrbach rented the building from
1970-1973 buying cream and eggs.  In 1975, the building was sold to Janice
Malsam who opened a craft shop which was named the "Believe it or not" shop.
In 1981, Emma Feyereisen bought the building and opened E T Dairy which she
operated until 1985.  At present the building is unoccupied.

THE HOSMER ROLLER MILL COMPANY

The Hosmer Roller Mill Company was built and operated by Fred Kunde, Adam
Preszler, and Fred Sonnenfeld in 1901.

The exterior of the building is pictured in the first picture below.  The
second picture shows the interior.

Later in 1917, the owners installed the power plant shown in the last picture
and supplied electrical power to the city as well as for their own needs.

The Hosmer Roller Mill Company operated in this community until it was
completely destroyed by fire in 1930.

HOSMER STATE BANK

The Hosmer State Bank was incorporated under the laws of the state of South
Dakota in 1901, by the following incorporators: B. F. Pluckett, Christoph
Heckenlaible and Jacob Kundert.  Four years later, Kundert sold his shares to
John and Jacob Heckenlaible.  The officers were President, John Heckenlaible;
Vice President, Jacob Heckenlaible; Cashier: B. F. Puckett.  The Board of
Directors was the same.

This bank operated until 1918, when it was sold to C. Vorlander of Eureka, who
had a number of banks in North and South Dakota.  The Hosmer Bank was handled
by President, Vorlander; Vice President, William Keim; Cashier, D. M. Joachin.
During the depression many banks were closed with the First National Bank in
Eureka one of the first to close its doors.  The Hosmer State Bank closed on
May 15, 1924.

This well known Hosmer land mark was wrecked on May 9, 1961, to make room for
our new Post Office.

JACOB MOHR & SONS CONSTRUCTION COMPANY

Jacob Mohr began his history of construction in the Hosmer area by plastering
homes in the late forties.  Jacob worked with his brother, Theobald, and
brothers-in-law, August Martel and Reinhold Maisch.  In the early fifties they
began doing brick and block work along with plastering.

In 1957, Ray joined in the construction work and they organized as J and R
Construction.  It was at this time that they had their first bonded job which
was putting in the addition between the two existing school buildings.

In 1959, Jacob and sons, Ray and Werner, reorganized as Jacob Mohr & Sons
Construction Company.  Their work included all types of general construction.
Milton Mohr, Jacob's nephew, joined the company as a partner in 1962. The
Company continued to do commercial and residential construction until 1983 when
the business was dissolved because of poor economic conditions.

They erected buildings in McLaughlin, Mahto, Miller, Gettysburg, Hoven,
Ipswich, Roscoe, Bowdle and Eureka.

In Hosmer they built the Holy Trinity Catholic Church, Hosmer Nursing Home,
Hosmer Post Office, Municipal Liquor Store, Jacob's Fairway, John Heckenlaible
Corp. and several homes.

KNITTEL GENERAL MERCHANDISE STORE

This picture shows the interior of the General Merchandise Store owned by
William Knittel with Mrs. Fred Hertel, as a girl, and Emanual Murshel taken in
1908.

Mr. Knittel purchased this store from Mr. Hasen in 1907. The next picture shows
Christ Huber, Dan Reide, Mrs. Hertel, Mrs. Knittel and Jacob Schumacker taken
in 1910.  At this time the front part of the building was used as a general
merchandise store, while the back was used as a cream station.

Later, the upper part of the store was torn down. However, this building served
the community as a cream station, barber shop, and Hosmer Liquor store.  This
building was eventually torn down and the present city Liquor Store was erected
at this location.

MARTEL'S CREAM STATION

The Cream Station operated by Carl Martel had been used as a cream buying
station for thirty-two years.  It was owned and operated by the Equity Union
Creamery, Inc. of Aberdeen for eight years.  After Equity Union went out of
business, Carl operated the business as an independent.

During the time that Carl bought cream, the lowest price paid to the farmer was
II during the dry 30's.  The highest prices paid were in 1947 and 1948 when the
price rose to .95 a pound for butter fat.  In 1962, the price averaged .60 a
pound for butter fat.

During this time, the most butter fat Carl purchased in any one year was in
1947 when he bought over 125,000 pounds.

Carl is now retired and is residing in the Senior Citizen's Home in Hosmer.

CHICAGO, MILWAUKEE, ST.  PAUL & PACIFIC RAILROAD COMPANY

By the fall of 1886 most of the road bed for the train had been started and
partly completed.  In the spring of 1887 some crews worked north of Hosmer,
others finished the bed south of Hosmer, and another crew started laying ties
and rails. Some of the men were experts in driving the spikes and prided
themselves on how many they could do in a day.  By July, 1887, the railroad was
completed as far as Eureka.

The people in the Hosmer area were used to seeing work trains bring materials
to build the railroad, but the anticipation of the first passenger train was
great.  Hearsay has it that on the morning the first passenger train was to
arrive all of the area people were at the depot waiting.  All of a sudden the
whistle could be faintly heard in the distance, all was silent, and then
another whistle!  Then the black coal smoke was spotted, and it came closer and
closer, and finally the whole train was visible coming over the rise a mile
south of town. What excitement!  As the locomotive got closer, the people
backed away from the tracks as the locomotive made a terrible noise.  The
wheels started to squeal and slip and finally it came to a stop.  The conductor
stepped out, looked at his big railroad watch which he had on a chain in his
vest pocket, and said "just on time."

As immigrants from Russia were coming everyday, it was not surprising that
several families of immigrants were on that first train.  Relatives of these
immigrants were at the depot trying to persuade them to get off at Hosmer, but
because their ticket said Eureka they refused.  They were not going to break
any rules in this new country.

Train service through Hosmer to Eureka was begun on July 24, 1887, with the
following schedule:
Train No. 15           leave Eureka                3:15 p.m.
                       leave Hillsview             3:56 p.m.
                       leave Hosmer                4:32 p.m.
                       arrive Roscoe               5:30 p.m.
Train No. 13           leave Roscoe                9:40 a.m.
                       leave Loyalton             10:22 a.m.
                       leave Millard              10:59 a.m.
                       leave Faulkton             12:05 p.m.
                       arrive Orient              12:40 p.m.
Train No. 14           leave Orient                3:30 p.m.
                       arrive Roscoe               5:55 p.m.
Train No. 16           leave Roscoe               10:00 a.m.
                       leave Hosmer               10:56 a.m.
                       arrive Eureka              12:15 p.m.

The remainder of the line north of Eureka to Linton, ND, was constructed in
1903.

Because of the train being able to bring food and supplies, the town began to
grow.  The sod shanties soon disappeared and small homes and businesses were
built of lumber.

Agents who have been stationed here were: R. F. Walker, 1901; J. O. Steffre,
1903; Neshion and Mrs. J. F. Geeslin, 1904-1922; Ed Shelb, 1923-1930; Nickolas,
1930-1933; Coleman, 1933-1935; Earl Twedt, 1935-1939; E. C. Weatherly,
1939-1944; Art Schultz, 1944-1946; Gordon Beck, 1946-1948; Milberf Lammle,
1948-1949; Ardner Fasching, 1949-1950; H. W. Mehlhop, 1950-1964; Ole Olson,
1964-1965; Melvin Geist, 1965-September, 1966; and until December 20, 1966
Milbert Lammle, Roscoe, SD, handled whatever business was necessary.

Section foremen have been: Tony Bitz, 1916; John Krischofski, 1916-1922;
Miller, 1922-1923; Henry Krebs, 1923-1957 (retired); and from July 1, 1957, the
section had been abolished with the Eureka crew caring for the track.

Daily passenger train service was discontinued in the 1930's.  This train ran
from Roscoe to Linton and returned daily.  A freight train also went north one
day and returned the next.  There was only one major accident in the immediate
area which was on February 12, 1923, during a big snow storm.  The passenger
train was derailed three miles south of town causing the day coach to be
overturned and several passengers injured.

In 1960 the depot was torn down to be replaced with a smaller vacant depot
brought in from McIntosh, SD.  How else would it be moved to Hosmer other than
on a flat car on rails.  This depot was used until 1966 when the train service
ended and the little depot was again on its way to another home in Regent, ND.
The railroad company sold the tracks and ties which were removed in 1981.  In
the spring of 1983 the railroad right-of-way was sold mainly to individuals
with adjoining land.  So the history of the Milwaukee Railroad in Hosmer
spanned almost a century.

PIETZ REPAIR SHOP

Gust and Maggie Pietz Family

Gust Pietz and his wife Magdelina Dockter were united in marriage on November
8, 1924.  They farmed 10 miles northeast of Hosmer until 1946 when they moved
to Hosmer. They were blessed with 2 sons, Alexander, who is farming the home
farm and Alfred who passed away while living in Washington.

When Gust first moved to Hosmer he worked for the Ford Garage for nine months.
In March, 1947, Gust went into business with Gust Job.  The business was
located in the former Lone Tree Repair Shop.  The two ex-farmers were willing
to tackle any repair job that came along -- from the smallest motor to the
largest truck or tractor in need of service.

In the summer of 1949, the business was moved to the present location known as
the Blacksmith and later as Cox Plumbing.

In February, 1954, the partnership was dissolved and became Pietz Repair Shop.
Gust continued the repair service as well as handling Phillips 66 gas and oil
products, as long as his health permitted.

In June, 1966, Gust had to close the business due to poor health.  His health
continued to fail and he passed away on September 21, 1971.

Maggie boarded teachers for 7 years, plus some baby sitting until 1974 when she
moved into the Central Apartments where she presently resides and continues to
baby sit.

G. SCHAIBLE MOTOR SALES

The G. Schaible Motor Sales was the Pontiac and Buick Garage at Hosmer, South
Dakota.  It was owned and operated by Gottlieb Schaible.  Mr. Schaible
purchased his first garage in 1926, located in the north part of Hosmer, on the
present site of Heier's Meat Market.  In the early 1940's, Mr. Schaible moved
his business to Hosmer's Main Street.  He purchased the former Thompson Lumber
Yard from his father-in-law, Martin Preszler, and converted it into the
Pontiac-Buick Garage and Standard Service Station.

Mr. Schaible was a well-known and successful business man in Hosmer until his
untimely death in March, 1952.  He died of a cerebral hemorrhage at the age of
53.

Mr. & Mrs. Gottlieb Schaible are the parents of Ella Bauer, Lavina Heer and
DarLou Ernst.  Mrs. Christine Schaible Buechler resides in Lodi, California.

STANDARD OIL COMPANY

After having an auction sale of his farm equipment in 1928, Martin Schaible,
with his wife and two children, moved to Hosmer to operate the Standard Oil Co.
business.  During this time, the price of 'Red Crown' gasoline was 17.5 to 20.8
cents per gallon/tax included and tractor fuel was a dime.  For most of this
period of time, the South Dakota motor fuel tax was 4 cents per gallon and the
federal tax was I cent.

Shown in the picture, which was taken about 1935, are two of his sons, Wilmer
and Delbert.

The family, with their 7 children, moved to Bowdle in November of 1941.

UNDER THE SPREADING CHESTNUT TREE--

The blacksmith was a necessary individual in the early pioneer days,
consequently, there was a blacksmith shop quite early in Hosmer.  The first
blacksmith seems to have been George Abell.  In 1899, we find that someone else
had taken over; namely, John Muehheiser.

The spot where the smithy stood was close to the livery stable.  Some years
later Frederick Gutjahr took over and had the shop until he moved to Leola.
Later John Schlaht & his brother, Christian, operated the shop until Christian
started a pool hall and barber shop.  The original smithy was located where
Gust Pietz had his repair shop.  When John Schlaht retired, his son, Albert and
son-in-law, Sam Job took over the business and moved it into the building which
they bought from the Samuel Peterson estate.

WALTH MOTOR COMPANY

The Walth Motor Company was started August 1, 1947, under the name of Pietz and
Walth Motor Company, when the business was bought from Jacob Heckenlaible
(deceased) and son, Edwin, by Samuel Pietz and Reinhold P. "Chip" Walth.

The business consisted of a Chevrolet dealership, repair shop and hardware
store.  It also ran 2 to 4 school buses from 1954 to 1967.

On July 1, 1961, the partnership was dissolved, at which time Samuel Pietz sold
his half share of the business to Reinhold Walth.  The name was changed to
Walth Motor Company.  The business continued until May 2, 1973, when it was
destroyed by fire.

Employees of Walth Motor Co. were Dan Stabbe, Albert Stabbe, Roger Gramm and
Dale Docktor as mechanics.  Store clerks were Marvin Kunde and Reuben Neifer.

A. HERRBOLDT & COMPANY

A. Herrboldt & Co. has been in business since 1919.  The first business
partners were Adam Herrboldt, Edwin J. Herrboldt, Gottlieb J. Walth, and Eugene
Huber.  Sales, service and repair were included along with the complete line of
farm implements, tractors, threshers and cars.

The International Harvestor franchise was acquired as well as J. I. Case.
Overland and Star cars were also sold for a few years.  Farmers were going into
smaller tractors and threshers as opposed to the large steam threshing machines.
The company capitalized on these small machines as soon as they had proven
themselves satisfactory, selling the last steam engine to Ochs and Imbery in
1920.

During the years of depression that followed, business was very slow.  In 1924,
Walth left the partnership, followed by Huber in 1927.  Arthur Herrboldt joined
the firm in 1926.

The partnership included Edwin J. Herrboldt, Arthur J. Herrboldt, and Arnold
Herrboldt, until the death of Edwin in 1964, upon which time his wife Olga
became a senior partner. After her death in 1977, Arnold Herrboldt became the
controlling partner; Arthur and Milton Herrboldt were sharing partners.  Art
Herrboldt passed away in 1983.  Arnold and Arlene Herrboldt bought the shares
of the company from Milton Herrboldt in 1984.

Roger Gramm, Dennis Gab and Herbert Graff are presently employed with A.
Herrboldt & Co., the name that has been retained since its beginning.

DALE'S AUTO TRIM & UPHOLSTERY

In October of 1985, a new building was constructed on the east edge of Hosmer.
In this building Dale Aman began his Auto Trim and Upholstery business.  The
work consists of automotive interior repair, convertible tops and furniture
upholstery.

Dale received his training at North Dakota State School of Science at Wahpeton,
ND, graduating in 1985.

DOCKTER'S REPAIR

Dockter's Repair is a business which was started in 1973 on the Elmer Dockter
farm, located seven miles north of Hosmer.  Dale Dockter, owner, had been
employed at the former Chevy Garage in Hosmer prior to starting his own
business.

The business consists of general repair of cars, trucks, tractors and welding.
In 1985, a new structure was constructed which houses the business at the
present time.

EISENBEISZ TRUCKING

Gardner Eisenbeisz started his trucking business in 1950. He began with one
truck, and later two livestock trucks.  Nick Heier and Tony Malsom were his
hired helpers.

At the present time he is operating a 45 foot livestock trailer, a grain
trailer and a flat bed.  He does mostly local trucking since his heart surgery
in June, 1984.

Gardner has seen many changes come about in the last thirty-seven years in the
trucking business.

FARMERS STATE BANK OF HOSMER

The Farmers State Bank was chartered under the laws of the State of South
Dakota on October 18, 1919, being capitalized at $15,000.00. On August 2, 1920,
it opened its doors to the public.  From its beginning to the present time it
has been a locally owned, independent bank.

The original stockholders were John Haerter, Arthur J. Haerter, Helen E.
Haerter, John Heckenlaible, Christ Heckenlaible, M. Plin Beebe, Christian
Ulmer, and Edmund C. Ulmer.  Its first officers were John Haerter, President;
Helen Haerter, Vice President; and Arthur J. Haerter, Cashier.

The bank soon found public acceptance, and went on to serve the needs of the
growing community.  On December 13, 1926, John Haerter, the founder of the
institution died suddenly. Arthur J. Haerter was then elected as President,
Christ Heckenlaible as Vice President, and Albert J. Haerter as Cashier.
Arthur Haerter retired in January, 1973, at which time John A. Haerter was
elected President and serves in that capacity today.  Albert Haerter retired in
February, 1977.

During the severe depression of the early 1930's, the bank rewarded the trust
of its many depositors and customers, by remaining open and operating on a
sound basis with adequate resources.  On May 2, 193 1, the Hillsview State Bank
consolidated with the Farmers State Bank.  The name of the bank remained the
Farmers State Bank.

John A. Haerter began his banking career with Farmers State Bank in 1950, and
has served in all capacities beginning with bookkeeper-teller and today is
serving as president and CEO of the bank.

Dennis Hollan was employed by the bank in 1971, is presently Vice President and
Cashier and also serves as a director.

George Mohr joined the bank in 1968 and is now assistant cashier and computer
operations manager.

Bonnie Schumacher began working in  1979 as a bookkeeper-teller and since
serves also as  a computer operator.

Delene Schutz was employed in 1984 as a  bookkeeper-teller and computer
operator.

Some of the former employees of the bank are: Erwin Keller, Theodore Stoecker,
Edward Preszler, Melvin Sonnenfeid, Peggy Kranzler Wilson, Lorine Hauck
Neuharth, Arlene Mehlhoff Herrboldt, Plin Haerter, Sylvia Forkel Preszler,
Carol Ulmer, Robert Stoecker, and Royal Stabbe who joined the bank in 1945 and
after forty years of service to the bank retired in January, 1986.  A former
officer-employee of the bank was Erwin Haerter.  The bank is grateful for their
services.

The members of the Board of Directors are: John A. Haerter, Dennis Hollan,
Royal Stabbe, Doris Haerter, and Bruce Haerter.

Farmers State Bank is a full service bank, able to provide in-house
computerized services of every type.  The building was purchased from the
Haerter-Heckenlaibe Corporation in February, 1985, with plans to redecorate the
interior by the Centennial year of 1987.

Throughout its history, Farmers State Bank has strived to better serve its
trading community, and help to make it a better place to live.  The officers,
directors, and employees congratulate Hosmer on its 100th Birthday and with
confidence we look forward to the coming years.

FARMERS UNION CO-OP OIL COMPANY

The Farmers Union Oil Company of Hosmer was started through a local Farmers
Union Organization formed by farmers who held monthly meetings of which Rupert
Ulmer was president and Sam R. Job was secretary.  It was decided at one of
these meetings in the fall of 1945 that they should start an Oil Company in
Hosmer.  They called Joe Fortner, a fieldman for Farmers Union Central Exchange
of St. Paul, MN. Mr. Fortner explained that if they could raise $5,000 within
the next two weeks selling shares, then the Central Exchange would help them
organize an oil company.  They then elected officers to conduct the business
affairs and sell shares. The officers were: Edwin J. Walth, President; Emil
Jung, Vice President; Arnold Hieb, Secretary-Treasurer; and Adam Stoecker and
Albert Aman, Directors.  They sold $8,850 worth of shares at $25 per share.  At
the next meeting plans were made to start a Co-op Oil Company and by March,
1946, the company was in operation with Eddie Sonnenfeld as manager.  They
received additional financial assistance from the Farmers Union Central
Exchange making it possible to purchase the old Pontiac garage one block north
of main street for $1,800 and set up three bulk storage tanks.

The Oil Company made very good progress and by 1951 was debt free.  Two more
bulk storage tanks were added in 1947.  In 1953, the Gottlieb Schaible Buick
and Pontiac garage building on main street was purchased.  The company moved up
to main street in Oct. 1953, after remodeling the building. Since then one
additional storage tank has been added and the Cities Service Bulk Plant was
bought out making 125,000 gallon storage capacity.  A pickup truck and another
gas delivery truck were added in 1956 and 1959, respectively.

In 1966, the Company changed their policy to paying straight salary instead of
commission for all employees.  Ron Hieb became manager with Carol Hieb as
bookkeeper.  In June, 1968, the fiscal year ending was changed to Oct. 31st
instead of March 31st.  Gary Weishaar was hired in Anton Gisi's place.
Throughout the years many changes were made.  A bulk tank was replaced.  The
I.H.C. truck was purchased to be used as the gas truck.  A new 1971 Chevy truck
and fertilizer spreader was bought.  Adam Heilman became an employee.  The
Co-op bought their first spray coupe and also a 40' x 80' Quonset.  About 1972,
the Farmers Union Central Exchange changed their name to CENEX with the motto
"Where the Customer is the Company." A new 1974 Dodge 1/2 ton pickup was
purchased.  In 1975, a Chevy truck was purchased for a gas truck.  The spray
coupe was sold to Dale Roesch.  In 1976, Marvin Steensen became manager and
Leona Pfeifle became bookkeeper.  A new spray coupe was purchased.  Adam
Heilman became manager in 1978.  The bulk plant was diked.  Later, gauges were
put in at the bulk plant.  Carol Hieb became bookkeeper.  In 1983, a 1979 Ford
F350 ton pickup was purchased.  New underground tanks were replaced at the
station for regular and unleaded gas. Don Neifer started to work at the Co-op.
In 1984, a GMC truck and tank was purchased which is the present gas truck. The
old truck and tank were sold to Don's Sinclair in Pierre. SD.

In the last twenty some years the following have worked at the Farmers Union
Oil Company in Hosmer: Reinhold Lammle, Dennis Ellwein, Ray Preszler, Anton
Gisi, Gerhardt Knittel, Donley Lang, Alex Heier, Reuben Neifer, Ken Hieb, David
Eisenbeisz, Roger Maisch, Steve Malsam, Linda Pietz, Kay Spear, Tom Salwei,
Floyd Mohr, Francie Ellsworth, Sam Job, Arnold Aman, Rhonda Hieb, Keith
Schumacher, Karen Steensen, Lew Paulson, and Don Neifer.

The Company started out with 115 shareholders and has grown to date to an
estimated 525.  The Company has been paying out cash dividends regularly since
1954.  It has a new worth of $573,503.

As of 1986, the employees are as follows: Adam Heilman, Manager; Gary Weishaar,
bulk driver; Marvin Malsam, Shop; Carol Hieb, bookkeeper; and Waldo Goehring,
helper.  Present Directors are: Herbert Schumacher, President; Ken Hieb,
Secretary-Treasurer; Henry Malsam, Vice-President; Eloy Heier and Herbert
Schaible.

GOOD FRIENDS TAVERN

John and Cora Heckenlaible were the original owners of the lots on which
today's "Good Friends Tavern" was erected.  This building has a history of
changing owners and managers and except for a short time has always been a type
of pool hall and eating establishment.  Bernard Ochs and Emil Sonnenfeld
purchased the lots from the Heckenlaibles on December 19, 1916, and built the
original building.  On January 11, 1918, Emil Sonnenfeld sold his share in the
building to Bernard Ochs who operated the business until 1919.

Throughout the years the owners were: 1919-Joe Benfit; 1920-Adam Heintz and
Joseph Geze; 1928-Andrew Gisi; 1941-Gust and Emily Maisch who converted the
business into a cream station and turkey plucking business.  He was associated
with North American Creameries; 1947-Oscar Krebs who again converted it into a
pool hall; 1949-Erwin Hieb and Ervin Spitzer; 1952-Harry and Alice Guthmiller;
1963-Jacob Goehring who leased the building to Leonard Vogele and Lawrence
Mohr; 1964-Lawrence Bonen who moved a country school house behind the business
in which he housed fishing supplies and later it was used for steak frys. Also,
he constructed a concrete block addition to the original building in which he
moved the fishing supplies and later sold and repaired guns; 1970-Janice
Malsam; 1975-Terry and Judy Cox who for a few months leased the business to Bob
and Susan Gisi; 1977-Charles and Sandra Wolf.  They leased for sometime to
Laurie Benton, Gene Allen Sandmeier, and Jeanne Stitte; 1980-Steve and Janet
Sheppard; and 1983-Dennis and Kathleen Kimmes.

On August 1, 1985, John and Luane Thurn purchased the tavern and are presently
operating the business with fine foods, beer on tap, a little pool, video
machines for the younger set, and cards for the card players.

THE HAERTER-HECKENLAIBLE CORP.

The Haerter-Heckenlaible Corporation which deals in farm land and investments
was incorporated on November 22, 1927, with three Directors: John Heckenlaible,
President; Arthur, J. Haerter, Secretary; and Albert J. Haerter, Treasurer.
Their office was located in the rear of the Farmers State Bank.

In 1935, John Heckenlaible left this corporation and formed his own
corporation.  On February 27, 1936, Erwin S. Haerter was appointed President;
Albert J. Haerter, Vice President; and Arthur J. Haerter, Secretary-Treasurer.

Plin Haerter became an employee of the corporation in January, 1962, and on
February 2, 1963, Arthur J. Haerter was appointed President, Plin Haerter as
Secretary-Treasurer.  Plin resigned his position on December 1, 1964, to
relocate in California in a more moderate climate due to his health.  John
Haerter was then appointed Secretary-Treasurer to replace Plin.

Floyd Stabbe was hired in May, 1964, as bookkeeper, in 1970 was appointed
Secretary-Treasurer, and was appointed as a Director in August, 1978.

On February 2, 1980, Arthur J. Haerter resigned his position due to health, and
John Haerter was appointed President; Charles Haerter, Vice President; and Floyd
Stabbe, Secretary-Treasurer and manager of the corporation.

In February, 1982, the corporation changed from a three director board to a
five director board with the members being John Haerter, Plin Haerter, Charles
Haerter, Alice Bakken and Floyd Stabbe.

In August, 1984, it was decided that the Farmers State Bank needed additional
space, so the corporation started to plan for a new building.  Hosmer Lumber
Co. started construction October 8, 1984, on a new 25' x 30' cement block
building with brick facing on the front.  This building is located just north
of the bank.  The new building was occupied on February 20, 1985, although the
brick facing on the outside was not completed until May, 1985.

The present officers of the corporation are: John Haerter, President; Alice
Bakken, Vice President; Floyd Stabbe, Secretary-Treasurer; Plin Haerter and
Charles Haerter, Assistant Vice Presidents.

JOHN HECKENLAIBLE, INC.

After having acquired land in the Dakotas and in Washington, The John
Heckenlaible Corporation was formed in 1935.  Members, in succeeding years,
have been John Heckenlaible, Cora Heckenlaible, Ramona Schaible and Arline
Heckenlaible.

This land has been leased and farmed by various renters.

The land in the state of Washington, having been farmed by three generations,
was purchased by third generations of this family before the demise of John
Heckenliable --members of whom have maintained contact with the present
shareholders.

Present ownership of land is in Edmunds and McPherson counties of South Dakota.

The current office building of John Heckenlaible, Inc. was constructed in 1976,
on the site of one of the first buildings owned by John Heckenlaible, a
building that had served as a store, post-office, barber shop, apartment,
restaurant, recreation hall and warehouse.

Since the demise of John in 1976, and Cora in 1981, the shareholders in the
Corporation are Ramona Schaible and Arline Heckenlaible.

HEIER'S MEAT MARKET

Joe P. and Josephine Heier purchased the Hosmer Meat Market from John and
Milbert Gutjahr in March of 1966. They moved into the city of Hosmer and took
over the business on April 15, 1966.  As the business progressed, renovations
were made and new equipment was purchased.  In 1970, there was a fire in the
building, after which it was completely remodeled - a cement floor was put in,
a new work area was created, and new wiring and plumbing was installed.

On September 13, 1974, Joe won a First Place Trophy (Championship) at the South
Dakota Association of Meat Processors in DeSmet for his Summer Sausage.  Joe and
Josephine also traveled to Las Vegas, Nevada, for a National Convention in
August of 1975.  Subsequent awards won by Heier's Meat Market for their various
meat products from the South Dakota Association of Meat Processors are: in 1980,
the Championship for Smoked Pork Sausage; 1981, Championship for Jerky; 1981,
Reserve Champion for Summer Sausage; 1983, Reserve Champion for Ham; 1983,
Championship for Smoked Pork Sausage; 1984, Grand Championship for Bologna;
1984, Championship for Unsmoked Pork Sausage; 1984, Championship for Bacon.

Heier's Meat Market is truly a family enterprise.  The entire family worked
very hard to make it as widely known as it is today.  Eight of the nine
children raised by Joe and Josephine are either currently employed or were
employed in the past by this business.  As many as nine people were employed
there at one time.  All employees were very good and are much appreciated by
the Heier's.  This Meat Market has done business with people from nearly every
state in the United States.

Joe P. Heier passed away on October 15, 1976, while driving the Meat Market van
on a regular delivery route. David Heier, the family's eldest son, took over as
manager of the meat market until his youngest brother, Gerard, returned to
Hosmer in 198 1. They are currently co-managing the family business with their
mother, Josephine, as sole owner.

Heier's Meat Market would like to take this opportunity to thank their many
customers in the Hosmer and surrounding areas and from all over the United
States for the loyal patronage they have enjoyed these past 21 years.  We thank
you sincerely.

HOSMER CITY HALL

The city meetings had been held in the Ulmer-Herrboldt Hall, and at the April
1, 1930, meeting a resolution was passed to have an election for selling bonds
to pay for a new city hall and fire hall.  An election was held April 15, 1930,
and the electors voted in favor of it.

At the May 6, 1930, meeting, the bid was awarded to Henry Huether of Ashley,
North Dakota, for $6,380.00 for a brick building with a reservoir under the
fire hall portion.  It was completed in 1930.  The architect was F. C. Kuehn
from Huron, South Dakota.

Bonds were sold for $3,500.00 to finance part of the new hall.  The hall was
used for city meetings, fire department meetings and many wedding receptions
and church suppers.

In 1963, the city council decided to repair the building because the mortar was
starting to crumble between the bricks, and so it was decided to stucco the
entire exterior, which was done by Jake Mohr & Sons Construction Company and
completed in 1964.

In 1966, a complete new roof was put on the hall because it was in bad shape.
This was done by Christ Bauer at a cost of $1,470.00.

An oil furnace was installed in 1973 for heating the hall, which previously was
heated with space heaters.

In 1986, the roof was repaired, new doors and windows installed, and a city
office built inside for keeping and storing the city records and books.

HOSMER CITY LIQUOR STORE

At the city council meeting of April 1, 1947, a petition signed by 45 electors
was presented by the City Auditor which had been filed with him, requesting a
special election to be called for the purpose of submitting to the electors the
question, "shall a license or licenses for the sale of intoxicating liquor be
issued to the City".  Therefore, a resolution was passed to have a special
election on April 22, 1947, and at this election 162 voted in favor and 134
against.

At the May 6, 1947, meeting the city council leased the building on lot 1,
block 5, O.P. from the Haerter-Heckenlaible Corporation for $40.00 per month,
which was the same building that the former liquor operators were in: The
former operators were Samuel Pietz, Gottfried and Rudolph Melhoff.  The
building was known as the Knittel Store building.  The City also paid the
former operators $1,550.00 for the equipment.

The City took over the Liquor Store on July 1, 1947, with Reinhold Lammle and
Arthur Sonnenfeld hired as managers to operate the business at $150.00 per
month each.

In 1948, Pete S. Imbery and August Mohr were hired to replace Lammle and
Sonnenfeld to manage the business. August Mohr died in 1964, and Frank M.
Imberi was hired to replace him.

In 1967, the city bought the building from the Haerter-Heckenlaible
Corporation for $1,900.00 with plans to either remodel or build a new building.

Pete S. Imbery retired in December, 1969, after working over 21 years, and
Frank M. Imberi resigned.  Eugene Aman of McLaughlin, South Dakota, was hired
for a few months as manager and Lawrence Bonen as assistant manager. Lawrence
Bonen became the manager April, 1970, and Julius Martell his assistant.

In 1970, the city council started to check into repairing and remodeling the
building, however, the cost would have been greater then the price of a new
building.  On July 12, 1971, it was decided to dismantle the building, and the
city leased the Legion Hall to move into temporarily until the new building
would be completed.  At the August 13, 1971, meeting it was decided to
advertise for bids for a new building, and the bids were opened on September 7,
1971, with Jake Mohr & Sons Construction Co. being the low bidder of $18,885.24
for a brick veneer building, and Adam Electric of Roscoe, South Dakota, for the
electrical, heating, air conditioning and plumbing for $7,088.79. The building
was completed by December, 197 1, and the City Liquor Store was opened for
business just before Christmas of 1971.

In August, 1974, Dean Schumacher was hired to replace Julius Martell, who
resigned, and in August, 1976, Lawrence Bonen resigned and Dean Schumacher
became manager. Dean resigned in December, 1979, to become a salesman for a
liquor company, and Jacob M. Aman was hired as manager. Jacob M. Aman resigned
in August, 1980, and Russell Rollo was hired until July, 1983, when Luane Lemke
was hired. Luane resigned in May, 1984, and Robert W. Johnson was then hired.
He resigned in July, 1985.  Mary Lou Mohr managed temporarily until October,
1985, when the city council hired Joyce Hoffer of Aberdeen to manage the store.

HOSMER DEVELOPMENT HOUSING, INC.

On September 10, 1973, a meeting to form a non-profit corporation to acquire,
construct, provide and operate a rental housing for low income residents of
Hosmer and the surrounding area was held.  Hosmer Development Housing, Inc. was
incorporated on October 29, 1973, with the following directors: Dennis Hollan,
John Haerter, Frank Gisi, Arnold Herrboldt, Arnold Pietz, and Gardner
Eisenbeisz.  Shares were sold for $15.00 per share.

On November 25, 1973, a contract was signed with the Heupel Construction Co. of
Eureka, SD, in the amount of $49,000.00 for construction of unit #1, Central
Apartments, a four-plex.  This contract covered all sidewalks, landscaping,
basement, refrigerator, stove, hoods, plus electric heat.

On September 7, 1977, Lots 1-2-3- in Block 1, Herrboldts replat of Lot 1-A was
purchased to construct unit #2, Somerset.  This unit is also a four-plex, one
bedroom apartment. In the spring of 1979, the Lamount Associates, Aberdeen,
South Dakota, were hired as architects to design this complex and a bid letting
was held.  Construction was started soon thereafter with the Mohr Construction
of Hosmer building this unit.

The following are serving as officers: President, Dennis Hollan;
Vice-President, Arnold Pietz; Secretary, John Haerter; Treasurer, Arnold
Herrboldt.  Amy Heckenlaible is the bookkeeper for the corporation while Dennis
and Lenhart Neifer are the maintenance personnel.

THE HOSMER ELEVATOR COMPANY

The Hosmer Elevator Company was organized in early 1971, when area farmers and
local business men got together and formed a stock company in order to reopen
the elevator for business which had been closed and vacated some time before.
However, the history of the building and the business goes back to the early
1900's when a rain and implement business known as John Haerter and Company was
formed. In the year 1910, Jacob G. Walth joined the business and the firm
continued until 1919 at which time John Haerter sold his share to Jacob Lammle
and left the grain and implement business to start a new automobile business.
Walth and Lammle as it was known continued for ten years at which time the
partnership was dissolved with Lammle keeping the John Deere implement portion
and Walth keeping the grain business.  During the year 1926, the old grain
elevator was torn down and the present building was erected in its place. Jacob
Walth remained in the grain business until 1945 when he sold the elevator to
Carl Stoecker and Jacob Goehrin.

The new firm was known as the Hosmer Grain Company A few years later Goehring
purchased the John Kranzler elevator north of main street leaving Carl Stoecker
as the sole owner.  In 1958, Carl Stoecker sold the elevator to Leo Goehring
who renamed the business the Home Grain Company. He remained in business for
about ten years and then sold it to a commission firm known as ADM.  Helmer
Leno, the former owner of the Bowdle Feed Mill then leased it for a few years
before it became vacant.

During this dormancy a committee was appointed to assess the situation and
found that with some major repairs the building could be put into a usable
state.  On January 10, 1972, the Hosmer Elevator Company was incorporated under
laws of the state of South Dakota.  The first elected board of Directors
consisted of Rupert Ulmer, Alex Heier, Henry Eisenbeisz, Alex Pietz, Alvin
Preszler, John Haerter, and Ed C. Ulmer.  The first manager was Wilbur Snyder.
Snyder stayed on as manager for approximately six months and then resigned to
assume a similar position in his home state of North Dakota.  He was succeeded
by Harry Kirschenmann. Harry managed the business until May, 1973, at which time
poor health forced him to resign.  Floyd Mohr became the new manager for the
next three years.  In 1976, Helmuth Mohr was hired to become manager.  He
managed the business until May, 1979, at which time he resigned to accept the
job as school custodian.  John Eisenbeisz was then hired to fill this position
and is currently still manager assisted by his brother, Peter.

The firm handles Spring, Winter, and Durum wheat, oats, barley, corn and Zip
commercial feeds.  Along with grain and commercial feeds the business also
handles a full line of animal health products.  Since the railroad no longer
provides transportation all grains are transported by truck to the market
terminals.  Along with the original board of directors, Joe P. Heier, Milbert
Eisenbeisz, Walter Gramm, Arnold Wiederrich, David Heier, Harry Fischer and
Arthur Mohr have served as directors.  The current board consists of Vernon
Walth, Floyd Mohr, Jerome Malsom, Jerome Kirschenmann, Glenn Jung, and Robert
Schumacher.

HOSMER RURAL FIRE DEPARTMENT

A rural fire department was established in 1959.  Rural members paid yearly
dues for fire equipment and operating expenses.  A 1937 IHC pumper was
purchased for $900.00. Later, a 1952 Reo 6 wheel drive truck was obtained
through the Forestry Department.  Fire fighting was provided by the Hosmer
Volunteer Fire Department.  The organization was disbanded in 1981 after the
Hosmer Rural Fire Protection District was organized.

HOSMER RURAL FIRE PROTECTION DISTRICT

The Hosmer Rural Fire Protection District was organized on June 23, 1981, and
incorporated July 15, 1981.  The organization is a political subdivision and
receives its operating funds through taxation.  The District provides fire
protection for an area of 216 1/2 square miles and the City of Hosmer.

The first elected board members were President, Herbert Schaible; Vice
President, Milton Herrboldt; Secretary-Treasurer, Delbert Gienger; members,
William Forkel, Jr. and Charles Wolf.  The present board members are President,
Herbert Schaible; Vice President, James Mohr; Secretary-Treasurer, Milton
Herrboldt; members are Alex Heier and Maurice Aman.  A new fire station
building was erected in 1983.  It consists of four stalls for trucks and a
meeting room. At present money is being levied for a newer and larger pumper.

HOSMER VOLUNTEER FIRE DEPARTMENT

The Hosmer Volunteer Fire Department was organized November 28, 1911. No
regular meetings were held until 1918.  During 1920 the following officers were
elected: Chief, Frank Boldt; Assistant Chief, Jacob Schaible; Clerk, Edwin
Herrboldt; Treasurer, Eugene Huber.  In 1920, it was moved that the department
would consist of a maximum size of 25 members.  Also members would be fined 10
cents for missing a meeting.

Original equipment consisted of two hose carts and a single cylinder gas engine
pumper.  The water supply was stored in a wooden cistern located under the fire
station.  A wooden tower behind the station was used to dry hoses.  In 1929,
two hand drawn chemical tanks were purchased.  In 1940, a used 4 cylinder gas
engine-300 GPM pumper was purchased from Luverne Fire Apparatus Company.  The
pumper was retired in 1953.  A new 1947 Ford 200 GPM pumper was purchased in
1947 and is still in service at the present time. Additional equipment in
service at present is a 1952 Reo 6 wheel drive tanker with 200 GPM pump and a
1965 Jeep 1 1/4 ton grass fire fighting unit.  Each fire fighter has a complete
set of turnout clothing.

The early fire alarm was a bell located on top of the fire station.  A 5 HP
electric motor driven siren was purchased in 1932 and erected on a tower
located beside the telephone building.  This siren was later replaced by a
larger one and located by the fire station.

The first fire station was located in the city hall.  Later the former Gust
Pietz Service Station was purchased and used until 1984 when a new four bay
building with a meeting room was built.

Present officers are: Chief, Milton Herrboldt; Assistant Chief, Stanley Mohr;
Captain, Dennis Neifer; Captain, Dennis Hollan; Lt., Peter Eisentleisz; Lt.
Dennis Gab; Training Officer, Bernard Grismer; Mechanic, Roger Gramm; other
members are Jere Hieb, Charles Mohr, David Heier, Christ Heilman, Frank Gisi,
Pat Gisi, Helmuth Mohr, Donald Neifer, Milton Mohr, Elmer Fauth, Dean
Schumacher, Floyd Mohr, Lew Paulson, John Jacobs, John Eisenbeisz, Dennis
Kimmes, Werner Mohr, and Gerard Heier.

Some of the major city fires were: Ford Garage, September 4, 1924; Hosmer Flour
Mill, February 8, 1929; Gottlieb Aman Elevator, July 19, 1936; Meat Market,
December 31, 1964; Farmers Elevator Company, February 5, 1967; Walth Motor
Company, May 2, 1967; Hosmer Lumber Company Warehouse, February 12, 198 1.
There were two fire related deaths.

EARLY FIRE ENGINE

Pictured is the second fire engine that was used in Hosmer.  This pumper was
purchased used from Luverne Fire Company, Luverne, Minnesota, between January
and April 1940.  It was re laced by a new pumper in 1948.

The pumper was built by the Waterous Company starting in 1908.  The only
changes that were made from the original design was that rubber tires were
installed.

The pumper has a 4 cylinder duel ignition engine and was capable of pumping 300
gallons per minute.  The pumper was kept in the city hall building, and when
there was a fire call the pumper was moved over a 4,000 gallon underground
reservoir.  Hose carts pulled by fire fighters laid hose from the station to
the fire scene.

This pumper is still in operating condition today and is owned by Ken Peterson,
Burnsville, Minnesota.

HOSMER GAMBLE STORE

The Hosmer Gamble Dealer Store was opened for business in August of 1936 by
Gerhardt Bauman and his wife, Hildegarde.  The Bauman's moved to Hosmer from
the Java, South Dakota, area.  Mr. Bauman's love for the retail business dates
back to the years of 1927-1929, when he was in the garage business in Bowdle,
South Dakota, and also was in the service station business from 1931-1933.

The original sales floor of the new Hosmer Gamble Store was 25 feet by 30 feet
as economic conditions were low during the '30's.  The Gamble Store made
possible many wonderful money-saving deals, as can be seen by the posters in
the pictures.

As the business grew, the Bauman's soon found themselves in need of more space
for their business.  This led them to purchase a different building in 1942,
which is their present location.  The new sales floor area was 25 feet by 66
feet, plus they had warehouse space.  They added the Red and White Grocery line
along with the Gamble line of merchandise. Mrs. Bauman's brother, Roland
Jakober, was manager of the Food Department until 1944 when Mr. Jakober moved
the grocery line into a separate building and the Bauman's kept their present
location for their store.

On September 1st and 2nd of 1961, the Bauman's were honored by a special
company sales merchandise show and a banquet given by the Gamble Skogmo Company
for being associated with the company for 25 years.

As the years passed by and our country advanced into a change of economic and
inflation era, it became more difficult for the wholesale sources and the
retailer to serve the trade with high quality merchandise at low retail prices.
 This caused many companies to merge or sell out to other companies. Gambles
sold out to Wick Corporation of California in 1979.  The Bauman's continued to
do business with Wick Corporation until 1982 when a change of warehouse location
did not serve the needs of their customers.  The Bauman's then changed to O.K.
Hardware and in 1984 the company added Trustworthy System to their O.K.
merchandise, which they then joined to acquire national advertising on all
national brands at better prices for their customers.

In 1979, the Bauman's put a new front on their building and changed the side
entrance to a center entrance which makes it easier to enter.

The Bauman's would like to add that they greatly appreciate having had the
opportunity to operate a retail store on Hosmer's Main Street, where people
were able to obtain their needs.  They celebrated their 50th year in business in
1986.  They also wish to thank all the people of Hosmer and the entire trade
area for their patronage these past 50 years.  It was Gerhardt's and
Hildegarde's desire to serve the people better by bringing them the best in
merchandise at the lowest possible prices.

HOSMER LUMBER COMPANY HISTORY

The origin of Hosmer Lumber Company dates back to June, 1940, however, its
history goes back to the year 1899 when a retail lumber yard was started on a
railroad siding.  All lumber was piled out in the open and the stock was mainly
rough boards in two by four dimension used for tar paper shacks and roofs to
cover sod houses.

In the year 1918, John Heckenlaible and William Meier, who had for some years
managed a lumber yard in town for the Interior Lumber Company, joined in a
partnership and built the present building on main street.  This was one of the
largest lumber yard buildings in the territory at the time and one of the few
completely under one roof.  William Meier was manager.

In early September of 1937, Mr. Meier passed away. Though by state law
concerning a partnership, the doors were to be closed, a court order was
acquired to stay in business.  In September of that year, Elmer Hieb, step-son
of William Meier, gave up his career as a classroom teacher and was elected by
the court and heirs to manage the firm.  Business continued under the name of
Heckenlaible and Meier with John Heckenlaible and Emma Meier, widow of William
Meier, owners.

In June of 1940, negotiations with another lumber yard across the street owned
by Martin Preszler resulted in a new corporation with John Heckenlaible, Emma
Meier, Elmer Hieb, and Ed Preszler as stockholders.

During the years following the death of Emma Meier in 1953, the stock owned by
heirs Elliard Hieb, Raymond Hieb, and Helmuth Hieb was purchased by Elmer Hieb
and son Lynn.  When Ed Preszler left Hosmer in retirement, his stock in the
firm was also purchased by Elmer Hieb.  With due respect, it must be said that
John Heckenlaible was a diligent and reliable advisor from the year 1899 until
his death in 1976 at which time Elmer purchased his holdings in the firm.

Today's owners are Elmer Hieb, wife Ellen, Lynn and Jere their sons.  Elmer is
semi-retired but visits the office daily to aid with calls and walk-in
customers.  Health problems necessitate his active role be modified.  He
remains principal stockholder.  It is with an element of pride that Elmer will
have been associated with Hosmer Lumber Company for half a century when Hosmer
celebrates its centennial in June of 1987.  The appearance of the original
structure will be somewhat altered as work on plans for repair and efficiency
have been started.  Jere is now manager and our area of trade has expanded.  We
hope to merit the loyalty and trust shown by this patronage.  The community has
been good to us and we have tried to be an asset and a service to our community
and customers.  With confidence we look ahead to the coming years.  Only God
holds the key to our endeavors.

HOSMER POST OFFICE

The Hosmer Post Office was established March 8, 1887. William P. Nagle was the
first appointed postmaster.  The post office served a population of
approximately 100 people in 1887.

James H. McKoane served from January 22, 1889, to June, 1890, and Meta A.
McKoane served from June 27, 1890, to 1894.  The post office was located in a
small building which was also Hosmer's first store.  It was located next to the
Farmer's State Bank.

Other postmasters    serving the area were:

   Van McCafferty            April 18, 1894-1895
   George M. Hasen           April 19, 1895-1896
   Christopher H. Sness      May 9, 1896-1897
   Annie T. Hasen            August 21, 1897-1901
   Gottfried Schulz          December 10, 1901-1902
   Frank Boldt               June 28, 1902-1912; post office was
                             located in Boldt Store during this
                             time
   James F. Geeslin          April 10, 1912-1916
   Elma A. Geeslin           February 29, 1916-1917
   John M. Schoch            February 24, 1917-1922
   Gottlieb J. Walth         March 4, 1922-1934; rural delivery
                             was started in 1925.  John Lehr was
                             the first rural carrier.
   A. Daniel Pietz           July 27, 1934-1935
   Walter Ulmer              August 21, 1935-1967

The post office was located in the building next to Boldt's Store.   In
October, 1961, Walter Ulmer moved into Hosmer's new Post Office building
serving almost 1,000 people in the Hosmer area.  The building was built by
Albert Haerter and leased to the United States Postal Department. The modern
building is completely fireproof, has an automatic oil heating system, and air
conditioning.  The outer building has a complete brick face with a flat roof.
The post office is equipped with all metal furnishings supplied by the Post
Office Department.  This includes modern boxes, a safe, a desk, cabinets for
forms and parcels, and an electric scale which gives the weight of the parcel
and the parcel post rate. At this time there were 179 boxes with 145 boxholders.
William Knittle, Christ Huber, Chip Walth and Herbert C. Aman served as clerks
during Mr. Ulmer's time as Post Master.  John Lehr and Mike Malsam served as
rural carriers.

Bernard Grismer has been serving as Postmaster since February 9, 1968.  Floyd
Stabbe serves as clerk and Dennis Neifer as the rural carrier.

The post office has undergone many changes in the first 100 years of serving
the Hosmer community.  At the present time there are 112 boxholders and 116
rural route boxes. First-class mail rates and cards changes are as follows:
