HOSMER SENIOR CITIZENS HOME

Submitted by Floyd Stabbe

A committee of Gottlieb J. Walth, Albert J. Haerter and Carl Stoecker from the
Hosmer Civic Club met with the city council in late 1964 discussing the
possibility of building a Nursing Home at Hosmer.  At a special meeting on
February 2, 1965, the city council decided that the city will hold a special
election to decide on the issue of Bonds for a Nursing Home.  City Attorney at
that time, Raymond Hieb, drew up the necessary papers for a special election
held on March 1, 1965, for the issuance of revenue bonds in the amount of
$190,000.00 with final payment due in 1990 and to be repaid solely from income
derived from the Home.  The majority people voted in favor of this project.
Reinhold P. Walth was Mayor at this time.

Harold and Winnie Ehresman donated the land for the Nursing Home site.

After bid lettings, construction contracts were awarded and actual construction
for the brick veneer forty-bed home began August 1, 1965, for an intermediate
care facility.  The general contract was awarded to Jake Mohr and Sons
Construction Company of Hosmer; Plumbing and Heating to Risager Plumbing and
Heating of Aberdeen, and electrical wiring to Holzworth Brothers of Mobridge.
The outside was enclosed before winter, so work continued all through the
winter finishing the interior, and in the spring the outside bricks were layed
and finished.  Sam Pietz was the Mayor when construction started at the Home.

The modern new home was fully equipped with a stainless steel kitchen, dining
room and Chapel-recreation room, meeting all state regulations.  The eight
private and sixteen semi-private rooms were furnished in a modern outline with
golden teak finish furniture matching the dining and recreation room.  The
building construction cost $180,000.00, and the room furniture and equipment
were all furnished by individuals or groups as charitable gifts and memorials.
Each donor had a name plate placed on the door of the room he or she furnished.
Approximately $12,930.00 was raised in donations, and in addition to this a
car raffle was held to raise money with 50 tickets being sold at $100.00 per
ticket.

Mr. and Mrs. Vernon Prafke from Glencoe, Minnesota, arrived in May, 1966, and
made final preparations for the admittance of residents.  Mrs. Ruby Prafke,
Practical Nurse and Administrator, started admitting residents for the Home June
1, 1966.  The first residents were: Mrs. George Martell, Katherine Dockter,
Mrs. Martin Job, Daniel Martz, Jacob Schmidt, Fred F. Hieb, and Arvid Wanttaga.
It took until January 15, 1967, to get the Home filled.

An open house was held on Sunday, August 7, 1966. Cookies, Kool-aid and coffee
were served to about 700 people attending the open house.

Freda Judd, LPN, became Administrator January, 1967, to replace Ruby Prafke who
had resigned.  The first 5 years were a struggle to operate the Home because of
expenses and vacancies.  In February, 1969, Bernice Weber, LPN, with past
experience in operating a Nursing Home, was hired as Administrator, and the
Home began to make progress.

In June, 1970, Robert Haugen became Administrator and Janice McChesney, R.N.,
was hired as Director of Nursing.  In 1972, Robert Lehmann became Administrator
and in February, 1975, Roger Feickert was Administrator for a short time.  In
February, 1976, Richard Arioso became Administrator. In June, 1977, Diane
Chapman became Administrator with Laura Natvig, R.N., becoming the new Director
of Nursing in November, 1977, replacing Janice McChesney who resigned.  In
June, 1979, Diane Chapman accepted the Administrator position at the Ipswich
Nursing Home and Carol Ulmer was hired July, 1979, as Administrator, and Connie
Woehlhaff, R.N., of Eureka, as the Director of Nursing in November, 1979.

A fund raising drive was started by Carol Ulmer, Administrator, in January,
1984, for the purchase of a Van, with funds matched by AAL, and a 1973 Dodge
Van was purchased for $5,000.00 equipped with a wheel chair lift in it, which
the residents really enjoy for outings.

Carol Ulmer resigned in December, 1984, and Lyle Spicer was hired in June,
1985, as Administrator, and Geralyn Malsom, R.N., was hired October, 1985, as
Director of Nursing to replace Connie Woehlhaff who resigned.  On May 12, 1986,
John Jacobs took over administrative duties at the Hosmer Senior Citizen's Home.

The Home has had a 98% occupancy rate most of the time, and employs 32 people.

The Hosmer community feels proud of its beautiful Home and the excellent care
it provides.

JACOBS' FAIRWAY FOODS

Jacobs' Fairway was purchased by Mary Margaret and John Jacobs on April 2,
1984, from Doug and Ileen Lindner. It was formerly Doug's Fairway.  The store
is an 80' x 40' concrete block building and is located a block north of Main
Street between the Bank and the 102 Civic Center.  The building was built in
1976.

Jacobs' Fairway is managed by Mary Margaret with the assistance of three
part-time employees.

JUDY'S BEAUTY SHOP

Judy (Malsam) Weishaar is a graduate of Hosmer High School.  After graduation
she attended Stewarts School of Hairstyling in Aberdeen, South Dakota.

In July, 1972, she graduated and went to work as an apprentice in the STYLET in
Eureka, South Dakota, for one year.  On completion of her apprenticeship in
1973, she opened JUDY'S BEAUTY SHOP in her home in Hosmer.  She has been a
licensed operator for 12 years.

MOHR AUCTION SERVICE

Lawrence Mohr started as an Auctioneer in 1954.  His first sale was his
grandmother Fredricka Mohr's household auction.  After that he helped at
several auctions.  Then in 1961 he graduated from the Fort Smith School of
Auctioneering, at Fort Smith, Arkansas.

Since then he has been buying, selling and specializing in all types of
auctions including antique auctions and farm liquidation auctions.  In 1970,
the auction service became a full-time family business.

L & M WRECKING

Larry's Wrecking began slowly in 1967 with the buying and selling of scrap iron
and metals.  Upon purchasing several lots on the north side of town, the
business grew.  More trucks were added for hauling the iron as the scrap iron
business was often in demand.

Larry also became the fur dealer for the Hosmer area, which included the
selling of bait, traps and shells.  His three sons, Lance, David, and Doug
worked closely with him in the business.

In 1979, Larry sold the business to his son Lance.  It then became L & M
Wrecking and continues to deal in iron, metals and furs.  Raising silver fox
has also become part of Lance's interests.

LUSTERMAR CULTURED MARBLE MANUFACTURING COMPANY

A business under the name of Cultured Marble Manufacturing of Hosmer was
started by Arnold Pietz in 1969. In 1972, this company entered into an
association with Lusterock and Marble Manufacturing of Fargo, North Dakota, and
both the plant at Fargo and the plant at Hosmer changed their name to Lustermar
Cultured Marble Manufacturing. This association was in effect until 1979 when
the manufacturing plant at Fargo dissolved and Lustermar at Hosmer took over
both areas with the manufacturing plant remaining at Hosmer.  The Lustermar
Cultured Marble Manufacturing business was sold to Chuck Pietz in January, 1982.

The Lustermar Company specializes in the manufacturing of cultured marble
products as bathroom vanity tops, counter tops, bar tops, window sills, shower
walls and tub enclosures, fireplace mantels and hearths, etc.

MARTHA'S STORE

Martha's Grocery Store is owned by Martha Job.  In 1970, she purchased the
grocery store business from Ben Gramm which at that time was in the Frank Boldt
and Son Building located adjacent to the Hosmer Lumber Company. On May 30,
1976, Martha purchased her present building from Arnold Pietz and moved her
inventory to this place. (This building was the former bowling alley, a pool
hall, and then an addition to the Super Valu.)

Olivia Kunde was one of Martha's first employees.  At present her daughter
Carol Paulson is employed full-time with Mary Lou Mohr and Joyce Herrboldt
being part-time helpers.

102 CIVIC CENTER

The original dance hall was built in 1939-40 and owned by Samuel Pietz, John
Lehr, Randolph and Gottfried Melhoff, and Constance Gaub.  In 1948, Samuel
Pietz became the sole owner.  It was first used as a movie theater and later
for roller skating and public and private dances.

In 1969, a group of local men formed a corporation and purchased the hall to
preserve it as an entertainment source for the community.  Members of the
corporation were: Ray Mohr, Werner Mohr, Milton Mohr, Freddie Mohr, Ferdinand
Hieb, Merle Hieb, Frank Gisi, Raphael Heier, and Arnold Pietz.

They decided to rename the hall "The 102 Ballroom" because at that time the
state highway leading from U.S. 12 to Hosmer was numbered 102.

They made several improvements on the building including installing running
water, a bar area, bathrooms, booths along the outside walls, and new siding on
three outside walls.

During this time and to the present, the hall was used for public dances,
wedding and anniversary dances and private parties.

In 1979, the Ballroom was sold to another partnership consisting of Wylie Mohr,
Dean Schumacher and Lew Paulson.  They operated the hall until 1983 when the
Hosmer American Legion purchased the hall and turned it over to the Hosmer
Civic Club.

It was decided at this time to expand the building to the north, south, and
east.  The shell of the old building was torn down in the fall of 1985 with
only the floor kept intact, and a new 54 x 96 foot steel building was erected.
The new hall includes an expanded seating area as well as a larger bar area and
a separate kitchen.

The cooperation of our small community was very much in evidence in this
project as all the work was done with volunteer labor.  Through the winter of
1985-86, on almost any given day, there were people at the hall donating their
time, energy, and equipment.  Almost 125 different people gave of their time
and talents to make this dream a reality. Special recognition must go to Arnold
Wiederrich who served as president of the Civic Club during this time and spent
many hours organizing this volunteer labor force.

The money for this project was also raised in the community. The fund raising
committee sponsored dances, raffles, bingo and various other fund raisers with
matching funds from the A.A.L.

When the project was done 199 pounds of nails, 18 tons of sheet rock, 164
pieces of steel, 50 gallons of paint, 7000 feet of electrical wire, 34 poles,
11 rafters, 4922 feet of wood, 158 pieces of insulation board, and 132 rolls of
insulation had been used.

The first dance in the new Civic Center was held on March 14, 1986.  A huge
crowd was on hand to see the beautiful new hall and admire what our community
could do in the spirit of cooperation.  It is hoped that this building will
serve as a social center for the area for many years to come.

PA TRUCKING

PA Trucking started in 1980 with one cattle pot trailer owned jointly by Curtis
Aman and Herman Poeppel.  The trailer was leased to Harry Kirschenmann
Trucking.  In the summer of 1981 Curtis bought a semi-tractor, which is driven
by Charley Johnson.  On January 8, 1982, PA Trucking was granted a PUC Class B
permit.  PA Trucking now consists of three trailers, one is pulled by Charley
Johnson, and the other two are leased to drivers owning their own tractors.
This company now hauls cattle and hogs to Minnesota, North Dakota, Nebraska,
Kansas, Iowa and Colorado, with the majority of the hauling being done in South
Dakota.

STAN'S AUTO

Putt!  Putt!  Putt! was a new sound on the main street of the little prairie
town of Hosmer.  Progress was taking place as the Hosmer Auto Company
introduced the automobile to the community.  The company was a partnership
formed in April, 1917, consisting of John Haerter, G. J. Walth, Edwin Herrboldt
and Jacob Walth.  Their business sold and repaired cars, with contracts for
Buicks, Maxwells and Fords.  The first World War broke up the partnership.  In
1918 this business was owned by John Haerter and his son Arthur.  They sold
Ford autos.  In 1920 Carl Stoecker bought into the business.  A fire, in 1920,
destroyed most of the garage, although the cement floor was used when it was
rebuilt and has been used up until the time Stanley Mohr bought the business
from Gideon Kaul in 1983.  At that time Stanley Mohr poured a new cement floor.

In 1929, J. C. Schaible and Jacob Lammle entered into a partnership and bought
the garage and automobile business. They named it the Midwest Motors.  In 1945,
Lammle sold his interest to Jacob Schaible, who continued the business with the
Ford Agency.  He remodeled the building which kept the same look until 1983,
when Stanley Mohr purchased it.

In August, 1956, Jacob Schaible sold his business to Albert Schlaht and Sam
Job.  They transferred their Case Implement Franchise to this new location and
also sold and serviced Ford cars and trucks.  It was known as Schlaht & Job.
Later they gave up the Case and Ford dealership and concentrated their business
on service and repairs.

In 1969, Schlaht sold his interests to Sam Job, who ran the business until
1971, when he sold his business to Gideon Kaul and in 1973 the building.
During this time the garage was known as Kaul's Re air.

In November, 1983, having worked at this business and then owning his business
for a total of 37 years and feeling he would like to try something else, Gideon
sold his complete business to Stanley Mohr.  Stanley operated the business until
the end of August, 1986.

Gideon reminisces that during the time of his association with this business
place several local men have worked there at one time or another.  Some of
these names recalled are: Paul Schock, Calvin Kranzler, Harry Martell, Herbert
Gienger, Herbert Guthmiller, Albert Stabbe, Frank Imberi, Carl Martell, George
Malsam, Herbert J. Hieb and Roger Gramm.

We do not have much history concerning this building before 1917 but there are
some indications from the Court House that there might have been a fire in
1910, probably destroying the building, which was built up again in 1917. Then
another fire destroying some of the building in 1920 or 21, being restored
again.  It was remodeled by Schaible to almost its present state, with some
changes by Stanley Mohr. And it is still looking good and proud of the history
that has passed through the doors of this notable Hosmer landmark. Proud enough
to be chosen to appear on the centennial cups, to be bought, cherished and
antiquated by those who love Hosmer History.

STOECKER'S JEWELRY & ELECTRIC SHOP

Johnny (Sparky) Stoecker was born November 23, 1914, the youngest of the
Gottlieb Stoecker family of seven children.  He started his business career in
1930 in a gasoline filling station that was built on the east side of A.
Herrboldt & Company.  That same building is now a car shed on the Terry Ulmer
farm.

Sparky and his brother, Albert, ran that gasoline station for a number of
years.  In 1935, Albert left, going into the grocery business with Edwin
Eisenbeisz, and Sparky continued working there until October, 1937, when he
went to CC Camp at Custer, South Dakota.  His father died on Christmas day in
1938 so he returned in April, 1939, to live with his mother.

About 1940, Sparky started working as a meat cutter for G. J. Walth & Company,
who bought out Eisenbeisz & Stoecker Grocery and Dry Goods Store.  While he
worked at Walth's Store, he did watch repair at home.  He had done some work on
watches at the CC Camp and received some help from his brother-in-law, Karl
Bongfeldt.

In October, 1944, he started a Jewelry and Watch Repair Shop in the building
that was the Frank Glatt Barber Shop and is replaced today by a new building,
the Haerter-Heckenlaible Corporation.

In May, 1946, Sparky bought the building he now occupies, and moved into it in
July, 1946.  This building was built by Dan Pietz about 1920 to be used as a
drug store.  It has a four-room apartment in the back.

On April 2, 1956, Easter Monday, Sparky married Ida M. Fischer of Leola, South
Dakota.  They continue to live in the apartment as Sparky continues his watch
work.  He has pick up places for watches, electric shavers, and jewelry repair
in Bowdle, Eureka, Leola, Ipswich and Roscoe.

On May 20, 1985, Ida had her right leg amputated above the knee.  She very
capably wheels in and out of the apartment and tends the store while Sparky
steps out for mail, goes to the store, does banking business, etc.  They own a
house on the east side of the Mohr Auction building, but Ida wants to be at the
store apartment where she can visit with people.

TERI'S SALON

Teri's Salon was started in December, 1973.  Teri (Kappenman) Heilman graduated
from Stewarts School of Hairstyling in September, 1972.  She worked under a
licensed beautician for one year.  She opened her own shot) in the basement of
the Heilman home.

Working with Teri in the shop have been Julie Hoff of rural Eureka (1983-84)
and Peggy Eberhart Mohr of rural Hosmer (1984-85).  Teri wishes to thank the
many good patrons for making the shop successful the past 12 years.

VALLEY TELCO

The telephone was not used in Hosmer until 1922 when forty-five people bought
seventy-nine $25 shares to start a telephone office.  They bought a small frame
building from Pat Ford who had used it as a paint office.

The first telephone operator was Matilda Stoecker (Mrs. Edwin Eisenbiesz) who
was operator for three years.

The Walth sisters, Lydia and Mathilda, were the operators for the next fifteen
years.  Mathilda Walth (Mrs. Sam Pietz) started as operator in June, 1924,
succeeding Matilda Stoecker, and worked until 1939.  Her sister, Lydia, was
assistant operator until the time of her death in 1933. Another sister,
Hildegard (Mrs.  Albert Stabbe) also assisted her sister in 1931 after high
school graduation until 1937. Wages were low and the hours were long; from 8
a.m. to 9 p.m. The switchboard consisted of 50 numbers; 20 business phones, 15
residential phones plus 4 farm lines, I toll line and long distance calls.
Albert (Slim) Kunde was the repair and line man.

A night phone was installed at the J. S. Walth residence for emergency calls.
It was the operators duty to answer all calls, even after hours, and go deliver
the message.  These varied from calling Dr. H. Neukamp, delivering a death
message, business calls or even a message from a young lady calling her boy
friend.

Additional operators were lone Gutjahr (Mrs.  Benny Keppler), Olivia Lauer
(Mrs. Hoff) and Viola Kammerer (Mrs. Gerhardt Knittel).

Officers of the first company were John Haerter, Sr. - president; Frank Boldt -
vice president; Gottlieb Walth - secretary, and Jacob Walth - treasurer.  They
were elected in 1921 and kept their offices until Bill Schilling bought the
shares in 1947.  The only change occurred when John Haerter, Sr. died and Frank
Boldt became president and Art Haerter vice president.

Bill Schilling's first operator was Frieda Kaul (Mrs. George Roiger).  The
other operators were Agnes Gisi (Mrs. Donald Roberts) and Carolyn Gisi (Mrs.
Gary Rath).

In 1953, Bill Schilling transferred the system to dial with 73 subscribers.
Twenty-seven of these were private lines with the rest 2 or 4 party lines.

In 1959, Valley Telephone Cooperative purchased the company from Bill
Schilling.  They then built a cement block building to house their physical
plant.  In February of 1961, Frank Gisi was hired as the local area manager.

The winter of 1965-66 proved to be a disastrous one for the phone company as in
December an ice storm downed many lines and then in March of 1966 a three-day
blizzard dropped several feet of wet snow in the area snapping lines and
breaking poles.  VTC estimated the damage throughout their whole area at
$150,000.

In 1973, the entire system was upgraded with underground wire replacing
overhead wires and private lines replacing old party lines.  An addition was
also built to the Hosmer office at this time to make room for all the new
equipment. Redwood siding was added to the building in 1984 to give the
exterior of the building a complete new look.

Toll free calling between Hosmer and Eureka was established on January 22, 1975.

In 1984, another building project was launched in Hosmer as a cable tv system
was installed with two large satellite dishes added to the physical plant.

The modern world calls for constant upgrading, so a completely new electronic
system was installed in the Hosmer office in 1986.

At present there are 290 telephone subscribers and 113 cable tv subscribers
serviced by the Hosmer office.

Milton Aman, Valley Telco director from Hosmer, and Frank Gisi, Hosmer Local
Area Manager, examine the new electronic board on cut over day, June 30, 1986.

        Hosmer's first bulk gas station owned and operated by Bill Jeneky.

                        Churches

HOLY TRINITY CHURCH

Early records show that a Catholic Church was first established in the middle
1880's with the first church being built about 8 miles west, 3 1/2 miles north
of Hosmer.  After this church burned another was built 7 1/2 miles west of
Hosmer.  This also burned.  The cemeteries in each location are the only
landmarks for the exact location of these two churches.

Horse and buggy was the most convenient means of transportation, but many
poorer families would walk for hours, just to get to Sunday mass.

After the fires destroyed these churches, the members affiliated with either
the Bowdle or the Hillsview church until it was decided to build in Hosmer.
Bishop O'Gorman had plans to build a new church, rectory, and school in
Hillsview, since the town was predominately Catholic.  At the same time, 30
families in Hosmer were making plans of their own to build a new church.
Father Borman, a priest from Ipswich would occasionally come to serve the
people here.  He encouraged them to build.  Other local instigators were Joseph
Schoch, Joseph Heier, Sr., Kasper Benefit, Sr., and Carl Hoffart, Sr.  These
same four made up the building committee and also served as the first trustees.

John Heckenlaible donated the land on the corner lot (the size of 4 lots to be
adequate for a church and rectory). The building was started in 1910 using
plans similar to the old Lutheran Church in Hosmer.  Ben Ochs and Gottlieb
Gross were hired as carpenters, and with the help of some parishioners, built
the church for less than $5,000.  The church was finished in the fall of 191 1,
however, this had been done without having consulted the Bishop, and when some
of the members went to Sioux Falls to deliver the title of the church, the
Bishop refused to accept it.  At that time, Dr. John F. Quinn was located in
Hosmer.  He and other members tried a second time to have the Bishop sanction
the church here, but he refused even more emphatically.

Eventually with the influencing aid of Father Borman, the Bishop did consent to
recognize the church in Hosmer. Thus it was officially accepted and dedicated
in July, 1912.

Martin Masch, son of Peter and Margaretha Masch, Russian immigrants, was the
first child baptized on September 8, 1912, by Rev.  J. A. Sullivan M.S.C. The
first wedding mass was celebrated February 3, 1913, for Stephen Strudel and
Margaret Stapler.  Altar boys were Joseph J. Heier, Jr., Martin Schoch, and
Andrew Gisi.

In 1913, Lawrence Hartman passed away and it was then that Joseph Schoch
donated one acre of land about 1 1/2 miles west of Hosmer for a cemetery, later
an adjoining acre was added.  Joseph Schoch also served as the first organist
in the new church.

Hosmer was a mission station of Hillsview until 1959.  In the early years,
during the winter months, the priest would come to Hosmer on Saturday by train,
stay overnight with some parishioners, often the Joseph Schochs or the Joseph
Heier, Srs., say the Sunday mass and leave by train for Hillsview on Monday
morning.  During the early years, until roads and transportation improved, mass
was only said in Hosmer every other Sunday.

In 1956, Fr.  Wisniewski along with the building committee, Leo Schwingler,
George Salwei, Joseph J. Heier, Jr., Jacob Grismer and Baldaser Grismer, made
plans to build a rectory directly behind the old church and remodel the inside
of the church.  Fr.  Wisniewski was transferred and Fr. Anthanmatten encouraged
the people to build a new church and rectory just west of the old church.  The
lots were bought from Paul Kunde and again the people worked hard hauling dirt
and rocks to fill in and level the lots.  A basement and rectory were finished
at a cost of over $40,000.  In July, 1960, the old church was moved to Eureka
and was used until 1985 as Eureka's St. Joseph's Catholic Church.

In 1959, the parish priest, Fr.  Jungwirth, moved into the new rectory in
Hosmer and in the spring of 1960 church services were conducted in the new
basement church.  The first baptism in the basement was Terrence Kappenmann,
son of Wilfred and Katherine Kappenmann on March 27, 1960.  The first wedding
was Janice Imbery and Jerome Arbach on May 23, 1960, and the first funeral was
for Leopold Schmidt on July 25, 1960.

In 1966, Fr.  John Hoorman as well as the building committee, George Salwei,
Joe Scherr, Raphael Heier, Frank Gisi, Adam Heier, and Leo Schwingler, began
making plans to build a new church above the existing basement.  Bernard
Hillyer of Bismarck was hired as the architect.  The first bid letting was held
on August 23, 1966.  The low bid of $111,600 was thought to be too high, so the
architect revised the plans and a second bid letting took place on February 21,
1967.  At this time, the general contract went to Jake Mohr and Sons
Construction of Hosmer, with an electrical contract going to Clarence Rath of
Long Lake and a mechanical contract to Ferdinand Hieb of Hosmer with a
sub-contract to Ell's Sheet Metal of Aberdeen.

The new church, a 94 x 56 foot brick and frame structure will seat 270 people.
It was built at a cost of $80,138.

Construction began on April 10, 1967, and was completed by December 8, 1967.
Dedication services were held on December 18, 1967.  Because of inclement
weather, Bishop Hoch was unable to attend, but he designated Rev. Daniel
Schaefer, C.P.P.S. to act in his stead.  Fr.  Schaefer was Provincial of the
Precious Blood Fathers of Kansas City, Missouri.  Father Hoorman belongs to
this order as did many former priests who served the Hosmer parish.

Father Hoorman and the parishioners were very happy to celebrate their
Christmas services of 1967 in their beautiful new church.

The first baby baptized in the new church was Anthony Heilman, son of Christ
and Agatha Heilman on March 3, 1968.  The first wedding was that of Dale Gerber
and Gloria Maisch on December 30, 1967.  The first funeral was Frank G.
Malsam's on January 15, 1968.

On June 1, 1975, another important event in the church history took place. For
it was at this time that a "mortgage burning" took place as the new church was
completely paid for.  Christ Heilman was chairman of the church council at this
time and served as master of ceremonies for the special event.  Serving on the
church council with him were: Frank Gisi, Raphael Heier, Josephine Heier, Dave
Malsam, and George Salwei.  Fr.  Francis Wisniewski was the parish priest.

At the present time Holy Trinity Church has a membership of 194 representing 70
households.

Priests who have served at Holy Trinity include: Rev. Fred Borman, 1911-13;
Rev.  Valentine Braun, 1913-16; Rev. P. G. Kelly, 1916-17; Rev.  Joseph
Hensbach, 1917-24; Rev. Henry Geilen, 1924-30; Rev.  Albert Wallock, 1930-38;
Rev. L. R. Harding, 1938-43; Rev.  Suitbert Brueckman, 1943-53; Rev.  Pius
Mardian, 1953-55; Rev.  Francis A. Wisniewski, 1955-57; Rev. Joseph
Anthanmatten, 1957-58; Rev.  Alphonse Jungwirth, 1958-63; Rev. Ernest Lucas,
1963-64; Rev.  John Hoorman, 1964-71; Rev.  Paul Becquet, 1971-74; Rev.
Francis Wisniewski, 1974-80; Fr.  Don Marohn, 1980-84.

Six churches in the area are presently served by a pastoral team consisting of
Sr.  Karen La Follette, Sr.  Darlene Gutenkauf, Fr.  Don Marohn and Fr. Owen
Klapperich with Fr. Klapperich residing in the Hosmer rectory.

Holy Trinity will celebrate its 75th anniversary in 1987.

HOSMER REFORMED CHURCH

The Reformed Church in the United States did not neglect the German immigrants
who emigrated from South Russia, but sent Home Missionaries who supplied their
needs. These Pioneer Pastors noticed that the members of one church settled in
one area.  They would establish a preaching point in that area and labor to
organize a church.  Rev.  Ulrich Reue gathered the Reformed people of Eureka
and Glueckstal.  Rev.  Peter Bauer came in 1892 and helped organize Heidelberg,
Calvin, and Neudorf in 1894.  These five churches were supplied by one minister
and were called the Eureka Charge. He completed the organization of the Hosmer
Church in May, 1903.  The names of the Charter members were: Johann Dewald,
Jacob Gundert, George Guthmiller Johann Wruck, Gottlieb Wiederich, Ludwig
Hoffer, Adam Preszler, Martin Preszler, Christoph Preszler, John M. Preszler,
Martin Hoffer, William Gundert, Jacob Aman, Michael Schnabel, Gottlieb Tesky,
John Wruck, Jr., Fredrich Guthmiller, and Johann Mehlhoff.

The History of the Hosmer Reformed Church divided itself into four natural
divisions.  The first period is from 1903 to 1916 and can be described as
'Laying the Foundations.' The second period from 1916 to 1934 are the years of
'Building the Superstructure' of the Church.  From 1934 until 1952 Rev.
Grossmann 'Strengthened the Foundations' of the Church and from 1952 until the
present the minister and members of the Hosmer Church have been 'Maintaining
the Superstructure.'

Laying the Foundation 1903 to 1916
        During these years Hosmer Church was a member of the Eureka Charge or
Pharrstelle.  The Charge had six congregations and so the minister came only
once every six weeks.  The other Sundays an Elder conducted the Service and
read a sermon. Rev. Jungeblut stayed only from April 15, 1903, until November
7, 1905.  He was followed by Rev.  Gustav Zenk. Rev.  Zenk arrived July 9,
1905, and served Hosmer until April, 1916.  He left a lasting stamp upon the
Eureka Charge. Most of the records we have come from his pen.

Building the Superstructure 1916 to 1934
        In 1915, the Manse was built in Hosmer.  The following summer the
Eureka Charge was divided and the Hosmer Charge was organized with Heidelberg,
Calvin, Neudorf and Hosmer as member congregations.  In November, 1916, Rev. E.
H. Oppermann was installed as the minister of the Charge. He served the four
congregations until July, 1919.  Johann Gotermann came in December of 1919 and
ministered to the four Churches until March of 1922.  Peter Schild came in May
of 1922 and stayed until January, 1927.  He designed the present church
building which was built in 1925.  When the present building was completed
Heidelberg decided to dissolve and join the Hosmer Congregation.  This action
was taken in May of 1926.  After Rev.  Schild left in January, 1927, Rev.  D.
Buelter served the three remaining congregations until November of 1933.  These
years in the life of the Hosmer Church were years of transition, of growth, and
consolidation.

Strengthening the Foundations 1934 to 1952
        The third period covers the years of the Ministry of Rev. Walter
Grossman.  Rev.  Grossman came in April, 1934, and served the Hosmer Charge
until July 1, 1952.  During the ministry he worked ardently to keep the
Churches of the Hosmer Charge from joining the proposed merger of the Reformed
Churches in the United States and the Evangelical Synod of North America.  He
first carefully strengthened the internal structures of the Churches, then he
completely reorganized the foundations and legal basis of the congregations and
had the congregation vote unanimously not to join the union.  He gave his
energies not only to his church but also to the town and school.  He stood and
faced down the ecclesiastical forces of the 30's and 40's but the economical
factors were too powerful.  Farming and rural life were being turned upside
down.

Maintaining the Superstructure 1952 to 1962
        When Rev.  Grossman left, the change in the rural Dakota immediately
expressed itself.  The members of the Calvin Church realized that they were
only eight miles from Eureka on a good road with good cars and so they
dissolved. During the fourth period, we experienced the population decline, the
wearing away of towns, the consolidation of churches.  Some thought they could
turn the clock backwards or at least hold it still.  However, it will not do to
drive a 1940 automobile, neither will it do to have a 1940 church program.
Others felt that all they had to do was to join a large organization and that
such an action would solve all their problems.

This period of change has been reflected in the pastors. Rev.  Klaudt came in
July, 1952, and stayed until November, 1954.  The church was vacant from then
until December, 1955.  At this time, Rev.  Lenard Stockmeier came.  He felt
that the farming revolution demanded that the Neudorf Church join with the
Hosmer Church and he worked toward this goal until it was accomplished in 1956.
Rev.  Stockmeier left in August of 1957 and the church was again vacant until
November, 1958, when the retired Rev.  Henry Huenemann came while the
congregation continued to look for a younger pastor.  They found Rev.  Hessel
Stevens who came in 1960. During Stevens' pastorate new hymnals were bought and
the church basement was remodeled.  In 1971, the RCUS annual Classis meeting
was held in the newly remodeled building. Rev. Stevens resigned in 1977, having
completed a full seventeen year pastorate. He was followed by Rev.  Daryl
Dockter who served from 1977 to 1979.  In 1979, new sidewalks and a corner
standing area were poured.  After a two year vacancy, Rev.  Sam Allison came in
1981 and he continues to the present. In 1982, the exterior of the church was
painted from the top of the steeple down to the ground.  In 1983, the interior
of the basement was also painted.

        The Reformed Church of Hosmer built in 1925.

What of the future?  The Hosmer church has had to continually adjust to
changing conditions.  Every period has been one of adjustment.  We must now
adjust to the fact that we are no longer a large church.  We must realize that
we are a mission church and that if we wish to survive, we must try to
establish Reformed young men and women on the farms and strengthen one another
even as our 'pioneer' forbearers have done.  As they worked to strengthen their
church, so must we if we are to survive.  "Let them praise the name of the Lord
for his name alone is excellent; his glory is above the earth and heaven."
Psalm 148 v. 13.

        The present minister, Rev. Sam Allison.

History of the Allison Family

Pastor Sam Allison was raised in Allegheny County in western Pennsylvania and
received his B.S. Degree with an Agriculture major from the Pennsylvania State
University. He furthered his training at Westminster Theological Seminary and
received a M. Div. in 1963.  He married Sonja Huttel from Philadelphia in 1960.
The Reformed Church in the U.S. ordained him to the gospel ministry in 1964 at
Ashley, North Dakota.

The Allisons have six children.  Peter is in the class of 1986 at the U.S.
Naval Academy with a major in electrical engineering.  Archibald is in the
class of 1988 at Hillsdale College, Michigan, with a double major in political
economy and German and a math minor.  Martha is in the class of 1988 at Grove
City College, Pennsylvania.  After her marriage to Hank Kenyon in May, 1986,
she plans to reside in western Pennsylvania.  Three children attend El Shaddai
Academy, a home school.  Gary and Benjamin (born 1970 and 1971) are in high
school while Ruth (born 1980) is in elementary school.

In addition to his pastoral duties, Pastor Allison is president of Sovereign
Grace Library, Chairman of the Hosmer Area Right to Life, on the Hosmer Medical
Board and the Eureka Hospital Board, and Stated Clerk of the Northern Plains
Classis of the Reformed Church in the U.S.

JEHOVAH CHURCH

The Jehovah Congregation of rural Hosmer was started in 1885.  A pastor from
Europe came to America.  They traveled from settlement to settlement and then
later from church to church bringing the word of God to the early settlers.
They used a farm house as their house of worship.  In 1887, the neighbors built
a small church, 20 by 30 feet and 8 feet high. The Emanuel, Solomon, and
Jehovah churches joined together as one.

The Jehovah Lutheran Church was built in 1911. The parsonage was located across
the road, one block from the church.  They usually had five weeks of summer
school.

Later the Hope Church northwest of Roscoe joined.  A call was sent to Reverend
Lehner in 1933.  At this time the Hope Church paid $600.00, the Jehovah Church
paid $300.00 for the minister's salary.  Rev.  Lehner served the church from
1933 to 1956 when he retired and moved to Roscoe.  Most of the members joined
St. John's Lutheran in Hosmer and the remainder joined the church at Roscoe.
The church had 10 pastors serving them.

        The above picture is of a class taught by Rev.  Lehner.

HISTORY OF ST. JOHN'S LUTHERAN CONGREGATION

St. John's Evangelical Lutheran Church of Hosmer was organized under the
leadership of Pastor J. Koeppel on March 3, 1895, eight years after the
founding of Hosmer.  The eight charter members who signed the constitution were
Emmanuel Reiner, Fred Kunde, Gustav Kunde, Jacob Schaeffer, Philipp Schaeffer,
George Heyne, Ephriam Heyne, and Fred Schlaht.  Since most of the families had
come from Neudorf, South Russia, they chose the name "Neudorf Congregation".
Pastor Koeppel, a traveling missionary of the former Iowa Synod, now a merged
part of the American Lutheran Church, served the congregation but one year.  He
left the field because of ill health in the spring of 1896.

In July of 1896, Pastor A. Ottersberg accepted the call to Hosmer and for a few
weeks ministered to the area from Eureka.  In August of that year at a meeting
in Jehovah church northeast of town five congregations agreed to form the
Hosmer parish.  These five congregations were: Neudorf in town; Jehovah and
Immanuel to the northeast; Hope to the southeast; and St. John west of Hosmer.
The pastor's salary was $350 and services for the Neudorf congregation were
held in the school house or in a vacant store building. The pastor was to
reside in Hosmer and in the fall a small two-room house was purchased as the
first parsonage.

Chiefly through the efforts of Emmanuel Reiner, the vacant store was purchased
and moved to the congregation's property adjacent to the parsonage.  With the
addition of a small tower, and the construction of an altar, pulpit, and pews
by the members, the congregation came into possession of its first house of
worship at a total cost of $600.  The dedication service was held on Pentecost,
1898, with the pastor's father-in-law, Dr. S. Fritschel from Dubuque, Iowa, as
the festival speaker.  The offering for the day totaled $31.43.

In 1898, also, the Haerter, Heckenlaible, and Sonnenfeld families from the
Menno, South Dakota, area arrived in Hosmer to establish various businesses and
subsequently became members of the steadily growing congregation.  The children
of the parish met at the church in Hosmer for "summer school" and confirmation
instruction.  Hence, the people became more Hosmer-minded. Additions to the
church property included a small organ, a bell purchased in 1900, and the
provision of a pulpit Bible and altar paraments in 1902 by friends in Germany.
A choir was organized, rehearsing chiefly during the winter months.

Records indicate that another congregation, Neuglueckstal, had joined the
parish during these years but now severed its connections and joined the Java
Parish.

The remaining five congregations called Pastor Richard Taeuber who arrived in
his first field of labor in July of 1903. It was Pastor Taeuber's conviction
that the smaller congregations of the parish should dissolve and join the
remaining ones in order that more regular services might be provided for all
members.  He worked diligently toward this goal.

During his pastorate the Ladies Aid and the Luther League were organized. In
1907, he resigned to accept a call to Ashley, North Dakota, and this action
furthered a division in the parish.  The Jehovah and Immanuel congregations
decided to form a separate parish.  There upon the remaining three
congregations pleaded with Pastor Taeuber to remain and extended a new call
which he accepted.  A new parsonage was constructed in 1908 at the cost of
$1,800 and in 1910 the new church was dedicated to the glory of God at the cost
of $5,000.  The steady growth of the congregation was manifested by the
circumstance that it was host to the District Convention in 191 1. Such growth
may be attributed in a large measure to the congregation's faithful training of
the young through a parochial school.

The closing years of Pastor Taeuber's ministry in Hosmer were darkened by World
War I, during which, because of German language restrictions, the congregation
was forced to worship in private homes.  As a testimony of the congregation's
loyalty to the country's cause, thirty young men of the parish responded to the
call for military duty.

Pastor Taeuber accepted another call in 1918, completing 15 years of service in
St. John's congregation.

In August of 1918, Pastor F. Braun became the next pastor.  He was confronted
with the great handicap of ministering to his parish without regular public
worship services. At this time also the "flu" epidemic was taking its heavy
toll.

With the close of the war, regular services were once more conducted in the
church and progress was evidenced in that the church was redecorated, a new
bell was hung in the tower, and an iron fence was erected around the cemetery.

A most joyous event of Pastor Braun's pastorate was the celebration on August
21, 1921, of the 25th Anniversary of the organization of the congregation.
Former Pastors Koeppel, Ottersberg, and Taeuber were the festival speakers.
The offering for the day totaled $108.80.

Pastor Braun accepted another call in August of 1924, and in January of 1925
Pastor Streeb arrived at St. John's. Pastor Streeb's chief contribution to the
growth of the parish during his six years as the pastor lay in his efforts to
bring peace, love, and unity to the triple pointed field.  During this time St.
John congregations severed its connections with the parish and became a part of
the Bowdle parish.  As a result more regular services were secured for the
Hosmer congregation. Pastor Streeb preached his last sermon at Hosmer in March
of 1931.

In June of 1931, Pastor H. Haase came to the parish. Shortly after his arrival
Hope congregation was granted permission to join another parish. The following
year, with the congregation numbering 110 family units, its name was changed
from Neudorf to St. John Lutheran.  In the same year a church building fund to
enlarge or build a new structure was begun. However, with no crops the next
four years, very little could be done to enlarge the fund.  Pastor Haase left
the congregation in January of 1942.

The congregation was served by Pastor Hans Senft from 1942 until 1944. During
this time a number of improvements in the interior of the church were made.
Sunday School classes were taught in the English language thus expanding the
foundation of concern and welfare of the future congregation.

Student Fred Mueller from Wartburg Seminary at Dubuque, Iowa, arrived in
November, 1944, and served the congregation for six months.  During this time
services in the English language were conducted twice each month and the
congregation purchased the home of Mrs. Gottlieb Stoecker as a future parsonage
for $7,000.  The parsonage was dedicated October 7, 1945, with Pastor F. Doyen
as speaker.

On May 20, 1945, Pastor Carl G. Fink was installed as pastor.  In the fall of
the same year the congregation contributed $5,000 for a new church and passed a
resolution to strive for a goal of $50,000 for this purpose.  The church paper
"Honor Roll" was soon accepted and during 1946 a building committee was
elected.  Daily Vacation Bible School was introduced, and the congregation
celebrated its fiftieth anniversary on June 9th and 10th of that year.

The Lutheran Brotherhood which was organized in 1947 supervised the "God's
Acre" Project in 1949 with an ingathering of $1,627.21 contributed to the
building fund.

The present church building was constructed at a cost of $156,583.80, plus
20,000 hours of donated labor.  It was dedicated on April 16, 1950, with
District President Dr. George Landgrebe and former pastor, Dr. Richard Taeuber,
as speakers.

Pastor Fink preached his farewell sermon on January 21, 1951.

In July, 1951, Dr. M. F. Amelung became the pastor of St. John.  During his
ministry a public address system with Tower Trumpet was installed and electric
candelabra for the chancel were purchased.  Although crops were poor in 1952,
1953, and 1954, the indebtedness of the church was reduced by $30,000.  In
1952, the old church was sold to the Hosmer Medical Association and converted
into a medical unit.

A Women's Missionary Society was organized in September of 1952 and it in turn
sponsored the Junior Mission Band.  The fiftieth anniversary of the Ladies Aid
Society was celebrated this year.

Following a six month period of illness, Pastor Amelung suffered a fatal heart
attack on December 3, 1955, bringing to an abrupt close the service of one of
the Lord's faithful servants.

Pastor Adam Meidinger accepted the call to St. John Lutheran congregation and
was installed June 3, 1956.  Prior to his coming the congregation had voted to
reduce German services to alternate Sundays.

In 1957, Jehovah Lutheran Church, northeast of Hosmer, closed and a number of
families joined St. John Lutheran. After a concentrated effort toward debt
retirement the goal was achieved and a mortgage burning ceremony was held on
April 20, 1958.  The first Easter Sunrise Service, conducted by the Luther
League was held.  Additional Sunday School classrooms were constructed in the
balcony, and a new kitchen was added to the parsonage. Pastor Meidinger left
Hosmer in 1959.

In May, 1960, Pastor George F. Dirks arrived at St. John. Numerous improvements
were made in the church and the parsonage.  In 1963, Council action provided
for German service on the fourth Sunday of each month. During this year also,
the women of the congregation re-organized and became St. John Lutheran Church
Women with membership in various working circles.

Pastor Dirks accepted another call in October of 1965.

On May 1, 1966, Pastor R. S. Roesler was installed as the new pastor. Since
that time all Sunday Services have been conducted in the English language with
German language services on occasions.  In September, 1966, property in the
neighboring block was purchased and converted into a spacious parking lot.
Added physical improvements have included the installation of Dew glass doors
at the east and south entrances to the church, and construction of new sidewalk
on the major portion of the church property.

In April of 1970, the 20th Anniversary of the dedication of the present church
building was noted with a special service at which Pastor Carl Fink was the
speaker.

On June 26 & 27, 1971, the 75th Anniversary of this congregation was celebrated
with guest speakers being former Pastors Dirks and Mueller, and sons of the
congregation Robert Herrboldt, Elton Walth, Paul Bauman, Otto Schaible and
Arthur Walth.

During Pastor Roesler's ministry at St. John's, a Cherub and Junior Choir were
organized, along with the long-established adult choir which helped beautify
the worship services.

Pastor Roesler preached his last sermon here in February of 1975.

Pastor Duane Neugebauer came to serve St. John's in August of 1975 and served
the congregation faithfully for three and one-half years.  He left in February
of 1979.

On July 1, 1979, Pastor Bruce Thalacker came and served St. John's for three
and one-half years.

In 1980, Carillon Bells were given as a memorial and were installed in the
church tower, bringing music to the entire community three times a day. During
Pastor Bruce's ministry early communion was started.  A two-octave hand bell
set was given as a memorial and a youth bell-choir was organized known as
"Bells of Celebration".

In December of 1982, Pastor Bruce accepted another call.

In March of 1983, Pastor Howard Stockman came to serve this congregation and is
the present pastor.  A gift of central air-conditioning was installed in the
church in 1983. Also community church services for Thanksgiving and Good Friday
were started, with a mass choir performing. In August of 1986, a chair lift was
installed so handicapped people can enter the sanctuary of the church and also
the fellowship hall in the basement.  Part of this chair lift was given as a
memorial, and the rest was from donations.

Since 1932, St. John Lutheran Congregation has witnessed the ordination into
the Holy ministry of several of its sons: Otto Schaible, Arthur Walth, Herman
Senft, Robert Herrboldt, Roland Jakober, Paul Bauman, James Haerter and Randy
Eisenbeisz.  Other former sons ordained elsewhere are: Arthur Braun, Elton
Walth and Martin Haase.

With grateful hearts we thank and praise God who has so richly blessed our
congregation during these ninety-one years.

THE HISTORY OF THE CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH

In the early 1880's, the German people from South Russia began to settle and
homestead in the Hosmer and Eureka area.  The immigrants had belonged to either
the Reformed or Lutheran denominations in Russia, but upon coming to this
country, they were bewildered by the many denominations within each church.
Their first interest was to find a place of worship and since they didn't unite
with any denomination, they met in their homes for worship and later, in the
warehouse in Hosmer.

Seeing that the Congregational Church was much like the churches to which they
were accustomed, these people decided to organize the St. Paul's Congregational
Church in 1889 under the leadership of Rev.  A. Kochendoerfer.  On June 20 and
November 24, 1899, land was purchased for a church site and in 1900 a church
edifice was built (above).  In 1946 this building was remodeled and moved to
its present location (above).  Also, two rural churches, St. Matthew's in 1941
and Emmanuel's in 1945 united with St. Paul's.

The Ministers who served this church were: The Rev.'s H. Hetzler, H. Vogler, A.
Suffa, J. Reister, Beckeschus, H. Baumann, A. Herforth, H. Dietrich, H.
Mertins, Fred Bauchistsch, J. Weber, E. Gackle, G. Keppler, Henry Hoersch, H.
E. Rath, Don Aisenbrey, and Keith Orr.

In the fall of 1963, the members of the St. Paul's Church decided to erect a
new church building.  On November 21, 1963, at a special meeting it was decided
to build a church 34' by 70'.

On April 12, 1964, ground breaking services were held with the Rev.  Adolph
Orman of Wishek, North Dakota, in charge.  On April 21, 1964, the contractor
started moving dirt for the church basement and the cornerstone was laid on
August 16, 1964.

Ministers that have served in the new church have been Harold Borhauer, Donald
Heeringa, John Iszler, Earl Lewis, Gideon Eberhart, and Alvin Schmierer.

The current membership is seventy-six.  Some of the groups of the church are
Women Fellowship, Sunday School (children and adult), Adult Choir, Youth Choir,
Youth Fellowship, and Young Couples Bible Study.

AMERICAN LEGION

The Adolph Forkel Post 189 was organized May 28, 1920, as the Schauer-Forkel
Post, with the following Charter Members:

  John Bieber                             Arthur Kunde
  John J. Gutjahr                         Carl Martell
  Arthur Haerter                          Jacob C. Schaible
  Edwin Heckenlaible                      Carl Schnaible
  Ed J. Herrboldt                         Henry Seibert
  Eugene Huber                            Ed. C. Ulmer
  David Hoffart                           Gottlieb J. Walth
  Wm. Janecke

When application was made to reinstate the Charter members and reactivate the
Post the following were added:

  Christ Bieber                           John Schnaible
  Jake Gottlieb                           Carl Stoecker
  John Guthmiller                         Earl Twedt
  Martin Heier                            George Wiederrich
  John J. Rohrbach

It was a happy occasion when the Post arrived at a membership of fifty during
that period.

During the dry years of the thirties, the Post again met difficulties, but
gained strength from or through additional members from Roscoe and Bowdle.
After World War II, Roscoe and Bowdle organized their own posts, decreasing our
membership.  In order to support our Post, all discharged men of World War II
were eligible for membership.  The peak in membership was reached during the
years of 1948-1952.

In 1952, the Hosmer Post purchased its own home at a cost of $8,250.00 from
Emil Sonnenfeld.  The Post had some funds on hand reducing the debt to
$6,600.00. This debt was retired July 1, 1959.

The building is widely used for various meetings and can rightly be classed as
a community home.  The main purpose of any American Legion Post is and must be
for the betterment of the community and especially for the benefit of our youth.

A few of the projects that the Legion is in charge of is the Summer Program
which includes: Boys T-ball and Midget teams, girls softball and swimming. The
Legion is also in charge of sending a Hosmer High School Junior to the annual
Boys State week held in Aberdeen, South Dakota.

The Hosmer Legion donated a large sum of money towards the tennis court, put up
a basketball court on the school ground, donated a chair lift for the Senior
Citizen's Home and in 1984 bought the 102 Ballroom donating it to the Hosmer
Civic Club.  These are also some of the projects that the Hosmer Legion has
been involved with.

The 1986 officers are:
  Commander: Milton Herrboldt
  1st Vice Commander: Arnold Wiederrich
  2nd Vice Commander: Herbert Schumacher
  Adjutant: Arnold Schmidt
  Finance Officer: Floyd Mohr
  Chaplain: Milbert Gutjahr
  Sgt. of Arms: Milton Aman
  Americanism Officer: Wilmer Heckenlaible
  Athletic Officer: Milton Mohr
  Service Officer: Arnold Pietz
  Children and Youth Officer: Walter Martell

VETERANS MEMORIAL
Adolph Forkel Post #189
Hosmer, South Dakota

"We remember, in honored glory, the American Veterans, from all wars, that were
called from their homes, were given arms and trained for war - we salute their
valiant courage and devoted service.

We are eternally grateful for the freedom and liberty that all Americans enjoy,
we ask all our fellow Americans to join us in paying tribute to the American
Veteran for their distinguished and dedicated service."
                                     American Legion Post #189

A memorial to honor the American War Veterans from our area has long been a
part of the Post program.  Finally, in the fall of 1983 a committee was
delegated to draw up plans for a Veteran's Memorial Park.  The committee
presented their plans to the Post membership in January, 1984.  Financially the
plan appeared to be out of reach.  However, with high spirits and a devoted
membership the Post commissioned the committee to proceed with the plan,
requesting to cut the cost wherever and however possible - with a further
request that the park be ready to dedicate on Memorial Day 1984 (4 months
away).  The land upon which the park is located was donated to the City of
Hosmer by Earl Walth in 1947 for the purpose of a city park - the Civic Club
planted trees and the City of Hosmer maintained the park for many years.  Upon
the request of the American Legion, and with a cooperative city council,
permission was granted to erect the Veteran's Memorial Park on this site.  Most
of the material that went into the make-up of the park was purchased at or
below dealer's cost - in keeping alive a tradition of the American service man,
some of the material was acquired by midnight requisition.  With generous
cooperation, all the labor to erect the park was donated by members of the
American Legion.  The American Legion Magazine recognized this park as unique
and carried it as a news item in their magazine.

The park was dedicated on Memorial Day, 1984.

AMERICAN LEGION AUXILIARY
Adolph Forkel Unit #189

"For God and country we associate ourselves together for the following purposes:

To uphold and defend the Constitution of the United States of America; to
maintain law and order; to foster and perpetuate a one hundred per cent
Americanism; to preserve the memories and incidents of our associations during
the Great Wars; to inculcate a sense of individual obligation to the community,
state and nation; to combat the autocracy of both the classes and the masses;
to make right the master of might; to promote peace and good will on earth; to
safeguard and transmit to posterity the principles of justice, freedom and
democracy; to participate in and contribute to the accomplishment of the aims
and purposes of the American Legion; to consecrate and sanctify our association
by our devotion to mutual helpfulness."

This is the Preamble to the Constitution of the American Legion Auxiliary. In
reviewing the records of the Auxiliary Unit, this is what has been their aim
and purpose in the fifty-six years that the Auxiliary has been organized.  The
Hosmer American Legion Auxiliary, Adolph Forkel Unit #189, Department of South
Dakota, was organized on November 20, 1931, with the following twelve ladies
present: Rose Gottlieb, Helga Twedt, Louise Heckenlaible, Adeline Gutjahr,
Lillian Kunde, Anna Guthmiller, Olga Herrboldt, Rose Haerter, Helen Stoecker,
Anna Huber, Olivia Kunde and Emma Walth.  Rose Gottlieb was elected temporary
President, and Anna Guthmiller temporary Secretary.  A membership committee was
appointed, and within the thirty-one day grace period the unit boasted a
paid-up membership of twenty. The unit then applied for a charter with the
following additional members: Amanda Heckenlaible, Alice Ulmer, Ernestine
Kunde, Emilia Wiederrich, Martha Herrboldt, Fredericka Herrboldt, Magdalina
Schaible and Christina Schnaible.  Each member was assessed ten cents to have
her name put on the charter.  The dues were set at $1.50 per year The unit
meetings were held in the homes of the members.

In the fall of 1933, several ladies from the Roscoe and Bowdle area joined the
unit, as they did not have enough women to organize their own unit.  In 1935,
several ladies from Hillsview, Java and Eureka joined the group.  This group of
ladies remained active until March, 1943.  The membership was kept up but there
were no meetings held until January, 1946, when the unit was reactivated with
41 members.  After World War II, in 1945, only ladies with a Hosmer and
Hillsview address remained members in Hosmer.

The membership of the unit has varied from 20 senior members in 1931, to 62
senior members and 39 junior members in 1974-1975.  At the present time, 1986,
the unit has 42 senior members and 13 junior members.  Women who are eligible
for membership in the American Legion Auxiliary are: mothers, sisters,
daughters and grand-daughters of a member of the American Legion; or a man or
woman who was killed during or died while serving in World War I and II, the
Korean Conflict, or the Vietnam Era; or a veteran of either war who has died
since honorably discharged from war service, or a woman veteran of the wars.

Americanism has always been stressed in the work of the American Legion
Auxiliary.  The unit presents flags to the kindergarten class on President's
Day, and also has them recite the Pledge of Allegiance.  They are taught to
respect the flag.  The unit awards cash prizes for Americanism essays and
poetry contests at school.  They have had several winners on the district and
state levels.

The unit has sponsored a delegate to Girls State since 1948.  Girls State was
held at Dakota Weslyan at Mitchell until 1985 when it was changed to South
Dakota State University at Brookings, South Dakota.  The following girls who
were chosen by the high school faculty have attended the sessions:

   1948 - LaVera Keisz                1968 - Patricia Keisz
   1949 - Elodie Kunde                1969 - Donna Gienger
   1950 - Lora Herrboldt              1970 - Londa Hieb
   1951 - Donna Krumm                 1971 - Sydney Herrboldt
   1952 - Freida Mohr                 1972 - Marcella Forkel
   1954 - Betty Heyne                 1973 - Cheryl Aman
   1955 - Elaine Gross                1974 - Joyce Kirschenman
   1956 - Ardella Goehring            1975 - Jane Malsam
   1957 - Judeen Lammle               1976 - Debra Preszler

   1961 - Shirley Neifer              1980 - Margo Mohr
   1962 - Patty Ulmer                 1981 - Jane Heilman

   1964 - Gail Schlaht                1983 - Shirley Sandmeier
   1965 - Ranae Lammle                1984 - Theresa Schmidt
   1966 - Sandra Martell              1985 - Connie Thomas
   1967 - Linda Pietz                 1986 - Dawn Aman

The unit has been active in community activities.  They have presented Poppy
Day programs, Mother's Day Teas and programs, sponsored immunization clinics
for many years, provided food baskets for needy families at Christmas, and
purchased dresses for the young girls of a family at Easter.  At Christmas time
the ladies visit the Senior Citizen's Home and present a program and give each
resident a gift of fruit.  The unit also sponsored the Girl Scouts and Brownie
Scouts for several years.  When the city park was being equipped they helped by
raising money to purchase playground and picnic equipment.  Throughout the
years they have helped with many things to make Hosmer a better place to live.

The uppermost aim in the work of the Auxiliary is to help the veteran in the
Veteran's Hospitals.  They supply them with playing cards, diapers, bibs,
hospital gowns, bedside bags, Christmas gifts for the veteran and his family,
and bingo prizes.  Carpet rags are sent to the hospital for the veterans to
make rugs, and then the ladies of the Auxiliary purchase the rugs from them.
This gives the veteran something to do and also gives him some kind of income.
Each year in May, a day is set aside as Poppy Day.  On this day poppies made by
the handicapped veterans are sold to the public.  The poppies are purchased by
the unit, and after the cost of the poppies has been deducted, 25% of the poppy
sales income is returned to the veterans who made them. Other hobby shop
articles made by the veterans are also purchased.  The tote bags made out of
bread wrappers have been a very popular item.

The unit assisted the Legion in acquiring their home which was purchased in
January, 1951.  The ladies furnished the kitchen with donations of dish towels,
utensils, etc.  The unit purchased the stove, refrigerator, dishes, electric
roasters, coffee makers and water heater.  Chairs and tables for the hall and
bathroom equipment were also purchased. The kitchen cabinets, sink and counter
top were furnished by the Auxiliary.  The ladies served a banquet for the
mortgage burning ceremonies.

To provide finances for the work that is done many fund raising projects have
been used, such as bake sales, plays, card parties, raffles, cake walks,
concession stands, programs, banquets, Snow Queen luncheons, sale of greeting
cards, cookbooks and vanilla, rent of tables and appliances, and rent of
kitchen.  The one fundraiser used the longest is monthly civic club dinners.
Many "fleisch kuechle" have been fried and enjoyed in the thirty years the
ladies have been serving.  The unit started this project in September of 1956,
and at this writing are still continuing to serve these dinners.

The unit has five Gold Star Mothers, Mrs. Henry Forkel, Mrs. Fred F. Mohr, Mrs.
Henry Mehlhoff, Mrs. Anton Malsam and Mrs. Jacob Martell.  The last Gold Star
Mother, Mrs. Henry Mehlhoff, passed away in 1982.  The mothers were remembered
with cards and gifts at Christmas and Mother's Day.

In November, for the Auxiliary birthday, the unit has a pot-luck supper for the
senior citizens and the junior members.

In March, for the Legion birthday, a pot-luck supper is also held with the
Legion members as the guests.  At Christmas time the Legion and Auxiliary have
a party with lunch, card playing, gift exchange and the singing of Christmas
carols.

The members of the Auxiliary are a group of women dedicated to the work of
helping the veteran in whatever way
possible, and it is their hope that this dedication will never
end.

THE BELLS OF CELEBRATION

A two-octave handbell set was given to St. John's Lutheran Church in 1981 in
memory of Arthur J. Haerter. During Bible School in June of 1982, a youth group
of ringers was organized by Arlene Herrboldt, who has directed since then.
Seven of the nine ringers started with the group.  They have performed at
community functions such as: Memorial Day service, Snow Queen Show, Senior
Citizen Home programs, Baccalaureate, a wedding, area church programs as well
as regular worship services and concerts in their own church.  Four of the
girls have played solos or duets.

They are a member of the American Guild of English Handbell Ringers thus being
invited to the National Festival in Ames, Iowa, in June, 1985.  Three members
and the director attended workshops there, learning new skills and techniques
and also playing in the massed choir.  In 1986, all the members but one
attended the Area VII Regional Festival at North Dakota State University in
Fargo, North Dakota.  At that time they played two numbers in concert and also
in the massed choir with 650 other ringers.  Susanne Walth, accompanied by
Karen Walth on the piano, played a solo in concert.

        Holly Hieb, Donna Schaible, Denise Haerter, Joleen Mohr, Becky Mohr,
        Karen Walth and Gina Hieb; (back) Arlene Herrboldt, Director.  A
        substitute ringer is Jessica Roesch.

BETHANY CEMETERY ASSOCIATION

The first meeting for the purpose of organizing a cemetery association to
combine the existing Reformed Gemeinde cemetery and the St. John's Lutheran
Gemeinde cemetery was held in Jakober's Store on May 6, 1930. Committee members
representing the Reformed Gemeinde were John Mehlhoff and Gottlieb Aman;
committee members representing the St. John's Lutheran Gemeinde were John
Heckenlaible, Christ Jakober and Emil Sonnenfeld.  At this meeting, plans were
made to form an association to be called the Bethany Cemetery Association, the
constitution and the by-laws were drafted, the results of this meeting were to
be presented to a joint meeting of the respective congregations at a later
meeting.  Also at this meeting, Mr. and Mrs. Fred Vilhauer offered to donate
lots 1 and 2 of block 14, Milwaukee Land Company, 2nd addition to the town of
Hosmer and this donation was gratefully accepted.

The first annual meeting with full representation from the Reformed Gemeinde
and the St. John's Lutheran Gemeinde was held on June 5, 1933, at the city
hall.  Rev. Builder acted as temporary chairman.  At this meeting the name of
the association, the constitution and the by-laws were adopted as presented by
the committee.  The Bethany Cemetery Association was then incorporated.  Money
was needed for the upkeep of the cemetery.  A resolution was adopted to assess
every member of each congregation $1.00 per year for this purpose.

By 1945, it became apparent that the cemetery needed to be enlarged.  Mr.
Rudolph Hieb, also recognizing this need and owning land adjacent to the
cemetery, donated lot 3 northeast 12-124-74 of the town of Hosmer, to the
cemetery association.

In 1956, a perpetual care fund was established.  This untested idea was to
create a permanent financial fund from which only the interest could be used
for the maintenance and upkeep of the cemetery.  The money to establish this
permanent fund was to be raised by assessment of each member of the respective
congregations, by the sale of burial lots and by donations.  At this writing
the perpetual care fund has proven to be successful, the fund is growing and
the idea was sound. As a result, the Bethany Cemetery will be maintained into
perpetuity.

In 1968, a new entrance to the cemetery was constructed. Permission was granted
to the family of Gottlieb J. Walth to erect this entrance as a memorial to Mr.
Walth for his long and dedicated service to the cemetery association.

Members of the joint congregations that served as committee members of the
association from 1930 through 1986 are as follows:
      John Heckenlaible                   Raymond Hieb
      Christ Jakober                      Edward Mehlhaff
      Emil Sonnenfeld                     Elmer Hieb

BUSY BELLES EXTENSION CLUB

The Busy Belles Extension Club was organized October 31, 1973.  Meetings were
to be held at the homes of club members on the second Monday of every month from
September through May.

First year members of the new club were: Edna Chase, Winnie Gab, Jeannie
Jackson, Katie Kappenman, Florence Malsam, Leona Malsam, Margie Malsam and
Gloria Salwei. Officers elected were Margie Malsam, chairperson; Katie
Kappenman, vice chairperson; Edna Chase, secretary and reporter; and Jeannie
Jackson, treasurer.

The 'Busy Belles' true to their club name, are active in a variety of
activities.  Once a year the club sews a quilt to be raffled as a fund raiser
and some of the proceeds are given to a worthy cause or organization selected
by the group.  Other yearly activities include entertaining at the Senior
Citizens Home, skip day, men's guest night, family picnic, Christmas party, and
if requested to do so the club will also prepare and serve a meal for an
organization.  Crafts and needlework skills are the clubs main projects.  They
have also taken part in educational study projects such as family life and
health, foreign culture, and many other social living related topics.

During the time of the club's existence, several members have left the
community and the club, but were soon replaced by new members.  In 1986, the
club remains strong and active with 11 members.  The present members are Kathy
Aman, president; Pauline Aman, vice president; Virginia Aman, secretary;
Gerilyn Malsom, treasurer; Leona Malsam, historian; Edna Chase, Martha
Kappenman, Vera Eberhart, Rose Malsam, Kathy Docktor, and Winne Gab.

HIGHLIGHTERS EXTENSION CLUB

The Highlighters Extension Club was organized on August 8, 1968, at the home of
Jessie Lovo with the help of Eleanore Krokosh, Home Extension Agent.  The first
officers were: Chairperson, Emmay Mayer; Vice Chairperson, Fay Anderson;
Secretary-Treasurer, Karen Neifer; -Historian, Lea Geffre; Reporter, JoAnn
Bieber.

We began with 13 members and have 12 members today. Two of the present members,
Ella Neifer and Karen Neifer, are charter members.

The club participates in the county lessons and workshops.  We have shown
movies at the local school on drugs and the harm they can do.  Several members
have taken the CPR class.

We support the 4-H, entertain yearly at the Senior Citizen's Home, have a
family Christmas party and family picnics.

The club participates in the County Holiday Fairs, and donates to Susan Wilder
and International Funds.  We have served at auction sales, entered floats in
Hosmer and Roscoe parades, and visited the Dakota Prairie Museum as a group.

Present officers are: Chairperson, Virginia Hieb; Vice Chairperson, Doris
Vilhauer; Secretary and Reporter, Eva Schmidt; Treasurer, Ella Neifer;
Historian, Milna Fauth. Additional members are: Vickie Forkel, Vera Jung, Sharon
Hieb, Jeannette Lunders, Marcella Mohr, Karen Neifer and Sonja Anderson.

HOSMER HOMEMAKERS

The first meeting of the Hosmer Homemakers Extension Club was held April 9,
1942, at the home of Mrs. Elmer Hieb. This was an organizational meeting at
which the first officers were elected.  They were: Chairwoman, Mrs. Elmer Hieb;
Secretary, Mrs. Walter Grossman; Treasurer, Mrs. Emanuel Hauck.  The first
members included: Mrs. Jacob Dockter, Mrs. August Dockter, Mrs. John Gisi, Mrs.
Walter Grossmann, Mrs. Elmer Hieb, Mrs. Emanuel Hauck, Mrs. Sam Job, Mrs.
Edward Kiesz, Mrs. Gust Koerner, Mrs. Willard Loudon, Mrs. Albert Schlaht, Mrs.
Ray Stewart, and Mrs. Ed.  C. Ulmer.

Some of the projects the club has carried out over the years are: a supervised
playground at school for the young children during the summer of 1949; teas to
honor the senior high school girls; sponsored the Girl's 4-H Clubs, giving
Christmas gifts to the Redfield State Hospital; initiation of the hot-lunch
program at school and new school stage draperies; money-raising projects to aid
in the equipping of the city park; sponsored a quilt showing; planted and cared
for flowers in the park; entertained annually at the Senior Citizen's Home;
made donations toward the Hearing-Dog Program; made donations to the School of
Hope; and the final project was to make a candlewicking quilt which was given
to the Centennial Committee to be used as a fund raiser.

In 1979, the Hosmer Homemakers Extension Club dropped their affiliation with
the County Extension Service and at that time changed their name to the Hosmer
Homemakers. From that time on the individual club members were responsible for
the lessons.

Ellen Hieb and Martha Job were charter members of the club and have continued
their membership to date.
