The Hosmer Area Right to Life was formed in November, 1984, to protect the
lives of those who cannot speak for themselves--the unborn, the newborn, and
the long born.

The first officers elected were Mr. and Mrs. Sam Allison, co-chairpersons; Mrs.
Dallas (Sonja) Anderson, secretary; and Mrs. Arnold (Jeanette) Lunders,
treasurer.

HOSMER CIVIC CLUB HISTORY

The Hosmer Civic Club was organized under the name Hosmer Commercial Club in
May, 1920.  The purpose of this organization was to work for the betterment of
the Hosmer Community.

The Commercial Club became inactive in 1922 and was reorganized in 1927 as the
Hosmer Civic Club and has been actively involved in the community ever since.
Membership in this organization was comprised of Hosmer businessmen and at one
juncture the club had a total of 65 members.  The organization invited farmers
and other community people to meetings and encouraged them to become members.
A Ladies Night was an annual event but it wasn't until November, 1975, that the
club went on record to invite female business people to its membership role.
This evidently was a wise move for the following year, December, 1976, the
wives were also invited to become members.  At present the club's membership
totals 45.

Whenever there was a community or citizens need, the club worked to meet those
needs.  Much was achieved through appointed committees.  It was through various
committees that many important projects were initiated and because of members'
hard work and devotion to the cause projects were completed.

The Hosmer Civic Club was instrumental in securing proper drainage for city
streets, a hard-surface road to Highway 12, in 1972 black-topping of all
streets, and in 1976 hard-surface roads east and west of Hosmer.

The City Park was another project which originated in a Civic Club Committee.
Most of the, monies and labor required were donated by various city clubs,
businesses and individuals.  The entire park was sodded with volunteer help.  In
1981, the club replaced many of the trees in the park.  Due to disease, 220
trees needed to be replanted and the necessary labor was donated by the members.

After conducting a survey, the Nursing Home Committee Chairman, Albert Haerter,
reported the need for a senior citizens home to service the area and benefit
the community. Through the club's efforts in 1964, the Senior Citizen's Home
became a reality and still contributes enormously to the community today.

Since senior citizens are one of the community's great assets, the club again
took action and helped organize a Senior Citizen's Center which is located next
to the Legion Hall.

Two main housing projects to benefit citizens were the initial work of the
Housing Committee Chairmen, John Haerter and Dennis Hollan.  One low-rental
housing unit was completed in May, 1974, and the second project, Somerset, was
completed in May, 1980.  These two housing developments add greatly to the
community's appearance and help meet the needs of the citizens.

The youth of the community are an ever present concern of the club and for this
cause the Improvement Committee Chairman, Dennis Hollan, was successful in
obtaining a $4,000.00 grant for the construction of a tennis court completed in
1975.

In 1976, the club donated funds for lights for the softball field and in 1981
money was donated for a basketball court located on the east side of the
elementary school.

A city skating rink was a re-occurring topic of concern for the club.  Many a
meeting was spent discussing the "Rink".  In past years the rink did provide
enjoyable sport for the youth of the community but because of liability,
maintenance, warm winters and numerous other problems the rink remains a
nemesis in the club's history.

One of the main fund raisers for the club has been the Farm and Home Show.
This annual March event was organized by Dennis Hollan in 1977 and has proved
to be an added attraction for the city of Hosmer.  Dennis has served as
chairman for this event since its inception.  Because of the income generated
by the Farm and Home Show, the club has been able to donate substantially to
the celebration fund, purchase chairs for the school, donate to the renovation
of the 102 Civic Center and to the Medical Clinic.

In 1979, Dankfest was born - an appreciation day initiated by the club for the
community.  Dankfest originated in the Civic Club and began with Turkey Day, a
meal served to the community followed by a social hour and dance.  At present
it has evolved into a day of community appreciation held in the city park every
summer.

Promotion of Hosmer has always been an objective for the group and in 1964 the
club purchased signs promoting Hosmer which can be seen on Highway 12 and
Highway 47 greeting motorists with a big "Hello from Hosmer".  In 1981, a large
banner was purchased, by the club.  This banner proclaiming "Welcome to Hosmer"
hangs on Main Street during all community celebrations.

In 1980, the Board of Directors for Medical Services was chosen: President,
John Haerter, Arnie Pietz, Dennis Hollan, and Jere Hieb.  The Board was
instrumental in the creation of the Hosmer Medical Clinic.  Open House at the
clinic was held in November, 1981.  Arlene Heckenlaible donated the furniture
and floor covering for the waiting room.  Dr. John Ottenbacher was the first
doctor to provide services to the community in the new clinic.  The Medical
Clinic is housed in the former Hosmer Theater.

In 1983, the 102 Ballroom was purchased by the American Legion and they in turn
gave it to the Civic Club. Since then the name has been changed to the 102 Civic
Center.  The club plans to complete renovation of the center in time for the
Centennial Celebration in 1987.  Work on the Center was begun in October, 1985.

The Industrial Development Committee, Arnie Pietz, John Jacobs, Emma
Feyereisen, and John Eisenbeisz are working to bring industry to the community.
 Through their efforts Hosmer now has a dentist who serves the community one
day a week.

In addition to all of the aforementioned projects, the club sponsors Santa Day
for the kiddies which includes a free movie, treats and a visit from Santa
Claus.  Another important annual event is the Snow Queen Contest.  The Club has
held an annual contest for the past 31 years.

Past presidents of the Civic Club who still reside in the community are as
follows: John Haerter 1965, 1970, 1979; Arnold Herrboldt 1966; Lyle Clopine
1967; Elmer Hieb 1971; Ronald Hieb 1972; Dennis Hollan 1973, 1982; Jere Hieb
1980; Arnold Wiederrich 1984, 1985.

HOSMER MEN'S SOFTBALL

Softball in Hosmer got its start through the efforts of Charles Coan, a past
resident of the Hosmer Senior Citizen's Home.  In the summer of 1968, Charles
collected enough money to buy a bat and ball.  He then asked anyone that was
interested in choosing sides to come to the park on a Sunday evening.  After
several weeks of Sunday evening play, someone suggested they play other towns.
One thing led to another and a Yellowstone Trail Softball Conference was
started.  The team moved from the park to the Legion baseball diamond,
shortened the fence, and lights were installed in 1974 through a community
effort.  The team has won several league championships and placed in many
invitational tournaments.

        Troy Heib, Roger Malsam, Charles Mohr, Pat Gisi. Missing are: Peter
        Eisenbeisz, Eloy Heier, Jeff Aman, Clyde Mohr.

HOSMER SENIOR CENTER

The first meeting in the interest of organizing a center for the Senior
Citizens in Hosmer was held in the city hall in April, 1973.  It was conducted
by Marian Skarnagel of Sisseton.  Following a discussion, a decision was made to
organize and Anna Aman and Ida Rueb were appointed as co-directors.  A
membership drive resulted with sixty members.

The name Hosmer Area Senior Center was chosen, a constitution was written, the
Legion Hall was rented and in July the first meeting was held.  The officers
elected were: Rev.  Roesler, president; Ida Rueb, vice president; John
Stoecker, secretary; and Anna Aman, treasurer.  The regular routine included
monthly meetings, potluck dinner, two bus trips to Aberdeen each month, and
various other activities.

In July, 1975, the organization had the opportunity to acquire its own
building.  What was once the Golden Eagle, became the Senior Center.  The
renovation took much planning and much hard labor and money, but thanks to some
willing and capable volunteers, the cost was kept within means and the results
were very satisfactory.  Some modernization took place later with electric heat
replacing a space heater, the floor was carpeted, a pool table was acquired and
various replacements were made.  The Senior Citizens now have a pleasant and
convenient place to congregate.

The present membership is III and the officers are Rupert Ulmer, president;
Albert Stoecker, vice president; Emma Martell, secretary; and Magdalena Mohr,
treasurer.

HOSMER SPORTSMEN'S CLUB

The Hosmer Sportsmen's Club was originated in 1953 when the citizens of Hosmer
felt a group was needed to develop a lake area 10 miles west of Hosmer, which
later was named the Hosmer-Bowdle Lake.  The first membership had 125 members.
Several years later the organization became incorporated.  The first years were
spent developing the Hosmer-Bowdle Lake Area.  This project was a huge success
as in 1954 over 1,200 people attended a fishing derby held at the lake which
was sponsored by the club.  The lake remained popular for many years for
picnickers and fishermen, and until recent drought conditions held an ample
water level for a good fish population.  After the drought, the lake area has
turned into an excellent and popular hunting area, hosting an ample supply of
pheasants, deer, and sometimes, ducks.

In 1955, the club built the area's first trap shoot which is located 1 1/2
miles west of Hosmer.  Today, this trap shoot is being maintained by the
current club and is fully functional.

In the early years, the club sponsored an annual hunter's safety course for all
youngsters.  One of the highlights of the year was the Annual Buffalo Feed.

After many successful years, interest in the club waned to the point where it
became inactive.

In 1979, a group of people felt the time was ripe to reactivate the club.
Currently the club consists of 29 active members.

The year is always started with a supper of game feed for all members prepared
by Hosmer's Busy Belles Club.  One of the most popular and important activities
is the Annual Smoker held every year on the Wednesday before Thanksgiving. This
event has grown in popularity throughout the Edmunds and McPherson Counties.
The Sportsmen's Club works with several other organizations in hosting this
well-attended gala.  Guests enjoy free deep-fried chicken wings, smelt, and
gizzards while participating in many enjoyable games.  Many fresh, locally
raised geese, ducks, and turkeys are raffled.

The money raised by the Sportsmen's Club at the Smokers, dues and other
activities are donated to various needs, some of which are: the Eureka Lake,
Thomas Bay on Lake Oahe for boat ramps, a concession stand at the Hosmer
Softball Complex, and a basketball court at the Hosmer school.  Presently, they
are erecting a community bulletin board.

Other activities enjoyed by the members are the smelt catch at Lake Oahe, the
annual smelt fry, and occasional fishing derbies held at local lakes.  The club
also sponsors two youths to attend the yearly State Conservation Camp held in
the Black Hills area.

The members of the club work closely with local Conservation Officer, Dick
Marko, in supplying information needed to set hunting seasons for the area.
Also, the club works with area farmers to help in controlling predators.

It is understood that the prime objective of a Hosmer Sportsmen's Club member
is to be a good sportsman.

MOONLIGHTERS BIRTHDAY CLUB

In 1967, the Mixed Rose Birthday Club was organized by Mrs. Harold (Winifred)
Ehresman with 8 to 10 ladies as members.  After some time it was agreed that
the husbands be included as guests.  The name was then changed to the
Moonlighters Birthday Club

In 1983, the club returned to only having women as members.  As of January,
1986, there are 10 members: Erna Aman, Maggie Bieber, Gladys Gramm, Martha
Harty, Tina Hieb, Alma Martell, Emma Martell, Anna Mohr, Matilda Rohrbach and
Viola Weishaar.

When a member has a birthday, the second Monday of that month is designated for
the club to get together.  Bingo and various games are played.  To end the
evening, gifts are exchanged and a lunch is served by the hostess.

NORTH STAR 4-H CLUB

The North Star 4-H Club was organized in 1976 at the Darwin Ulmer home with
County Agent, Herb Brandner, present to explain the 4-H program to the group.

The following area youth have been members of the club: Patrick Forkel, Kristi
Forkel, Audrey Hieb, Rosina Hieb, Robin Jung, Tera Ulmer, Kris Ulmer, Brad
Ulmer, Kim Preszler, Stuart Wiederrich, and Laura Wiederrich.

The present four club members are: Sheila and Rebecca Sandmeier, daughters of
Roland and Sharon Sandmeier; Holly Hieb, daughter of Willard and Sharon Hieb;
and Tricia Ulmer, daughter of Darwin and Kay Ulmer.

Main projects of the club have been arts and crafts, dairy , foods and
nutrition,  gardening , clothing , and photography.  The club has been active
in safety, community service, and citizenship projects.  Members are also
active in 4-H judging areas and all members have been on county judging teams
at the State Fair.

Rosina Hieb won a 4-H trip by placing 4th high individual in judging arts and
crafts at the State Fair.  Kristi Forkel received recognition on the county,
state and national level in the National Poster Art Contest.  Holly Hieb was
awarded a plaque for winning first place at the State Fair Junior Judging Home
Environment.  Older members are active in the county 4-H Council and have
attended club conference at Brookings.  Patrick Forkel, Brad Ulmer, and Kristi
Forkel attended Washington Focus in Washington, D.C. They traveled to
Washington for a week of citizenship training and touring historical sites.

Community service projects of the club include entertaining at the Hosmer
Senior Citizen's Home and making favors for the residents, singing songs, and
playing bingo. The club has also sponsored an annual Easter Egg Hunt which is
held each spring at the Hosmer City Park.  This annual event is well attended
and the children enjoy finding eggs and receiving Easter treats.

HISTORY OF THE YOUNG AND THE RESTLESS 4-H

The Young and the Restless 4-H was organized on December 2, 1977.  The charter
members were: Dawn and Kari Aman, Amy and Janet Gisi, Cindy Gisi, Denise
Haerter, Susan Lindner, Julie Malsam, Joleen and Pauline Mohr, and Paulette
Vetch, with Berniece Mohr as leader.

The Young and the Restless 4-H Club has always tried to make the best better by
working for their club, community, county, and country.  Their members have
received many county and state awards.

The present members are: Amy Gisi, Denise Haerter, Liza Heier, Carolee Malsam,
Jayne and Sarah Mohr, Joleen Mohr, and Holly Weishaar, with Berniece Mohr still
as their leader.

SENIOR CITIZEN'S HOME AUXILIARY

In July, 1966, the first official meeting of the Senior Citizen's Home
Auxiliary was held with 60 members present. Mrs. Ruby Prafke, Administrator of
the Nursing Home, conducted the meeting and explained the purpose and duties of
an auxiliary.  The first elected officers were: Rose (Albert) Gross,
Chairperson; Amy (Wilmer) Heckenlaible, Vice Chairperson; Maggie (Julius) Mohr,
Secretary; Clara (Val) Krumm, Treasurer.  It was decided to have a $1.00 yearly
membership fee, and to meet the fourth Monday of each month.

Volunteer committees are: Hobby - responsible to bring and direct the making of
items to sell; Entertainment - responsible for Sunday afternoon entertainment,
various games for the residents, outings, bingo, birthday and Christmas
parties; Mending - responsible for mending the resident's clothing as needed;
Friendship - responsible for sending birthday,. get-well and sympathy cards;
Flowers - responsible for planting the beautiful flowers out front. These
committees were called upon to fill the needs of the Home until the Home could
afford to hire laundry and activity personnel.  Throughout the years the
auxiliary has purchased and donated various items needed within the Home.

As the membership decreased and rising costs increased, the auxiliary decided
to have a booth at the annual Hosmer Farm and Home Show to raise funds to help
defray expenses. The membership dues were raised to $2.00 in 1986.

Present officers (1986) of the auxiliary are: Emma (Walter) Martell, President;
Arlene (Arnold) Herrboldt, Vice President; Tamara (Donald) Neifer, Secretary;
Phyllis (Herbert) Hieb, Treasurer; Orma (Rupert) Ulmer, Friendship; Emma
(Walter) Martell, Historian.  Other members of the auxiliary are: Anna Aman,
Erna Aman, Maggie Bieber, Mariam Eisenbeisz, Florence Gisi, Arvina Goehring,
Judy Grismer, Margaret Heier, Alita Heyne, Ellen Hieb, Tina Hieb, Naomi Hieb,
Martha Job, Dorothy Jung, Leona Malsam, Alma Martell, Selma Mohr, Magdalena
Mohr, Anna Mohr, Maggie Pietz, Ida Rueb, Carol Ulmer, Lorna Ulmer, Viola
Weishaar, Connie Woehlhaff, Irene Brockman, Martha Halverson, Mary Margaret
Jacobs, Ruth Spicer.

                        SCHOOLS

HISTORY OF THE HOSMER PUBLIC SCHOOLS

One fall day in the year of 1888, a new sound broke the stillness of the crisp
autumn air in the little village of Hosmer, South Dakota.  It was a lovely
sound for many people, though perhaps a little fearful for some of the younger
ones - the ringing of a bell signifying the first day of school in the brand
new one-room school building.

        Typical one-room schoolhouse of yesteryear

The actual wheels of organization leading to the erection of the school had
been set in motion some months earlier at a meeting held in Ipswich, SD.  At a
regular county commissioners' meeting held Jan. 3, 1888, a petition for the
organization of Hosmer School District Number 7 had been presented and a motion
had been made and carried ordering  a local meeting of organization to be held
at the home of L. Williams.  Mr. Williams had been ordered to post notices and
to have charge of the meeting.  At a later commissioners' meeting held in
Ipswich, SD, on April 2, 1888, a motion was made and carried approving the
formal organization of the Hosmer district and ordering a meeting for such
organization to be held on April 11, 1888, at McKoane's store in Hosmer, SD.
Jonathan Williams was appointed to post notices and to have charge of the
meeting.  This date of April 11, 1888, then, marks the actual beginning of the
Hosmer Public School System.

On May 13, 1888, the district was bonded to the amount of $100 for a one-room
school house to provide educational facilities for the children of the
community.  The first apportionment of $15.95 was received in July, 1888; and
another of $20 in Oct. of the same year, from J. J. Skahen, County
Superintendent of Schools, to Charles Gipple, treasurer of the new Hosmer
School District.  This apportionment was for 29 pupils.

The ringing of that first school bell must have caused a good deal of
excitement - excitement such as we who today take all our educational
opportunities so much for granted, can scarcely imagine.  Many of the children
who were to attend the school had never been in an American school of any kind
and lacked the fundamentals which we consider essential to the learning of the
"A.B.C.'s". In this connection it seems appropriate to mention the great
courage which one particular group of young men and women who pioneered on
these prairies of the Dakotas must have possessed - the public school teachers
of the pioneer days surely deserve special mention in the pages of our history.
They must have encountered seemingly insurmountable difficulties.  Take John
E. Rarrick, the first teacher of the Hosmer Public Schools, as an example.  He
had before him 29 pupils in need of instructions; but there were no clearly
defined classes, there were no suitable textbooks, and, as if that were not
enough, many of his pupils had absolutely no knowledge of the English language.
Others of the courageous group who are worthy of special mention are: Arthur
McCafferty, Nettie Elrige, Mary Pitz, and Sarah McAssey.

It is also notable that since there was no law for compulsory education during
these early years, many pupils of school age did not attend school at all, and
many others attended only a few days out of the year.  However, in 1891, a law
was finally passed stating that every person who had a child between the ages
of eight and fourteen years should have such child attend a public school at
least twelve weeks of the year.  The chairman of the board was to see that these
children attended as stated.  No child of school age could be employed while
school was in session unless a permit should be obtained from the chairman of
the board.

The school board at that time consisted of three members: the chairman, the
treasurer and the clerk.  In the first record obtainable of the Hosmer district
the following members are listed: Karl Hoffart, chairman; Leonard Muenz,
treasurer; John Haerter, clerk.

As more settlers arrived and the community grew, it was found necessary to
expand the facilities to accommodate the rural children of the community.  Thus
three rural houses were added to the Hosmer district in the year 1898.  At a
regular school board meeting held on July 9, 1901, as recorded by clerk John
Haerter, the board levied $1100 for school purposes on the taxable school
property of Hosmer School District Number 7. It was also decided at this
meeting that school would be held for seven months a year in the town school
and for six months in the rural schools. (This custom of holding school for a
longer period in the town schools, oddly enough, continued for some time though
the time was in later years extended to nine and eight months.) The salary for
town teachers was set at $35 and for rural teachers at $30.  The school census
of that time showed 121 children over six years of age and under twenty-one.
Of these, 95 were attending school.

On June 23, 1904, the rural school west of Hosmer was struck by lightning and
burned to the ground.  An increase in both interest and enrollment prompted the
thought that a new school building was needed in town, as the old one-room
building still in use had more than served its purpose. Therefore, the board
agreed to move the town school to the west rural district to replace their
burned building and to build a new and larger schoolhouse in the village of
Hosmer.

On August 2, 1904, therefore, an election was held to bond the district to the
amount of $1,000 for a new, two-room schoolhouse.  The building which was
erected with this money served our district until April 19, 1915, when,
enrollment again having increased to the point where the building could no
longer accommodate it, the voters again went to the polls to determine whether
they would support a $10,000 bond issue for the purpose of building a new and
better school.  The results showed that 54 of the qualified voters of the
district favored the issue and only 7 were against it.  The resulting brick
structure, built in block I of the Heckenlaible 2nd addition of Hosmer, still
serves our community today as the Hosmer Grade School.  The school board which
was responsible for this improvement was John A. Peterson, chairman; Christ
Jakober, clerk; and John Heckenlaible, treasurer.

At this time and for a number of years afterward, the district was unable to
offer its children more than a grade-school education.  Some of those desirous
of obtaining a secondary education were able to do so by attending school in
other towns, such as Eureka or Aberdeen; but others, less fortunate, were
unable to secure more than an eighth grade education.  In 1927, the district
felt that this situation could be remedied and on April 19th of that year the
citizens of the community voted on a $25,000 bond issue for the purpose of
constructing a high school building in our town, so that all children might
have an equal chance to obtain a high school diploma.

September 3, 1927, had been set as the opening date for the new school; but,
since the building was not finished by that date, high school classes were
begun in the building known as the Hosmer Hotel. (This building was located just
south of the site of the present post office and was later sold and demolished,
the lumber being used for farm buildings.) The following people were serving on
the school board when the high school was built: John Heckenlaible, chairman;
Jacob C. Schaible, clerk; Emil Sonnenfeld, treasurer; Christ Jakober, Anton G.
Malsam, Anton Hopfinger and John G. Kranzler.  Note that by this time the
school board consisted of seven members.  The first high school superintendent
was Mr. Ben Simmons.  The first graduation class in the spring of 1928 of the
new school consisted of one member - Mr. Otto Streeb.

During the time which has elapsed between the graduation of that first student
and the present day, many changes have taken place in our high school
organization.  These are mostly concerned with expansion and enrichment of the
curriculum, as it has always been our policy to try to offer the students as
many advantages as a school the size of ours could reasonably do.  From a
three-teacher high school it has developed into an eight-teacher school, with a
principal as well as a superintendent.  From a schedule which placed emphasis
on business and commercial courses, it has expanded its program to include
liberal arts and science and has added an industrial arts department and most
recently computers. Consideration of the fact that a larger percentage of
graduates each year will attend college has made it necessary to provide a more
diversified course of study to satisfy the needs of all students.

As the curriculum developed the extracurricular program was added and expanded
correspondingly.  One of these extracurricular activities was the development
of the musical talent that flourishes in this community.  Our school is justly
proud of some of the honors which it has received from participation in
extracurricular activities.  The whole town of Hosmer was proud when, in 1937,
under the direction of Mr. Alfred Heckenlaible, our school band, still a
comparatively young organization at that time, won a superior rating in Class C
at the State Music Contest.  The music department ever since then has entered
many contests and has returned from the contest many times with a superior
rating not only for the band but also in chorus.  The marching band, attired in
handsome new uniforms, participates in Gypsy Day parades and also neighborhood
homecoming parades.

Of all the sports in which our students participate, basketball is still the
favorite of the local sports fans. Honors which we have won in this field
include Conference Championship in 1942 under the direction of Coach Bob Lenihan
and again in 1953 under Coach Don Talcott.  In 1982 under the leadership of Lew
Paulson the Tigers were conference and district champions and runner-up at the
Regional tournament. Although our school has participated in track meets for
many years, these events have been emphasized much more in recent years.  In
the year 1959 Hosmer High added football to its athletic schedule, but it has
been dropped because of lack of participation.  A co-op has been formed with
the Roscoe school where a few football players participate.  In recent years
girls' basketball has been added, giving girls the opportunity to participate
in sports.

In the 1940's inadequate housing facilities in our town were making it
increasingly difficult to hire a superintendent for our school system.  For
this reason the board of education purchased the Fred Eisenbeisz home with the
intention of keeping it available to superintendents.  As this housing problem
also affected other married teachers, the board decided to buy more houses to
rent to teachers and, in carrying out this plan, bought the Friedericka
Albrecht home in 1960 and the Rosina Job home in 1961.  In recent years this
situation has changed with the coming of mobile homes.  Teachers also car pool
and commute.  Only one of the three homes mentioned is occupied.  The other two
houses were sold.

At a meeting held on March 29, 1954, the school board decided that the rural
children of the district should be brought in to the town school that they
might have opportunities equal to those offered to town children - these
included instruction by teachers holding State certificates and participation
in music and athletic events, particularly in band and basketball.  In acting
upon the decision the board closed the Malsam and the Heier schools immediately
and the Grismer school at a later date.  The district contracted two buses from
Pietz and Walth to transport the rural children to town school.

In the year 1956 Sangamon township closed the three schools belonging to their
district and made arrangements with the local board to bring their children to
school in our district.  Two buses were again contracted, this time from
Schlaht and Job, to transport the Sangamon children.

Meantime, on September 13, 1954, a hot lunch program sponsored by the school
and aided by the state had begun to function with Mrs. Paul Kunde and Miss
Alice Ulmer employed as cooks.  Naturally this had a direct bearing on the
large number of rural children attending town school, as it provided them with
a warm meal at noontime which they would not otherwise have.

The large number of rural children now attending town school caused the
enrollment to be increased to such an extent that the old elementary building
could no longer accommodate all the grade classes.  For this reason, the board,
in 1957, secured the services of architect Marion E. Smith to draw plans for an
addition to our school.  The resulting structure was the brick building which
now connects the original elementary and high school buildings.  This was built
at a cost of $25,000 by Jacob Mohr and Sons in the summer of 1957. As
originally planned, it consisted of a kitchen and dining hall in the basement,
a band room on the first floor and two high school classrooms on the second
floor.  The steady increase in enrollment of elementary students due to later
reorganization of school districts made it necessary to convert the band room
into two grade school classrooms and to turn one of the high school classrooms
over to the elementary school.  Also, the shop equipment was moved to the
basement of the industrial arts building so that grade school classes might be
held on the main floor of that building.  This industrial arts building or shop
as it was more commonly called, was not a new structure, but was made up of two
rural school buildings which had been moved to town and set up west of the main
school building.  The following people were serving on the school board when
these improvements were made: Albert J. Haerter, chairman; Amy Heckenlaible,
clerk; Albert Schlaht, treasurer, Elmer Hieb, H. W. Melhop, Frank Grismer and
Nick G. Malsam.

A very important milestone in the history of our school was passed on February
9, 1960.  Prior to this time the boards of reorganization of Edmunds and
McPherson Counties had devised a master plan for the reorganization of school
districts within their boundaries.  On that momentous day voters in the
districts involved went to the polls to decide whether their children should
attend Hosmer School District No. 37, as it now is.  Districts concerned were
as follows: Hosmer Independent School District No. 7, Edmunds County; Sangamon
Common School District No. 6, Edmunds County; the North-half in Lake Common
School District No. 8, Edmunds County; Sections 4 and 9 in Cottonwood Lake
Common School District No. 10, Edmunds County; the two-thirds in Bryant Common
School District No. 3 and 4, McPherson County; Cleveland Common School District
No. 1, 2, 3, and 4, McPherson County; Blaine Common School District No. 1, 2,
3, and 4, McPherson County; Sections 22, 27 and 34 in L. and M. Common School
District No. 10, McPherson County; Moscow Common School District No. 13,
McPherson County; District No. 2, McPherson County. Of these districts, all
voted into our new district except the Cleveland Common School District No. 2
and Sections 22, 27, and 34 in L. and M. Common School District No. 10.
Cleveland No. 2 has since petitioned into the new district.

This absorption of rural districts increased the enrollment of our school to 86
high school and 236 elementary students.  The board of education was now able
to employ one teacher for each grade.  One or two grades had to be divided from
time to time when the enrollment for one room was too great.  Also, they now
hire seven high school instructors in addition to the superintendent.  This
enables the school to offer all the necessary courses and meet other
requirements of the State Department of Public Instruction to become 'First-
Class Accredited.'

With the increased enrollment came a need for more school facilities.  Plans
for the construction of a $200,000 building program were put into motion and on
June 20, 1961, the necessary bond issue was approved by a vote of 314 to 135.
This new building now houses a new and larger gymnasium, a music room and
additional classrooms for the high school students.  The members of the school
board at the time of these additions were: Rupert Ulmer, chairman; Amy
Heckenlaible, clerk; Royal Stabbe, treasurer; Albert Haerter, Melvin Jung,
Eugene Graff and Arnold Pietz.  In the fall of 1962 the construction of this
new project was begun and was completed and ready for use by December, 1963.
This new addition to the school facilities now made more room for the
elementary classes which had by now become overcrowded. Kindergarten classes
could now be added along with special classes such as remedial reading,
remedial math and speech therapy.  Large classes in the lower grades were
divided giving the students a better opportunity for a first-rate education.

Instead of contracting school buses the district board decided to own all
buses.  During the summer of 1965 a new eight stall bus storage shed was
constructed making room for storing seven buses plus a heated service shop.  A
very smooth and efficient running school system was now in operation and has
been operating well despite the fact that declining farm population, poor farm
economy and dropping school enrollments are all beginning to take their toll.
In recent years, grades have been combined again and the faculty has been
reduced.

Many young men and women have graduated from Hosmer School since that long-ago
fall day in 1888 when the courageous Mr. John Rarrick sat before his little
group of 29 students in the little one-room wooden building.  Some have gone on
to pursue a higher education in the colleges of our state and other states.
Some have entered their careers immediately upon graduation while others have
had to further their knowledge in the school of experience.  Many have returned
to Hosmer to live their lives, marry, and raise their children to attend the
old alma mater.

At this time of our centennial 928 students will have graduated from the Hosmer
High School, 58 of these have already passed away.  During this 100 year span
the grade school starting with an enrollment of 29 students peaked at 248
students in the year of 1963, while the high school having a beginning with 31
students enrolled, peaked at 108 students in the year 1971.  The current
enrollment stands at 50 students but will drop quite a bit after the 1987 class
of 20 students graduate.  The elementary enrollment stands at 71 students.  It
may be interesting to note of how many things and numbers have changed
throughout the years.  Salaries paid started at $30 per month, today the
monthly salary is at $1100 to $1200. When electric lights were installed in the
school house in 1920, the electricity bill was $1.50 per month, today the
average monthly bill is $735.  Telephone service was also installed during the
1920's with the service bill being $1 per month.  Today the average monthly
telephone bill is $145.  The first superintendent that was hired by this school
system was paid $1700 per year.  During the mid 30's when the Depression was at
its worst, that salary was reduced to $1200 per year.  After the economy began
to improve this was gradually raised throughout the years until today's figure
reads $24,000.  Many changes have taken place throughout the years and no doubt
many more will take place.  What the future holds for our school system remains
to be seen.

In 1977, the position of clerk and treasurer was combined into one position
known as the business manager.  Amy Heckenlaible, having been clerk for many
years became the first business manager.  She served in this capacity until she
retired in 1981.  Jacalyn Fauth Uhrich then became the business manager and is
at the present time.

The present board of education consists of Raymond Jung, Roland Sandmeier,
Berniece Mohr, Maurice Aman, and Peter Heier.

REMINISCENCES OF JOHN NEIFER

The following is an excerpt from an article written by John Neifer about his
early boyhood.  Mr. Neifer was born in Bon Homme County, Menno, South Dakota,
on August 10, 1885, and died in Hosmer at the age of 97 years.  He spent most
of his life in the Hosmer area.

My mother came to this country from Russia in the year 1872 with her parents,
Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Wruck.  Yankton, South Dakota, was the end of the railroad,
so they moved 28 miles northwest and homesteaded.  In the spring of 1873, my
grandfather went to Scotland, South Dakota, a stage driving station, and hauled
manure for 500 a day.

I can remember when the worst blizzard in history hit South Dakota on January
12, 1888.  In the northern part of the state it was not so severe as it was in
the southern part.  In the southern part of the state in the Scotland and Tripp
area, it arrived about noon.  Several people lost their lives in this blizzard
and many became handicapped.  At the age of six years, I saw a man who, in the
blizzard, had frozen his hands and feet so badly they had to be amputated.  I
saw him walking on his knees.  He had a four inch board buckled with leather
straps to each knee so he could walk.  There were many such incidents that are
almost unbelievable if one had not seen them.

In the same year, on April first, the town of Leola burned down with the
exception of two buildings and the courthouse. I can remember this very
distinctly since I was only a boy living at Leola with my parents, one brother
and a sister, when this happened.  We, too, lost our home, and all we had left
were the clothes that we were wearing.  We spent that night in the Courthouse
since there was no other place to go. My uncles came to Leola by wagon and a
good team of horses to take us to their farm for about two weeks.  Then my
father moved us to Eureka where he established a home for us.  He became a well
driller.  In the summer of 1888, my sister died. On October 10, 1890, my father
died after being ill with pneumonia for 2 weeks.  My mother took the children
and went to make our home with an aunt and uncle, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob Gross.  In
the fall of the next year, my aunt went to Scotland to visit some relatives and
took me with her.  She returned home leaving me with my grandparents, Jacob and
Katherina Wruck.  I remained with them for eight years.

In 1900, at the age of 15, I returned to the vicinity of Hosmer.  I got a job
working for John Ehresman with whom I remained for seven years.  Then I
returned to my uncle, Jacob Gross, and worked two years for him.

In the year 1908, on October 27, I was united in marriage to Rosina Hieb.  In
the fall of 1909, we moved to Lemmon, South Dakota, in Perkins County where we
endured a drought and were unable to raise crops and animals to make a living,
so we decided to come back.  We made our home in Rosette Township about 8 miles
north of Ipswich until 1916. From here we moved to a place 14 miles east of
Hosmer where we established a farm.  We moved a small house there and started
from "scratch." We lived there and raised a family of 9 children.  In 1948, we
moved to Hosmer until ill health forced us to move to the Senior Citizen's Home
in 1976.  My wife died on March 9, 1977, after 68 years of married life.

I have seen many changes in farming in my life.  The first year my grandfather
was in America, he harvested his crop with a cradle.  The next year he plowed
up some more prairie and that year a neighbor cut it with a Reaper that laid it
in bunches.  After that the Harvester came out, but with no binder head, so my
mother and her brother and sister stood on it and caught the grain, tied it
with the straw, and made a band-tied bundle.  My mother walked barefooted while
doing this.

After harvest time the grain had to be stacked up.  Those good old days we
worked all day, early in the morning 'til late in the evening.  We didn't get
chaff in our shoes for we either walked barefooted or wore high cut shoes.
After the stacking was done, the threshing started in about 2 weeks.

The threshing started with horse-powered machines.  In 1891, the headers began
to come into this northern part of South Dakota.  In the year 1914, the self
binder came in use again whenever there was a good crop standing on fields.
This was done to save the straw.  We hauled the bundles with horses and wagons.
In the year 1896, steam threshing began. This lasted until about 1919 and then
the gas powered threshers appeared with smaller separators.  Later the combines
began threshing in northern South Dakota.

When John Neifer died at the age of 97, he still possessed his keen mind and
was always anxious to talk of the "good old days".

The Old Daze
By R. O. Dudley

(Taken from an August 28, 1969, Hosmer Bulletin)

The following account was written by Mr. R. 0. Dudley who first came to Hosmer
as a school teacher in 1905.  He is pictured above with his wife.  They came
back to Hosmer from Huntington Park, CA, early this spring and have lived in
the house they purchased.  The huge timber yard has kept Mr. Dudley busy.  He
will be 80 years old in a few weeks.  Mrs. Dudley spent a few weeks back in
their home in California but returned two weeks ago with their fourteen year old
grandson, Billy.

The Dudleys are enjoying their summer home and our town and say they will be
back next summer if health permits.

My first experience with Russians was in 1905. I worked at the Brokaw Hotel in
Bristol, SD, for my first board and room and attended Bristol High School.  I
had many and varied activities.  I met all passenger trains and carted valises
to and from the hotel.

Traveling men, tourists, and at times Indians going to distant schools,
patronized the hotel.  The Russians did not; but stayed in the station waiting
rooms.  I did not know that they were Germans.

Mr. H. H. Hunter, President of the Milwaukee Land Co. located settlers on
western lands to make business for the railroad and also helped to finance
them.  These settlers carried feather beds, food, tea and babies and their
mothers nursed their babies and attended to their needs.  I became quite used
to their customs and admired their patient and stolid attitude under difficult
circumstances.

Seven years later I came to face some of their problems of survival under
adverse conditions.  Miss Lewis, the Edmunds County Supt. of Schools, urged me
to accept a short termed school at a meager salary.  I passed up a chance to
teach in Andover.  She said that I would have problems but she believed that I
could handle them.

The train rolled into Hosmer and I gazed out at another world.  I could not
believe what my eyes, ears and later my nose brought to me.  Was this Siberia?
Houses, cow-barns, hen-coops, pig-pens, gardens, and odd looking mounds (I
later was told this was "mist"),* were all enclosed inside beautifully white
painted picket fences.

Redeeming features were four churches also painted white.  I distrusted a
stolid, square built building said to be a hotel.  From past experience I had
little use for hotels and especially the female help therein.  I took a very
dim view of it.

I took a look at the school house and I began to feel sick. An old two-roomed,
two story building that still had last years storm windows in place.

A six lever padlock guarded it from intrusion and there was a disreputable
double outhouse.  I cautiously peered inside and found broken down partitions,
supposed to separate the sexes.  I was furious.  I consulted the primary
teacher who also ran the hotel while her mother was visiting relatives in
California.  She could do nothing and refused to have anything to do with it,
so I went to the main man on the school board and told him what needed to be
done.

He was dignified, rather aristocratic and a determined man.  He promised me
nothing, and the harder I argued, the less interested he apparently became.
Later I tried to board and room with him but his wife discouraged that.  He
introduced me around town as Prof.  Dudley and as Onkle Dudley to his beautiful
daughter, Ramona.

Mr. Puckett, Mr. Meier and Doctors John and Bob Quinn assured me that I could
not manage to board in a private home and must live in the hotel.  If I did not
have a contract to teach in Hosmer, I would have quit.

I attended institute in Aberdeen and returned and "Ho! and Below!" a beautiful
new out-house for girls and the old one enlarged and repaired.  John H.
promised nothing BUT he did all that I asked of him.

Julius Sonnenfeld gave me pointers, what to do, how to do, and taught me that
the natives were not hostile, but that we lacked communication.

Julius picked up a rock and hit the padlock and it opened immediately.  He
showed me how the bell rope operated and how to keep it out of the reach of
small children.

Perhaps the same bell is still in use, if so, I saw it hanging by the rope down
the east side of the school house after the big wind of 1968.

When noon arrived practically the entire student body went home.  The primary
teacher went to the Hosmer Hotel, donned an apron and waited on tables.  My
board and room cost $18.00 a month.  I could hardly believe it.  I noticed the
men were respectful and took no liberties with the waitress. At some hotels
some men got disgustingly frank, but not at Hotel Hosmer.

Soon I was invited to join the town band under the direction of William Meier.
I think that was where I really became acquainted.  Every week rehearsal was
held at the school house where one third of the band consisted of my seventh
and eighth grade boys.

There were five red elevators one of which was also a fine flour mill.  At the
depot many came to welcome the daily train, and my friend, Jake Schlaht, took
delight in laying me carefully between the rails as the train slowly coasted
into town, to the delight of the inhabitants.  My derby hat, white collar and
cheap suit did not greatly benefit by the experience. There was little I could
do about it.  He was the best competition in town and I tried.  Jake and I
became firm friends. We often wrestled for fun.  He later taught me to test
cream.

The Lutheran Pastor decided that the saloon keeper's families should be
excluded from the congregation.  Old Mr. Schlaht got up and argued that the
families and children were all the more in need of the fellowship and service
of the church.  He spoke so eloquently for them that the congregation voted to
keep them in membership.

Katie Satler never forgot that a few families had privately built cement
sidewalks.  They placed huge boulders at each end of the walk to discourage any
use of the walk.  Kerosene lamps were in use and some even used candles.  Fuel
was "mist", hay, corncobs and coal and later lignite began to be used about
1918-19.

Florence Geeslin's first term of school was four miles west of Hosmer.  She
walked to school every Monday carrying an alarm clock.  She boarded with
Imberis, where Malsoms now live.  Fridays she walked to town in the evening.
One time she met an elevator man driving furiously and lashing his team of
horses.  When she got to town his elevator was burning.

An old church building over a large cistern was the fire house.  A push cart
with hose and volunteer firemen protected the town.

On special celebrations, special police wore red, white and blue sashes around
their shoulders and carried buggy whips.  I never saw them used.

Secretive men came to the hotel occasionally for private talks with the hired
girls.  I thought they were relatives.  I later learned that they were engaged
in finding wives for young men and were paid go-betweens.  They were sometimes
called "couplers." I think the automobile killed their business.  Oh! for the
"good old days!"

Mr. Geeslin used to say that when one entered a home in winter you had to
contend with two tubs of "mist", Schnapps, Turkish cigarettes, sunflower seeds,
peppers and corn drying in the ceiling and babies with bare bottoms crawling
among the sunflower seeds.  There were odors of out- houses, hen-coops,
pig-pens, and "mist" piles.  There were no trees and few telephones.  Big news
came only to the railroad station telephone or by train whistle.

Farmers tried to buy farms close to their own to make homes for their children,
so that the country often had private shortcut roads radiating out from a
central home to their relatives.  These centrally located family homes and
communities seem to be "Gone With the Wind".

At weddings the groom was decorated as elaborately as was the bride.  All
relatives and friends came.  Deep dishes of a very thin soup was served to all.
If many came, more water could be added to the soup.  The bride's male
relatives thundered into the celebration on farm horses pretending to rescue
her from a "fate worse than death".  As the bride did not want to be rescued
the party settled down to the serious business of making music and testing
refreshments.  The preacher usually had a special not-so-potent drink and best
food available.

One cold winter's day a farmer hitched his team unblanketed beside Knittel and
Eisenbeisz's store.  He stayed all day between the two saloons while his wife
wearily waited for him to take her home.  When he finally arrived in front of
Hosmer Hotel, the mother-in-law had a six inch board and belabored him soundly
over the head until the board was broken into little pieces.  She had three big
men standing by her as enforcers and he stood and took it.  "Ach Himmel!" The
Good Old Days.

SATURDAY NIGHT IN HOSMER

Business on a Saturday night in 1987 is calm and quiet as only those places of
business catering to relaxation are open for business.  This is worth
mentioning because business on a Saturday night now, as opposed to then, has
changed dramatically.  The bustling activity, the pleasurable excitement, the
community of common interests and the good fellowship are gone, and the much
used expression - auch wiedersehen - is much less common.  The reasons for these
drastic changes are many, perhaps the big contributor to the gradual reform was
the improved method of transportation, better highways and modern technology.

From the favorable position of the old timers that were born and raised here,
it is interesting to look back and remember when:

The community was small and growing.  The only private mode of transportation
was horse and wagon.  The business trips to town were few and absolutely
necessary.  Since there were no cafes the high point of this trip was a fresh
sausage from the local butcher shop and a warm beer or pop.  During the winter
months the trip was more drastic and difficult - it was on some of these
occasions that a good shot of John Barleycorn before the homeward trek made the
trip more tolerable.

Along about 1920 the automobile became more common and the rural shopping trend
towards a Saturday night began to emerge.  The business community was rapidly
expanding to meet the challenge of the market place.

By 1935, the automobile was no longer considered a luxury but rather an
inevitable necessity.  This drastically changed the traveling habits of the
rural community.  Probably because it was considered neglectful that the men
should leave the family farm during workable daylight hours, and, probably
because it was considered recklessly absurd for women to drive an automobile,
the era of a Saturday night in town was here and it became a family affair.
The business community quickly became aware of the fact that a trip to a
neighboring town was no longer considered a distant point, consequently, the
Hosmer Commercial Club became more active in business promotion.  The period of
silent motion picture had arrived and the Commercial Club sponsored free,
out-door, silent motion movies every Saturday night during the summer months.
The movies were shown on a vacant lot behind the Farmers State Bank, old planks
borrowed from the lumber yard were the best seats in town.  This promotion drew
standing room crowds only.

The time interval between 1945 and 1960 was a period when the population
density, of both the rural area and the city, reached a peak.  The fascinating
excitement of a grand explosion was in the air every Saturday night.  The
farmer had absolutely no respect for the time clock, their working day began at
sunrise and ended at sunset, consequently, their weekly trip to town on a
Saturday night did not begin until 9 or 10 o'clock.  After the farmers
delivered their cream and eggs to their favorite buyer, the women would do the
necessary household shopping.  The men would go separately to do their business
with the car, implement, lumber dealers, etc.  No one was in a hurry and no one
got excited.  The grocery carts at the grocery stores were bumper to bumper
from 9 o'clock in the evening until well after midnight - it was not uncommon
for the grocery carry-out boy to deliver groceries four to five blocks away.
The sharpening of plow shares and other major machine repairs was an absolute
necessity, consequently, the ring of the blacksmith's hammer on the anvil could
be heard until 1 or 2 o'clock in the morning. Among the high points of a
Saturday night was the good- fellowship, the joy to visit and tarry and say
"hello" to everyone on the street.  And then, finally, the treat of the week
(rural electricity was not yet established, and, therefore, ice cream and cold
pop was a luxury not found on a farm), the family would gather at the Ice Cream
Parlor - by 2 o'clock on a Sunday morning, Saturday night came to rest.

Today, on a calm and quiet Saturday night, the old time farmers and businessmen
can reminiscently grin and lustily sing:

"The old Saturday night, she ain't
        what she used to be,
Ain't what she used to be..."
                Submitted by Arnold Pietz

SHOPPING IN HOSMER ON SATURDAY NIGHTS

In the late 30's, 40's, and most of the 50's Wednesday and Saturday evenings
were the most important hours of the week.  This was the time when farmers
lined the streets of Hosmer to sell their cream and eggs, buy groceries and have
farm machinery repaired.  They would also discuss politics, the state of the,
economy, the strength of a team of horses and/or a make or model of a tractor,
visit with a friend or neighbor, settle arguments or cleanse their throats of
the week's accumulation of dust at the local bar.

The selling of cream and eggs were the primary reason for the trek to town as
their products had to be moved every few days in the summer time.  Almost every
farmer milked a few cows and had chickens.  Mechanical cooling was unheard of
and cellars served this purpose but didn't quite do the job on hot summer days.
During that time there were three or four cream and egg buyers on main street
plus the three grocery stores trading in eggs.

Purchasing groceries was next in importance.  As mentioned earlier three stores
provided this service from the early forties through the fifties.  The popular
brands that were available were IGA, later Super Valu, Red & White and Our
Family.  These grocery stores served as a place for the farm wives to do their
socializing as they provided benches to sit on which were usually occupied by
the early shoppers.

Buying machinery parts and getting things repaired were the chores of the men.
There were three machinery dealers at the time plus the blacksmith shop and a
repair shop.  Many parts had to be rebuilt, welded or completely remanufactured
as most of the equipment was old and new parts were not always available.
While waiting for the shops to perform these services the farmers would visit,
discuss politics, etc. These repair shops were overloaded with work and many
times were open until after midnight which kept all other business places open.
Before the farmer had his repair he would not pick up his wife and the
groceries.

The town would bustle with people in every business place.  Main street was
crowded with cars and it was not unusual to have to park in the residential
area until later in the evening.  Sometimes cars with wives and families were
momentarily lost as the farmer was forced to park in an unfamiliar spot and
forgot where he had parked.

Wherever a group of farmers met, politics and the economy were the main topics.
If the people running this country would have listened and heeded some of the
philosophies discussed, the country's problems might have been solved a long
time ago.

Differences of opinion were sometimes solved in a more physical manner.  If you
couldn't resolve an agreement you rolled up your sleeves.  On one particular
Saturday evening four such disagreements sprang up and were settled in a matter
of fifteen or twenty minutes on main street.  Excitement of this sort assembled
crowds in a big hurry.

The local bar was the last place to stop for most farmers just before going
home.  Many times there were customers three and four deep at the bar.  You
bought your drink and stepped back to make room for the next customer.  The
noise was deafening as every one talked at once and the earlier customer with a
head start on the rest of them became quite vocal.

Such were the Saturday evenings in those days - an end to a week's hard labor -
church on Sunday morning - the beginning of another week.
                Written and submitted by
                Alexander Pietz

SILVER FOX RANCH

The Hosmer Silver Fox Ranch was started in the early 1920's and was located on
the southeast edge of Hosmer.  The enterprise was instigated by a dentist by
the name of Dr. Swinghammer who had originated from Canada.  Along with Dr.
Swinghammer, John Heckenlaible, John J. Gutjahr, Arthur J. Haerter, Jacob
Schaible, Eugene Herrboldt, Carl Stoecker and August Docktor formed a
corporation and purchased $6000 worth of Silver foxes from Canada.  Pens were
built with posts and wood framing and covered with chicken wire including the
floors so as to keep the fox from digging out. Crude hutches were in the pens
for shelter.  The property also included a well and a building for storage
purposes.  August Docktor was the keeper for many years and later John Maisch
was the keeper of the farm.  In later years a portion of a vacant building on
main street, which had been a hardware store, dance hall and numerous other
businesses was moved to the farm.  A storage freezer was installed in this
building for the purpose of storing raw meat for the foxes.  In addition to the
raw meat a mixture which looked and smelled like oatmeal was cooked once a day
and fed to the foxes.  The raw meat was obtained from mostly crippled, lamed,
blind or old horses and rabbits.  Chip Walth related the information that as a
young teenager he would walk to the surrounding farms and ride if possible the
old horses to town, sometimes collecting as many as ten horses in one trip.  He
also stated that at one time a farmer came to town and looked over the slaughter
horses and stated that one particular horse was better than the one he owned
and would like to trade.  A deal was made but by the time the horse was
delivered to the farm the farmer's horse had died.  Being unable to trade the
farmer paid for the horse.

They had as many as 36 pairs of foxes, raising as high as 100 or more pups in a
year.  When the fur was prime the foxes were chloroformed and skinned and sold.
The business evidently was profitable as it remained in existence for over 20
years and was closed down because of a disease epidemic in approximately 1947.
The farm was then dismantled and the freezer building was sold and moved to
Ipswich.  The land was used as cattle pasture for some time and is now owned by
the Dennis Hollans.

During the research of this article a similar enterprise was discovered.  John
Maisch and his sons had a small scale fur business on the farm eight miles
north of Hosmer.  Instead of raising fox, they raised skunk and badger.  Many
rabbits and gophers were hunted as a food supply.  They also ate chicken, beef,
and pork scraps.  The skunk was also fond of watermelon.  The original parent
stock was trapped and then caged.  The badger was very difficult to confine but
the skunk became very tame.  Skunk litters were from 7-10 young.  When the
enterprise was begun, the price of a skunk pelt was $7.00 but soon thereafter
the fur market dropped to the point where the business was no longer profitable
and was discontinued.

        Maisch Fur Farm

Lance and Melody Bonen are currently in the silver fox business along with the
wrecking business.  The fox pairs are kept in wire cages and hutches suspended
on a wood frame so they are not on the ground.  They keep from 10-15 pairs which
will produce from one to nine pups but a good average is four pups per adult
pair.  A concentrated dry cereal mixture mixed with water is fed to the fox
along with raw meat as rabbit, beef and pork lungs, etc.  A balanced ration
produces a quality fur.  Fur prices are very erratic with pelts selling as high
as $340 per pelt to a low of $40 per pelt.  Lance finds this to be a high risk
business but enjoys raising them.  He, however, does not like visitors as the
foxes tend to become very nervous when strangers appear.

        Kermit Ulmer, Kangaroo Court

        Wilmer Heckenlaible, Streets & Concessions

DIAMOND JUBILEE 1962

The motto for our Diamond Jubilee was "LET US LOOK BACK TO HELP US MOVE
FORWARD".  The steering committee for this successful event was: Chairman-
Arnold Pietz, Secretary-Rev. George Dirks, and Treasurer-Royal Stabbe.
Committee chairmen were: Pioneer and Historical-Gottlieb Walth, Exhibits and
Relics-John (Sparky) Stoecker, Publicity and Finances-Plin Haerter, Jubilee
Book-Miles Miller, Entertainment-Arnold Herrboldt, Whiskers-Reinhold (Chip)
Walth, Dance-Sam Pietz,  Hospitality-John Lehr, Ladies Dresses-Rose Gross,
Kangaroo Court-Kermit Ulmer and Leo Goehring, Lunch-Amy Heckenlaible, Streets
and Concessions-Wilmer Heckenlaible, Parade-John Haerter, and Decorations-
American Legion.

Everyone was busy with meetings planning for the big event.  The Jubilee Book
"Trails and Tales" had arrived and was sold for $3.00. The ladies were busy
sewing Jubilee dresses, baking kuchen, and helping make pies, which were frozen
and then baked on the days the Jubilee in the Lutheran Church kitchen.  Floats
were being built and the excitement of the approaching Jubilee Days was in the
air.

The Kangaroo Court took place every Wednesday evening with Sam Job- Defense
Attorney, Miles Miller-Prosecuting Attorney, Lawrence Mohr-Sheriff, Clarence
Dewald and Floyd Ulmer-bailiffs, Kermit Ulmer-Judge, and John Huber and Alex
Pietz-members of the jury.  The Judge hadn't sent anyone to the gallows and
felt that he should fulfill his duties, so he finally found a lady (Vera Pietz)
who had sold a pair of red flannel underwear to a customer on a very warm day
and sent her to the gallows.

The Murphy's Northern State Shows and Carnival and the Marlow Review Stage Show
arrived on the to set up their rides and stage.

On June 15th, the pioneers and distant visitors registration began at 9:00 a.m.
and continued throughout the two days.  During the day we all enjoyed a Hosmer
High School Band concert, the Charmettes, a girls singing group directed by
Vera Pietz, an Indian Dance and the crowning of an Honorary Chief, Albert Gross.

The High School Reunion was held in the Hosmer school with about 300 alumni
from 16 states registering.  In the meantime the Hosmer Baseball Team beat the
Gettysburg Radar Base Team 12-7

Entertainment for the evening included the Rockettes, Paulette and Sydney
Herrboldt, Nancy Walth and Debra Haerter, doing several tap dancing routines.
This was followed by the judging and presentation of awards for the best
whiskers and Jubilee dresses.  There was, however, from time to time some
worrying, confusion and laughter in the judging of the dresses as the judges
forgot the bustles (the judges were men)-but the contest was a pleasant event.
The grandmothers were charming, the mothers beautiful and the daughters lovely.

What would happen if a man with a full beard tried to eat cotton candy?  Jake
Voegele agreed to help answer this question which turned out to be an
unsuccessful attempt on his part.  The cotton candy was given to a young boy
who was watching and who enjoyed it more than Jake.

The Marlow Reviews Stage show, a great family show featured trained animal
acts, aerial acts and comedy performances. During intermission The Honorable
Ralph Herseth made an appearance, followed by the Fireworks Display and dances
with music by Whoopee John and Dick Gross and the Uniques.

On June 16th, the Old Timers Breakfast was served to 87 pioneers in the
Lutheran Church parlors at 8:00 a.m. The rain was a big concern for the day,
but the weatherman finally decided to cooperate.  He had dumped two inches of
rain on the town during the night, delaying the parade for an hour. Despite the
mud and damp weather the parade proved to be one of the highlights of the
celebration.  WHAT A PARADE!!!  There were about 3,000 people lining the streets
to watch the floats, bands, horses, covered wagons, horseless carriages,
locomotives, elephants, clowns and the honored King Baldaser Gisi, Sr. and
Queen Regina Stabbe.  There were 100 entries in the parade.  The parade was led
by the American Legion Honor Guard and the Hosmer High School Band.  Eureka's
High School Band also participated in the parade.

In the afternoon, the Marlow Review Stage Show put on their final act.
Governor Archie Gubbrud greeted the people. Motorcycle races and the dropping
of watches and other gifts from an airplane, Indian dances, and two big dances
concluded the two-day Jubilee celebration in Hosmer.

Lunches were available in the Legion Hall and in refreshment stands on the
street throughout the celebration.  You could buy a HOTDOG for a dime!!

The chairmen and committees, all the people and area farmers did themselves
PROUD!!!  Yes, "LET US LOOK BACK TO HELP US MOVE FORWARD".
                Submitted by Vera Pietz

JUBILEE RAFFLE

Above is proof of what one dollar can purchase.  The 1962 Jubilee Committee
raffled a car as a fund raising project. Tickets were sold for $1.00 each.
August Rieger bought one ticket for one dollar and was the winner of a 1962
Chevy II Nova.  Mrs. Rieger drives the car today.

THE SIGHTLESS WALKER

Through the trees he gently goes,
He whistles and howls and mournfully blows.

He goes between and underneath-
Even around the Christmas wreath.

He swiftly picks up speed
And hungrily takes the bird's milkweed.

Then slows to take a rest
And picks up speed to destroy a nest.

The Wind! how he howls and groans
To humbly fall within the stones.

He twirls and twists and takes a bow
To show the people the mighty how.

He whispers to a neighboring tree
Which tells others the gossip of me.

If of all the things I said are true,
REMEMBER - the silence of me and you.
                Audrey M. Hieb

Was einer ist,
        was einer war,
Beim Schleiden wird es offenbar
Wir horan nicht, wenn Gottes
        weise summt
Wir schaudern erst, wenn sie
        verstummt.

Muh und Arbeit war ihr Leben
        Treu und fleiszig ihre Hand
Muge Gott ihr Ruhe geben
        Rasten hat sie nie gekannt.
                Author Unknown

        FULL CIRCLE

When you've lost your intrigue in the foreign,
No longer yearn for the strange,
When you long for the place you were born in
The homely home on the range;
When you want to commune with your kinfolk
In the language you spoke as a child,
When you've lost your intrigue in the foreign
In the strange, the far-out, and the wild--
Then, return to the town you grew up in,
Be greeted by friends of your folks,
Feel at home as you roam about Main Street
Exchanging the same worn-out jokes;
Be warmed at the hearth of hearts caring,
Be charmed by the young and the old,
When you've lost your intrigue in the foreign,
WELCOME!               BACK TO THE FOLD.
                Arline Heckenlaible
                Hosmer, SD 57448

CONFIRMATION SCHOOL PAU'SE (RECESS)

We all ran down, to the end of town
Where the circus pitched a tent,
While we hung about, with the roustabouts
And breathed the sawdust scent;
We forgot about school, and broke the rule
To be at our seats on time,
While the boys got jobs
And the girls dreamed scenes
Of a world out there -- more prime.
As we tagged our way back,
On the hot, dusty track,
We heard a distant bell
And hastened our pace
(tho not quite to a race)
As the sound of it broke the spell,
We knew very well, we'd be lectured to hell
Once our mentor came face to face
Then we'd settle down, beneath his frown
Although never to be the same
As the day before that open door
To a world out there of fame...
Such were the ways, of those long days,
Pre-TV and a larger world,
In a little town, in another phase
While a hundred years unfurled;
Pray the years hence, about to commence
A new era, leaving the past,
May be as blest with that kind of zest
Making memories wanting to last.
                Arline Heckenlaible
                Hosmer, S.D. 57448

NOSTALGIA OF A PRAIRIE SKIPPER

With the surf pounding hard on the old sea wall
And the wind howling wild in the dark,
I long for the peace of the prairie I knew
As I feel I have missed the mark;
I'm sighing for soft summer breeze through the trees
And missing long walks in the park,
Along with the gentle rains in the spring
And the song of a meadowlark.
I'm missing the calm of a cradle moon
Shining down on a town sound asleep,
And as I recall my love for it all
The thought of it all makes me weep,
For the ocean is wide, and my prairie afar
And suddenly life seems stark,
With the surf pounding hard on the old sea wall
And the wind howling wild in the dark.
                Arline Heckenlaible
                Hosmer, S.D. 57448

THE BLIZZARD OF '66

March came in with a promise of spring, but by the evening of the second, North
Dakota and western and northern South Dakota began receiving wind driven snow.

By the morning of March third, it was snowing hard and the wind began to pick
up speed.  Many farmers still ventured out to do chores that evening, but by
morning they knew it wasn't safe to be out until the winds subsided.  The winds
ranged from 60 mph to gusts as high as 100 mph in the western part of the
state.  With winds that strong, the snow sifted through the cracks thus adding
another chore after the storm, that being to remove snow from attics and
outbuildings.

Probably the thing most missed was electricity.  We were now living in an era
where almost everything we had was run by electricity, so most homes had little
or no heat.

When the blizzard was over there were buildings and cars buried, livestock dead
or strayed from home, and Hosmer got in the news with their 21 foot high snow
bank on Main Street. We were very fortunate though as no lives were lost in our
community.
                Phyllis Hieb

TWO HOMEMAKERS WHEEL AROUND HOUSE

Taken from March 13, 1969 Hosmer Bulletin

Two women who have lived many years in wheel chairs are Mrs. Gottlieb Mohr and
Mrs. John Stacker.  Mrs. Mohr has been in a wheel chair for eleven years and
Mrs. Stacker for eighteen years.

Mrs. Mohr became paralyzed from polio 44 years ago. She had been married four
years, had one son (who passed away at the age of 27 after being wounded in the
Philippines) and then Otto, was born after polio struck.  Later, Helmuth (42)
and Freida (Mrs.  Arnold Schurr, 32) arrived.

Mrs. Mohr enjoys baking, as can be seen on the photo- she once kept everlasting
yeast for nineteen years.  Mr. Mohr is a carpenter and is often away from home
during the day, but Mrs. Mohr keeps busy with her baking, sewing, quilting or
just watching TV.

She loves to plan and prepare meals at Christmas and Easter time for all the
children and 10 grandchildren.  She also proudly stated that they have two
great grandchildren and two grandsons in Vietnam at the present time.

Mrs. Stacker has been afflicted with multiple sclerosis for twenty-five years.
Massage and heat applied for one year was done by their youngest daughter,
Virginia, as recommended at Mayo Clinic.  Virginia is now Mrs. Donald Anderson
of Minneapolis.  The Stackers have one other daughter, (Marcella) at Lodi, CA.
The children and grandchildren manage to come home every summer.

Mrs. Stacker helped her husband make rugs with a loom for four and one-half
years, filling the shuttle, sorting colors, taking orders, etc.  Mr. Stacker
helps his wife with all the cooking, washing, baking, etc.  She loves writing
letters and likes to get mail, too, as seen in the photo.  Listening to church
services on the radio and reading is a habit.

Both the Mohr and Stacker kitchen counter tops were lowered a few inches to
help in cooking chores.  During the earlier years of their handicaps, both of
the ladies were able to get to church etc. with the help of men carrying them.
Neither one was ever hospitalized.

Both women give much of the credit of their positive approach to life, to their
husbands, who have helped all along the way.  They have made them feel like a
person, not a person in a wheel chair.  Both admitted sitting from 16-18 hours
at one time, but say "why complain about something that can't be changed?" Mrs.
Stacker said, "I'll take life as it is." Mrs. Mohr says, "I can't complain
since I have no pain." She had psoriasis for one year and was unable to use her
hands-"that", she said, "was hardest on me."

JOHN LEHR RETIRES; CARRIES MAIL 45 YEARS

Taken from a July 16, 1970, Hosmer Bulletin

Pictured above is John Lehr, at 9:30 a.m. on Friday, as he makes his last
journey delivering mail.  His retirement began the following day, July 11.

Lehr started working as a rural mail carrier on July 1, 1925.  At that time he
traveled 30 1/2 miles every day.  Ten miles were added to the route within the
next year and a half, at which time he had 118 patrons or boxes.  At the time
of his retirement, he was traveling 47 1/2 miles daily, having 105 boxholders
including the city extension.

While being interviewed, Mr. Lehr reminisced about the first few years when he
used sled and horses in the winter, and changing teams halfway.  The gas heater
in the sled was started early in the morning to add a little comfort during the
long, cold ride.

The first automobile he used was a 1924 Model T Ford.

When roads were impassable, he often delivered groceries and tobacco to
patrons.  In the winter of 1950-51, Lehr hired a light two-passenger plane to
deliver mail, dropping it on farms and school houses.

He recollected delivering 27 daily papers at one time, after roads had been
blocked, to a patron, Rudolph Hieb.

Mr. Lehr used 26 automobiles during his 45 years of service and drove a total
of 670,000 miles.  He accumulated 285 days of unused sick days, and used 300
days since 1925.

He has a record of never having had an accident, and has received several pins
and awards from the Nation Safety Council for professional safe driving
performance for the U.S. Post Office Dept.

An incident he'll never forget was the time it had rained for a week, when his
automobile got stuck in the mud; then a flat tire-and changing the tire in the
heavy rain.

Patrons he has had throughout the entire 45 years were: Mrs. Fred W. Mohr, Mr.
and Mrs. Solomon Ulmer, Mrs. Emil Hieb, Mr. and Mrs. Theo. Hieb and Mr. and Mrs.
August Kirschenman.

Postmasters he has served under were: Gottlieb Walth,  1925-1934; Dan Pietz,
acting for 14 months; Walter Ulmer,  1936 to 1968 and Bernard Grismer, acting
postmaster since  February of 1969.

FIRE RAZES WALTH MOTOR CO. WEDNESDAY

Taken from May 10, 1973, Hosmer Bulletin

Pictured are the remains of the Walth Motor Co. after it was destroyed by fire
last week Wednesday morning.  Fire departments from Bowdle, Roscoe and Eureka
joined the Hosmer department in battling the blaze and kept it from spreading.
Albert Stabbe, one of the mechanics, had removed a gasoline tank from a vehicle
when some of the fuel dropped on a toggle light resulting in an explosion.
Albert and his brother Dan, both were engulfed in flames as they fought the
fire.  Also at the scene were two other employees, Dale Dockter and Marvin
Kunde.  Owner Reinhold (Chip) Walth, had been doing his bookkeeping and took a
coffee break at the time of the incident.

The building, entire stock, equipment and two customer cars is a total loss.
The lumber yard storage building next to the garage (pictured) was estimated as
having approximately $2500 damage to the building, plus $2000 loss on the
storage contents (roofing), according to owner, Elmer Hieb.

Paint and window damage was reported at the Hosmer Theatre, City Hall and
Farmers Union Coop Oil Co., as a result of intensive heat.  Buildings
threatened which required water spraying were Hosmer Lumber Co., Christ Heilman
and John Neifer garages.

The building was erected 53 years ago by Jacob Heckenlaible who started the
automobile business, later adding the hardware store.  Edwin Heckenlaible sold
the business to Samuel Pietz and Reinhold Walth in August,  1947.  Mr. Pietz
sold his share of the partnership to Mr. Walth  in July, 1961.

Mr. Walth and his wife won a trip to Rome last spring after he sold his quota
of Chevrolet cars.  In January, this year, he received a plaque in recognition
of his 25 years in Chevrolet dealership.  At this time, Mr. Walth's plans are
indefinite.  In Hosmer's history, this was the sixth business establishment
completely destroyed by fire. An elevator burned down  in 1908; Hosmer Auto Co.
in September, 1924; the Hosmer  Mill, February, 1929; Aman's Elevator July,
1936; Hosmer  Meat Market in January, 1965; and the Farmers Elevator Co.  in
February, 1967.

MULTIPLE BIRTHS

Multiple births (twin, triplet, quadruplet, or quintuplet) are not an every day
occurrence in the human race and are becoming less frequent.  Scientific
research found a twin birth to be one in 80-87 births in the past but is now
one in 96.  A triplet birth was about one in 7,869 births but now has changed
to one in 9,000 births.  The survival rate was also low until modern medicines
were developed.  Scientists have also found twinning to be a hereditary factor,
a fact that has been proven in this community by at least three different
families.  Listed below are the multiple births that occurred in this community
for almost a century.
