MEDICAL SERVICES IN HOSMER

Hosmer has been fortunate to have excellent medical services for many, many
years.  The doctors will be listed as they served the area.

Dr. Frankenstein was the first doctor to come to Hosmer.  We do not have the
exact year, but it is thought it was about 1900.  He was followed by Dr.
Kettner who was a brother-in-law of Pastor Taueber who was the Lutheran
minister at that time.

Dr. John Quinn was a doctor first, but found time to be Hosmer's first fire
chief and was active in the Catholic Church.  He with other members met with
the Catholic Bishop to try and persuade him to sanction the church in Hosmer.
He was followed by Dr. Grace.

Dr. Wohileben was in Hosmer in 1918 during the influenza epidemic.  The school
was closed because of the flu and so it was converted into a hospital until the
epidemic was over.  The doctor would ask the young adults who didn't have the
flu to go to school and assist him.  When fatigued he would hire a driver for
his car to make calls to those who were ill in the rural area.  Everyone was
relieved when the flu epidemic ended.

Dr. Wager served Hosmer about 1926 and was followed by Dr. Neukamp who
practiced in Hosmer for quite a number of years.  His stay was ended when he
was killed in a car accident.  Dr. and Mrs. Neukamp are buried in the Hosmer
Cemetery.

Dr. Steiner came from Austria and was required to work under another doctor.
He joined Dr. Neukamp and served the area until Dr. Neukamp's death.  He
eventually was granted a license and began a new practice in Lemmon.

From the time of Dr. Neukamp's death in 1944 until 1952, Hosmer was without a
doctor, and it was felt a search should begin.  The Hosmer Medical Association
had its inception in the Hosmer Civic Club and was organized November 25, 1952.
This group was responsible for purchasing the old Lutheran Church and
converting it into a home and office for a doctor.  Dr. Karlis Zvejnieks, an
intern at the Sioux Valley Hospital in Sioux Falls, was contacted which
resulted in his moving to Hosmer in January, 1953.  The cost of converting the
church into an apartment and office was $10,200 which Dr. Zvejnieks bought.  He
practiced in Hosmer for four years and then went to Leola.

After Zvejnieks left, Dr. E. A. Schabauer, who had been practicing in Canada,
moved to Hosmer in July, 1959. However, his stay was short and in 1961 he moved
to Hoven. Hosmer was again without a doctor.

It was in 1980, a strong need for a doctor was felt and the Hosmer Civic Club
organized a Board of Directors for Medical Services.  This board consisted of
President John Haerter, Arnold Pietz, Dennis Hollan and Jere Hieb.  This Board
was instrumental in locating a doctor and in the creation of the present Hosmer
Medical Clinic which is housed in the front portion of the former Hosmer
Theater.  Open house was held in November of 1981. Arline Heckenlaible donated
the furniture and floor covering for the waiting room.

Dr. John Ottenbacher, Bowdle, began his practice in Hosmer in the fall of 1981
giving us excellent service for two years.  He was followed by Dr. Mary Klien
of Bowdle and Dr. Gordon Phillips of Selby.  Dr. Salimur Rahman of Eureka
served our community for a year.  In November, 1985, Dr. David Wolff and Dr.
Stephen Grant of Bowdle began practicing in Hosmer.  Dr. Wolff moved to
Aberdeen in September, 1986.

Dr. Grant's home is in New Jersey although he attended Georgetown University in
Washington, DC, and finalized his medical studies at St. Joseph's Hospital in
Phoenix, AZ.  He began practicing in Bowdle in July, 1985.  He continues to
serve Hosmer in the Hosmer Medical Clinic which was remodeled by Westworld
Community Healthcare, Inc. in the spring of 1986.

It is hoped this clinic will be used for many years!

RECOLLECTIONS OF HOSMER

1952 when I was interning in McKennan Hospital in Sioux Falls and only one year
in United States, fleeing from Russians, who had occupied my native country
Latvia, I did not know where I could land and where I would get shelter for my
family.  My wife Biruta and I were just married a year and we had a baby
Lilita.  South Dakota had shortage of doctors and we foreign doctors were
supposed to go to so called emergency areas, where such a doctor shortage
existed.  One day we were visited by a delegation from a small town north
-Hosmer.  They promised to supply me with an office as well as help me to
establish my practice lending me money for office equipment.  These people with
Gottlieb Walth as spokesman for them seemed to be very nice and friendly.  We
went to visit Hosmer, we liked what we saw and decided to locate there.  In
January, 1953, they made for us a great reception and although I did not have a
hospital in Hosmer, I started my practice.

The winter that year was tough.  Once of my first experiences with Dakota
winters was when, a few days after my arrival, I got a call to help to deliver
a baby 9 miles west from Hosmer at the crossroad of Hosmer-Java and
Bowdle-Eureka roads.  The lady was supposed to go to Eureka hospital under Dr.
McIntosh's care.  But I got a call early morning after a blizzard from Eureka
doctor, that the patient in labor is unable to go to Eureka nor the doctor to
her farm.  As I was only 9 miles away, perhaps I could make it to the farm.
Bill Schilling from the telephone company offered to help.  He had a four-wheel
drive van and organized quickly 4 more cars to come along.  But even the
four-wheel drive got stuck several times in the deep snow, and the men along
with us had to pull and push to get going again.  Biruta, who came along to
assist with the delivery, was sitting in the van and freezing, while we worked
frantically in the deep snow.  It took almost 3 hours until through the blowing
snow we saw the farm.  The farmer-seeing our predicament with the stalled van,
finally came with a team of horses, and the last half a mile we finished in a
sled.  Needless to say, meanwhile the baby was born already.  I just had to tie
the cord and deliver the placenta.

Few days later I got a call from Dr. Rosedahl in Eureka to help him with an
emergency appendectomy.  I don't know why he did not call the other doctor in
Eureka, Dr. McIntosh, but I obeyed and went.  While we were operating, it
started to snow stronger and stronger.  Wind was increasing and the snow was
blown around like white sand.  In our country Latvia there were hard winters
too, but snow came down slowly in big white flakes.  Dr. Rosedahl warned me not
to go back to Hosmer some 20 miles away any more, but stay with him.  But I had
not had any previous experience with blizzards and  bravely refused his offer.
"O.K." Dr. Rosedahl said, "you  go, if you want. But if you get stuck,
specially around the hills near Hillsview, stay there, don't leave the car and
wait.  It is  too dangerous in a snowstorm to seek shelter somewhere else. In
an hour I will call your home and if you have not returned by then, we go and
look for you." Sure enough, close to Hillsview there was just a white wall of
snow caused by blowing whirlwind and no road visible.  Cautiously I crept with
my car forward guessing the direction of the road.  Wrong!  I did not realize
at that particular place the road was turning and in the ditch I went.  I had a
shovel along.  I tried to dig myself out, but in vain.  I was stuck.
Everything was white around me. I did not see any farm, nothing.  Just
somewhere in the distance I heard a train was going by.  I remembered the
warning in Eureka, crawled back into the car, put the heater on and waited.  I
waited and waited, seconds felt like minutes and minutes like an hour.  I had
waited for about an hour, when I heard some motors and saw some light through
the white sand-like snow.  There was a wrecker from Eureka, several men and Dr.
Rosedahl.  He had called my wife as promised after an hour and when he found
out I had not returned, organized a rescue mission.  They pulled me out of the
ditch. Meanwhile my wife got concerned and worried about my fate and told the
telephone operator about my predicament.  Soon all Hosmer knew that the new
doctor had disappeared in the snow storm.  The whistle blew.  Bill Schilling
with his four-wheel drive and 4-5 other cars went to look for me.  We had not
finished with the Eureka crew, when from the other end the rescuers from Hosmer
came and together with them I triumphantly returned to Hosmer.  The doctor was
saved! Thus ended my first dangerous experience with South Dakota blizzard.

My office first was located on the main street across from Super Valu.   I
think, there had been a bank (shoemaker's store?) before me.  It was very
primitive, but I had a lot of patients from Hosmer, Hillsview, Java, Bowdle,
Roscoe, even from Eureka.  Toward spring they finished my new office and living
quarters, remodeling the old Lutheran church for that purpose: the office was
in front, living quarters in back of the building.  But soon I found the
disadvantages too.  So one summer morning, when at 4:00 a.m. I had just
returned from a delivery, at 5:00 a doorbell rang frantically several times,
while I was in bed sleeping 'Go, my wife woke me up, "it must be a terrible
accident." I jumped into my slacks and went to the door. There was a young
farmer.  "What is the matter?" I anxiously asked.  "I have ringworm," and he
showed a spot on his arm.  "I tried for 2 weeks with iodine and it does not go
away." "But why you come so early in the morning?  I just came back an hour ago
from the hospital?" "I thought you are awake already," the guy sheepishly
answered, "besides I wanted to be in the field by 6 o'clock."

We were well liked in Hosmer.  Part of it was because I could speak fluent
German.  "Unser deutche doctor," the old people called me and were glad I spoke
their language.  I heard everyone in Hosmer was German, except Mrs. Schlaht, who
was of Swedish descent, but married a German, while she was teaching in Hosmer.
 It seemed strange at first: there were 3 Lutheran services on Sunday - two
were in German (full, men on one side, women on other) and only one in English
(half empty).  People spoke more German than English.  Our baby- sitter (we had
our 2nd child, son Sigurd, born while we lived in Hosmer), Rose Guthmiller, was
raised and born in this country, but she could not speak English fluently.  The
farthest away from Hosmer she had been in her life was Mobridge west and
Aberdeen east.  That was all her world.

One of the greatest disadvantages for me was that we did not have any hospital
in Hosmer.  Hosmer was just in the middle between Bowdle, Ipswich and Eureka.
My Hillsview, Eureka and Long Lake patients wanted to go to Eureka; my Java and
Bowdle patients to Bowdle, Roscoe and Ipswich patients to Ipswich.  I was like
a traveling salesman every day and in one year I had more than 20,000 miles on
my car.  One night I had 2 calls: one from Eureka hospital, other from Ipswich:
2 of my expectant mothers had come in and were in labor.  The one in Ipswich
was first-timer, the other in Eureka expecting her 3rd child.  So I first ran
to Eureka, delivered the baby there, then jumped in my car and sped to Ipswich.
 Just got in time to deliver the baby there.  That was enough.  When the
opportunity came to move to Leola where they had a hospital, in 1956, I moved
to Leola.  But still the friendship with many Hosmerites and professional ties
have not been broken.  I still have lot of friends there.  Lot of Hosmer people
I still have counted as my patients and they have been faithful to me until my
recent retirement, and I have been proud to be their family doctor.  Several
girls I delivered some 20-30 years ago have been married and I have delivered
their babies.  A new generation has come into existence.  I hear, German is not
spoken so much any more, the small country schools with one teacher for all the
grades have disappeared, the schools have consolidated and busses bring in the
students to Hosmer every morning from surrounding area, people travel distances
and distances away.  Unfortunately many young people leave Hosmer and even
South Dakota and many former Hosmerites have scattered east and west, north and
south, many of whom will return as guests for this centennial celebration of
Hosmer.  Deep in our heart we have warm feelings toward Hosmer and people of
Hosmer.  Hosmer was part of our lives, too.  Coming from overseas from a
different country, my wife and I never felt as strangers in Hosmer and for that
- thanks, Hosmer!
                Written by
                Karlis Zvejnieks, M.D.

        Dr. K. Zveinieks' family at Christmas 1985

HOSMER DENTAL CLINIC

The Hosmer Dental Clinic became a reality due to the interest of the community
on October 10, 1985.

Dr. Curtis G. Baltz, of Hoven, was contacted by Arnold Wiederrich, then
president of the Hosmer Civic Club, concerning the possibility of opening a
satellite clinic to serve the needs of the community.  In August of 1985, Dr.
Baltz surveyed the area and felt that the community could indeed support a
dental clinic.  Following a meeting with the city the plans were finalized and
renovation of the "old" Hosmer medical offices was begun.  As stated earlier,
in October of 1985, family dental services were offered on a regular basis to
the community.

In January of 1986, the Hosmer Dental Clinic became part of a group of dental
clinics serving the rural interests of North Central South Dakota.  This group
of clinics is known as Dakota Dental Services, Inc. or D.D.S., Inc.  D.D.S.,
Inc. is pleased to serve Hosmer and the surrounding area.  D.D.S., Inc. also
has clinics in Hoven, Gettysburg, Roscoe and Bowdle.

Dr. Steve R. Tesch who is now with Dakota Dental Service, Inc. working with Dr.
Curtis Baltz of oven is serving the Hosmer area.  Dr. Tesch grew up in
Watertown, SD, receiving his Doctor of Dental Surgery Degree from the
University of Minnesota.  Dr. Tesch resides in Bowdle, is single and enjoys
hunting, fishing and golfing.

Carol Vogel, dental assistant, is a native of Hoven and a 1979 graduate of
LAVTI at Watertown.  She has been employed at Watertown and Aberdeen prior to
her new position with Dr. Tesch.

Dr. Tesch and his assistant also have office hours at Roscoe and Bowdle.

        Dr. Steven R. Tesch and his assistant Carol Vogel.

GRAIN WAGON RESTORED

This grain wagon was bought by Edward Ulmer between 1917 and 1919 through a
mail order catalog.  Rupert Ulmer purchased the wagon in 1937 and built a new
box for it in 1939.  Rupert used the wagon until 1960 after which it was then
set aside.

In 1985 Darwin Ulmer completely rebuilt his grandfather's grain wagon by
restoring the running gear and building a new box.

The wagon is now retired and resting quietly in the barn under a cover to
protect it from the elements.

REPRODUCED SURREY

This surrey pictured was built by Darwin Ulmer during the winter of 1985.  He
started the project with the running gear of an old buggy and rebuilt it
completely with new wheels and oak wood.  It was built from scratch which took
approximately 3 months to complete.

Berniece Mohr of Hosmer created the crushed velvet upholstery and Mrs. Ervin
Roesler of Eureka did the seaming on the imitation patent leather fenders and
the real patent leather front.  Many of the parts for the surrey were mail-
ordered from the Amish in Indiana.

The surrey was reproduced by observing an old advertisement which was produced
by the Ulcerate Surrey Co. in the early 1900's.

The surrey has been used at weddings, anniversaries, parades and has provided
many hours of enjoyment.

RENOVATION OF THE 102 CIVIC CENTER

Reminiscent of the early day barn railings, the people of the Hosmer community
got together and donated their time and talents to completely redo the 102
Ballroom.  The old hall was torn down in the fall of 1985 with only the floor
kept intact. Then a new 54 x 96 steel building was erected.  By the time the
hall was completed in the spring of 1986, approximately 125 people had donated
labor to make this dream a reality.  Money was also raised in the community
through various fund raisers.  Below are pictures depicting the various phases
of construction.

HOSMER COMMUNITY PARK

On February 28, 1956, fourteen Hosmerites representing the American Legion
Auxiliary, Extension, Legion, Civic and Sportsman Clubs, met at the Legion Hall
to organize a park committee.  In May, 1956, the Haerter family donated 300' x
673' of land on the northeast corner of Out-Lot G as a memorial to their
parents, John and Christina Haerter, to be used as the site for the park.

The initial project was the planting of 200 cottonwood trees and numerous
evergreens.  The cottonwood trees were short lived and were replaced with
American elm and ash. The American elm in time contracted the Dutch elm disease
and with the dry years the ash began to die.  About five years ago these trees
were replaced with established ash.

In the spring of 1961, after giving the trees an opportunity to root and grow,
about two-thirds of the park was sodded with donated labor.  Community
cooperation and spirit in Hosmer is excellent.  The remaining one-third has
been utilized as a ball park.  For a number of years the Hosmer Homemakers
Extension Club beautified the park by planting flowers.

Toilet facilities, playground equipment consisting of swings, slides and
teeter-totters, and permanent picnic tables were installed in 1962 in time for
the Jubilee.  Also, the brick entrance with flower boxes was constructed.  At
this juncture, the park was dedicated.

In 1983, a 54 x 24' wooden shelter was constructed at a cost of $4,200 by the
Farmers State Bank and Haerter-Heckenlaible Corporation, and in 1984 additional
playground equipment for young children consisting of a tornado slide, moon
ride, and bouncing animals, and three adult benches were donated.

Reunions, birthday parties, picnics, church services, and other gatherings
including the Dankest festivities have been held in the park.  Truly this park
has been serving the community needs.

ROBERT C. STOECKER, COUNTY COMMISSIONER

Robert C. Stoecker was elected County Commissioner in 1980, six years after his
father, Carl Stoecker, had finished serving twenty years as a commissioner.  In
1984, he was elected chairman of the board for a one year term.

Some of the highlights of the year Robert served as chairman were: The
introduction of CPR training under the direction of Mike Nehls and Tracy
Treichel; the law suit against the Oahe Sub-Conservancy District to stop the
$1,100,000 Ring Dike contract with the James River Water Shed.  Edmunds County
was joined by several other counties and eventually won the case; the budget
for 1984 was $1,493,000 with emphasis placed on the improvement of roads in the
southern part of the county.

The 1986 County Commissioners seated left to right are: Marvin Uhrich, Roscoe,
representing District #3; Robert Stoecker, Hosmer, District #4; Raymond
Bachman, Bowdle, District #5, Raymond Forkel, Ipswich, District #1; Standing is
Louis Thares, Ipswich, District #2.

Others from Hosmer that have served as County Commissioners were:

       John Haerter                             1906-1914
       John Heckenlaible                        1922-1926
       Frank Boldt                              1926-1930
       Solomon Ulmer                            1942-1954
       Carl Stoecker                            1954-1974
       J. O. Huntsinger                         1974-1980
       Robert Stoecker                          1980-19--

Edmunds County was organized July 26, 1883, by a meeting of the Board of
Commissioners appointed by Governor Ordway.  The first Edmunds County
Courthouse was built in 1889-90 of stone and brick at a cost of $11,000.  It is
interesting to note that this courthouse served as a shelter when there was an
Indian scare.  However, by 1930, the courthouse had become too small to
accommodate all the needs of  the county offices so steps were taken to
construct a new one.

The original courthouse was vacated, then torn down, and construction on the
new and larger courthouse was begun in the spring of 1934 in accordance with
the National Industrial Recovery Act.

Roland R. Wilken of Watertown, SD, was the architect and the Gray Construction
Company of Watertown, SD, was the general contractor.  The courthouse was built
to be completely fireproof with the exterior being constructed of brick and
trimmed with natural Bedford Stone.  The interior walls were finished with a
fine grade of pink Tennessee marble.  The total cost was $108,000 of which sum
the federal government furnished $32,300.

The county officers moved into this new building on January 16, 1935, with the
courthouse being dedicated in June of 1935.

                        SPECIAL

GERHARDT BAUMAN S. DAK. MUSIC HALL OF FAME

Gerhardt Bauman has been a lover of music from early childhood.  He was always
fascinated by the old organ standing in the parlor of his farm home east of
Java, SD and by his father's chromatic accordion on which he would play some
German-Russian folk songs during the long winter evenings.

At the age of six years, Gerhardt and his sister, Christina, were requested to
sing for school programs and various civic affairs.  At the age of ten years he
played a portable organ for public dances with his brothers.

Gerhardt's dreams were to some day own his own musical instrument.  At the age
of fourteen, he realized this dream when he purchased, with financial help from
his brother, Gust, his first small piano-key accordion from Sears Roebuck & Co.
for $79.95. As this instrument was limited in quality, his real dream came true
when, at the age of 16, he purchased his first full-size piano-key accordion
for $550.  He now played his music throughout the Dakota's, Nebraska, and
Wisconsin.

In 1929, he was engaged for a short time with Ted Fireito, playing over WCCO
Radio Station in Minneapolis, Minnesota.  After that he played one-nighters
with his own bands, consisting of between three to seven pieces.  In 1931, he
joined Wheatley's Novelty Band of WNAX Radio Station of Yankton, SD.  Since
that time he has brought his music to homes, parties, weddings, ballrooms, and
radio stations.

As musical quality improved and prices changed, Gerhardt's instruments also
changed.  He has owned 11 piano-key accordions ranging from one-switch models
costing $550 to 21-switch models costing $1650.  His latest addition was made
in 1954 when he purchased a full-sized Hammond Electric Organ.

Gerhardt's Organ Music took him from local small jobs to cities such as Sioux
Falls, SD, Fargo, ND, Frederick Martins Hotel at Moorhead, MN, Veterans of
Foreign Wars Hall at Lake Street in Mpls., MN, Red Wing, MN, Rapid City, SD,
Bay City, Wisconsin, Strasburg, ND and many more recent locations, such as
Washington State.

Because the organ was a heavy instrument to handle and move around - it
involved two to three men - in 1965, Gerhardt, with his accordion, joined Mike
Dosch, a great organist from Strasburg, ND.

In 1969, Gerhardt went into a contract with Excelsior Accordion Mfg.  Co. of
New York to have them build an accordion with an Electric Organ full-range
percussion built in, which the factory had delivered to him in 1972.  This
instrument was more portable so he could load and handle it alone. It had power
and sound to cover most size halls and clubs.

He had a one-year contract for three nights a week at the Spur Lounge in
Aberdeen, SD.  Since then, he has covered most of the clubs in nearby cities
and states.

On April 21, 1983, Gerhardt was honored by the SD Musicians Association at the
New Elks Lodge in Aberdeen, SD as the Musician of the Year.  He was to be
placed in the Hall of Fame after having brought the joy of music to people for
over 50 years.  It was, indeed, a glorious occasion and, according to the Elks
Manager, it was the largest crowd ever in the New Elks Club.  An estimated 700
people were in attendance. Gerhardt was the third Musician placed into the SD
Hall of Fame.

Gerhardt Bauman would never exchange his past music experiences or his many
friends made during his musical career for any sum of money.  Gerhardt
expresses his thanks to his many fans and the various ballroom managers for his
past success in which they played such a vital role.

JOHN HAERTER PRESIDENT OF SOUTH DAKOTA BANKERS

John's interest in the South Dakota Bankers Association began in May, 1978,
when he was elected to its Board of Directors for a three year term.  After
serving in 1981 on the South Dakota Bankers Long Range Planning Committee, he
decided to run for Vice President.

At the South Dakota Bankers Convention held in Rapid City in May, 1982, John
was elected Vice President of this association to serve in this capacity until
May, 1983.  From May, 1983, to May, 1984, he served as President Elect.  At the
joint centennial convention of the North and South Dakota Bankers held at the
Broadmoor at Colorado Springs, Colorado, in July, 1984, he was elected
President of the South Dakota Bankers Association for the year 1984-1985.
During his term as President, one thought of special interest was that he was
called to Washington, DC, to testify on behalf of small businesses and farmers
in a difficult agricultural economy in the Midwestern states.  Portions of this
testimony were shown on the CBS evening news.

In October, 1985, John was appointed to the American Bankers Association
Government Relations Council and in October, 1986, began his second year of a
three year term. The majority of these meetings are held in Washington, DC.

John feels the experiences encountered during these years contribute to his
better serving Farmers State Bank.

PIETZ INDUCTED INTO HALL OF FAME

Hosmer's "Mr. Baseball" Sam Pietz was added to the South Dakota Amateur
Baseball Hall of Fame at the annual awards dinner held in Eureka, SD April 16,
1962.  Sam was presented a certificate and a plaque in his honor was hung in
the Amateur Baseball Hall of Fame in Garden City, birthplace of the amateur
association in 1933.

Sam's nomination for the honor was made by Arnold Pietz of Hosmer.  Sam managed
Hosmer baseball teams from 1938 through 1960, and was credited with keeping
baseball alive in the Hosmer community for nearly a quarter century.

In 1940, he sponsored a project which raised $1,500 to construct a ball park,
which now consists of two fields and bleachers.  Later, lights were added.  The
Hosmer community is very proud of this field and of their ball teams.

YOUNG HOSMER BAND RECEIVES HONOR

On April 3, 1937, the members of the Hosmer band were to compete in the
district music contest at Mobridge.  Their director, Mr. Alfred Heckenlaible,
the accompanist, Miss Alice Kurle, and the instrumental soloists had gone to
Mobridge on the day before to compete in the contest.  During the night a
blizzard came up, and by morning all the roads were blocked.  The band was to
leave Hosmer at 7:00 a.m. but could not leave town until the snow plow arrived
to clear a road for them.  Finally, about 11:00 o'clock, the cars with the band
members followed the snow plow to Mobridge.  The director came to meet the
caravan of cars.  The judges permitted the band and the ensembles to perform as
soon as the members arrived.  The band received a superior rating in Class C
competition.  Having earned this rating, they were entitled to attend the State
Music Contest at Aberdeen in April.

The band felt proud of having the opportunity to enter the state contest.  They
played two marches and one overture. Eight other bands from the state were in
that division.

When the judge announced, "Class C Bands -Hosmer --Superior," everyone cheered
and applauded.  They were the only band in Class C to receive this honor.

That evening the band played in the Grand Concert. Their efforts were rewarded
with loud applause by the audience.

In the fall of 1937, they received another great honor. They were invited to
march in the Hobo Day parade at Brookings on October 23.  They were asked to
participate as they were one of the six outstanding high school bands in the
state.

The band members were very proud of the new uniforms they had; white trousers
with black satin jackets with orange trimming, white shirts and black neckties,
and white shoes and black socks.  The jackets were made by the mothers of the
band members, and the trousers were purchased.

There was a great deal of excitement in the school the Friday the band was to
leave for Brookings.  Most of the members had never been further from home than
Aberdeen. At first the band was not allowed to leave on their long 200 mile
journey over gravel roads, until school was dismissed at 4:00 o'clock, but the
Board of Education stepped in and permitted them to leave at 2:30.  The members
slept in gyms that  were furnished with cots for two nights.

The band received many fine compliments from the spectators of the parade.  It
was a young band with only twenty- one members but their performance in
marching and playing was excellent.

The band members were given free admission to the homecoming football game.
This was also a first for most of the students, as they had never watched a
live football game before.

The people in Hosmer were very proud of their band. They had earned an honor
which no other band in the surrounding area had received.

HOSMER BOYS DISTRICT CHAMPS

The 1981-82 boy's basketball team will long be remembered in the Hosmer
community.  They were the team that brought home the first and only district
basketball championship.

Actually the road to this successful season probably began with the 1980-81
team.  This was also a successful season with a final 15 win 7 loss record.  In
the District V Championship game Hosmer lost by one point to Eureka. The final
score was 52-51. It was during this season that coach Lew Paulson said that the
Hosmer team "learned they could be winners".

When the season started in December, 1981, Hosmer was missing Gregg Burrer,
Jeff Aman, and Nathan Herrboldt, seniors from the past year's team, but they
retained the winning spirit.

Talk in the community that winter centered on "our team" as they compiled their
20 win 4 loss season.  They were the Yellowstone Trail Conference champions
ending up with a perfect 9-0 conference record.  Hosmer was also the
Yellowstone Trail Conference Tournament champions.  In the championship game,
they came from behind to beat Hoven 67-63 in overtime.  This was also the first
time since 1953 that Hosmer had come home a champion in the YTC Tournament.

The District V Basketball Tournament started on February 23, 1982, at the Civic
Arena in Aberdeen.  Hosmer played Leola in the first round and avenged an
earlier loss by winning 60-55.  Roscoe was the second round opponent, and
Hosmer got into the championship game by blasting them 72-46.  This set the
stage for the championship game with Ipswich.  This proved to be a close,
exciting game all the way with Hosmer's ace Mike Heier calmly stepping up to
the free throw line with 6 seconds left on the clock and sinking both free
throws to give Hosmer the win 62-60.  This once again avenged a loss to Ipswich
during the regular season.

The following week found Hosmer back in the Civic Arena playing in the Region
11 Tournament.  In the first round game, Hosmer shocked the Mobridge Tigers by
defeating them 56-49.  The championship game was billed as a "Cinderella Game"
as both Hosmer and their opponent Warner were coming off their first ever
district championships. Hosmer finally ran out of steam and lost to Warner
77-62.

The Hosmer community was very much united in their pride in this team.  Crowds
were large at every game, it didn't matter whether it was played at home or
away.  During the district and regional tournaments the question was often
asked, "Who's turning out the lights in Hosmer?" as it seemed that the whole
town moved 60 miles east to the Civic Arena on game nights.  Those that didn't
make the trip had their ears glued to the radio in eager anticipation.

After the season was over an appreciation program was held at the Hosmer school
with approximately 225 supporters in attendance.  Roland Sandmeier, president
of the school board, was MC.  Dean Schumacher, president of the Sportsmen's
Club, presented Coach Paulson with an appreciation plaque and each player and
cheerleader also received a memento.

Players on this historic team were: Mike and Ed Heier, Jacob Lunders, Steven
Jung, and Tom Mohr as starters.  Also seeing considerable action were Bruce
Schumacher, Paul Mohr, and Keith Aman.  Other members of the team were Peter
Allison, Russell Grismer, Kurt Gab, and Rodney Grismer.  Student managers were
Wesley Schaible and Lynell Walth.  Cheryl Kirschenmann was the statistician.
Cheerleaders were Karla Mohr, Sherry Sandmeier, Janet Gisi, and Monica Graff.

        Mike Heier with the "victory net" after winning the District V
        Championship.

THE 1986 HOSMER GIRLS' BASKETBALL TEAM

The first girls' team to receive first place in a District Tournament, and also
the first girls' team to win a Yellowstone Trail Conference Championship
compiling a perfect 9-0 conference record was the 1986 Hosmer girls' Basketball
team. Starters for the team were Dawn Aman, Denise Haerter, Amy Gisi, Joleen
Mohr, and Jayne Mohr. Also seeing considerable action were Tracie Heier and
Sheila Sandmeier. They finished the season with an 18 win 6 loss record,
including a trip to the Region I Tournament in Webster.

HOSMER SNOW QUEENS

The Hosmer Civic Club has sponsored the annual Snow Queen Contest since 1955.
This event is held in November at the School Auditorium.  Along with choosing a
Snow Queen, a Jr. Snow Queen is also crowned.  The past three years the
crowning of a Little Miss Princess has been added to the evening's festivities.
Another attraction for the event is the unveiling of a Mystery Snowman or
Snowwoman.  Every year the audience is entertained with local and area talent.

Many volunteer hours are given to this annual project. Decorating of the stage
is a task which always reveals a spectacular sight.  For several years, it was
the Hosmer Homemakers Extension Club that undertook this project, but at
present it is up to a committee from the Civic Club that takes care of the
decorating.  Prior to the evening's event all Jr. and Sr.  Snow Queen
contestants have met with an advisor preparing the girls to walk properly with
poise and ease.

        HOSMER CENTENNIAL RIFLE: This is a one-of-a-kind Winchester .30-.30
        Model 94A-E XTR Rifle made by the Winchester Co. in New Haven, Conn.
        The engraving on the receiver of the rifle was done by Zenith
        Pictorials of Iowa. There is also an uncirculated 1887 Silver Dollar
        imbedded in the stock and the hammer and trigger are plated in 24K
        Gold. A raffle is being held for this rifle with 100 tickets being sold
        at $20.00 each.  The drawing is scheduled for during the Centennial
        festivities. The Gun Rack was made by Lew Paulson, Hosmer, and goes to
        the winner with the rifle.

        The Hazen General Store, Hosmer 1898. George M. Hazen, Hosmer
        Postmaster, 1895. Annie T. Hazen, Hosmer Postmistress, 1897-1901. The
        Hazens homesteaded in Hosmer and started the General Store that also
        sold farm machinery.  Also pictured are their children John, Helen, and
        Theresa.

        The above pictured house is believed to be the first frame home built
        in Hosmer.  The house was constructed in 1898 by Fred Sonnenfeld.  At
        approximately the same time, John Haerter also constructed a frame home
        in Hosmer.  It was not possible to establish which of the two homes were
        completed first.

        FREAK ACCIDENT!  In Oct., 1957, on the Joe P. Heier farm east of
        Hosmer, Joe Heier was driving the tractor which was pulling the truck
        and a large trailer loaded with flax. (The trailer was removed before
        this photo was taken.) Joe Huber, a neighbor, was. steering the truck
        and, while driving downhill, the brakes gave out and the horn didn't
        work.  Joe Heier happened to look around just before the truck hit the
        tractor and jumped.  His jacket was caught under the rear wheel of the
        tractor.  There was more damage done to Heier's and Huber's nerves than
        to the tractor or truck!

        heifers has had her first calf.

        This unusual rock is found southeast of the Arnold Schmidt farm,
        northeast of Hosmer. The height of the rock is 11 1/2 feet, width is 13
        feet, and the opening varies from 2 to 15 inches.

        WATERMELON PICKERS!  This about 3/4 of the Phillip Heier family taken
        in Sept., 1935, at the Phillip Heier farm 3 mi. NW of Hosmer. After the
        Heier family ate their fill, the watermelons were transferred to the
        root cellar for preservation and pickling.  Whole melons were pickled
        in 50 gal. barrels. Clockwise starting seated under wagon at rear
        wheel: Phil, Jr., Mike, Teresa, Margaret, Raymond, Jake, Adam, and
        Barney, standing.
